# On living beings.

<term>living beings</term> <note>original: "animantibus" — In the medieval tradition, this term refers to any creature possessing an *anima* (a soul or breath of life). This category typically encompasses both humans and animals, distinguishing them from plants and inanimate rocks or minerals.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a title or introductory leaf for a new section concerning "living beings," marking a transition from legal/spiritual topics to the study of animate nature. The page is notably damaged by moisture and environmental wear, typical of a well-used reference text.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The text is written in a Gothic textualis script, typical of 13th-14th century scholarly manuscripts. This page begins a discussion on the naming of animals, excerpted from Book XII of Isidore of Seville's *Etymologies*, a standard medieval reference work. The decorative 'O' marks the beginning of this section.</meta>

**O**n all living things, Adam first bestowed names, calling each one by a name from its present state, according to the condition of the nature it served. Nations, however, gave names to each of the animals in their own tongue. Adam did not impose those names according to the Latin or Greek language, nor according to the names of any of the barbarian nations; but in that language which before the Flood was the single language of all, which is called <term>Hebrew</term><note>Medieval scholars generally believed Hebrew was the original language of humanity and the language spoken by God and Adam in Eden.</note>.

In Latin, they are called <term>animals</term><note>original: "animalia"</note> or <term>living beings</term><note>original: "animantia"</note> because they are animated by life and moved by spirit. They are called <term>quadrupeds</term> because they walk on four feet; while these are similar to cattle, they are not under human care, such as stags, does, wild asses, and others. But they are neither <term>beasts</term><note>original: "bestie"; here referring to fierce or wild animals like lions.</note> nor <term>beasts of burden</term><note>original: "iumenta"</note> that can assist in human tasks. 

<term>Livestock</term><note>original: "pecus"</note> is the name given to everything that lacks human speech and form. Properly, however, the name for livestock is usually applied to those animals which are fit for eating—such as sheep and pigs—or those useful for human service, such as horses and oxen. 

There is a difference, however, between <term>cattle</term><note>original: "pecora"</note> and <term>beasts for slaughter</term><note>original: "pecudes"</note>. For the ancients used "cattle" in a general sense for all animals. "Beasts for slaughter," however, are only those living things that are eaten, as if the word were <gloss>eating cattle</gloss><note>original: "pecu edes". Isidore of Seville frequently uses these phonetic etymologies to explain the origins of Latin words.</note>. Generally, however, every...

<summary>This page discusses the origins of animal names, asserting that Adam named all creatures in Hebrew and categorizing them into groups like quadrupeds, livestock, and beasts of burden based on their use to humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Hebrew language, animal taxonomy, Isidore of Seville, etymology, livestock, quadrupeds</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal classifications from Isidore of Seville's *Etymologies*. The previous page ended with a distinction between different types of cattle and livestock. This section focuses on the specific names and roles of working animals and the distinction between types of groups (herds versus flocks).</meta>

<term>Animal</term><note>original: "animal"</note> is so called as if from "feeding."<note>original: "apascendo". Isidore often suggests that the word "animal" relates to the act of grazing or feeding (pascere), though linguistically it is actually derived from "anima," meaning breath or soul.</note> <term>Beasts of burden</term><note>original: "iumenta"</note> derived their names from the fact that they help<note>original: "iuuant"</note> our labor or our burdens by submitting to the assistance of the task or by plowing. For the <term>ox</term><note>original: "bos"</note> pulls wagons and turns the hardest clods of earth with a plowshare. The <term>horse</term><note>original: "equus"</note> and the <term>donkey</term><note>original: "asinus"</note> carry loads and ease the labor of humans by their walking.

For this reason, they are called <term>iumenta</term> because they "help" humans. They are, indeed, animals of great strength. Likewise, they are called <term>draft animals</term><note>original: "armenta"</note> either because they are suited for <term>arms</term><note>original: "armis"</note>—that is, for war—or because we use them in warfare.

Others understand "armenta" to mean oxen, named from "plowing"<note>original: "arando"</note> as if they were "plow-animals,"<note>original: "aramenta"</note> or because they are "armed"<note>original: "armata"; likely referring to the horns of the cattle as natural weapons.</note>. There is a distinction, however, between <term>herds</term><note>original: "armenta"</note> and <term>flocks</term><note>original: "greges"</note>. For "herds" consist of horses and oxen, while "flocks" consist of goats and sheep.

<summary>This page defines various working animals based on their utility to humans, using medieval etymologies to link their names to "helping," "plowing," or "warfare." It concludes by defining the specific terminology used for groups of large animals (herds) versus small animals (flocks).</summary>
<keywords>etymology, beasts of burden, oxen, horses, donkeys, livestock classification, draft animals, herds, flocks</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page begins the Bestiary section, a popular medieval genre that combined natural history with moral allegory. This text is heavily influenced by Isidore of Seville’s 7th-century encyclopedia, 'Etymologies'.</meta>

The term **beasts** <note>original: "Bestiarum"</note> properly applies to lions, leopards, and tigers, wolves and foxes, dogs, monkeys, and all others that rage with either tooth <note>literally "mouth"</note> or claw, excluding serpents. They are called "beasts" because of the force <note>The author is likely drawing a connection between "bestia" and "vis" (force)</note> by which they are driven. They are called <term>wild animals|ferae</term> because they enjoy natural liberty and are led <note>original: "ferantur"</note> by their own desires.

For their wills are free; they wander here and there, and wherever their spirit leads, there they are carried.

The name of the **lion** is derived from a Greek origin into Latin. In Greek, it is called *leon*, and it is a "hybrid" name <note>original: "nothum"; a linguistic term used by medieval grammarians for words borrowed from Greek and partially altered in Latin</note> because it has been partially corrupted. For *leo* in Greek is interpreted as "king" in Latin <note>This is a medieval folk etymology; the word "leo" does not actually mean king in Greek, but the lion was universally regarded as the king of animals</note>, because he is the prince of all beasts.

They are said to be of <term>three types|tripharium</term>: those that are short with curly manes are peaceful. The long ones with straight manes are fierce; their forehead and tail reveal their bold spirit. Their strength lies in the chest, while their firmness is in the head. They fear the creaking of wheels, but they fear fire even more. The lion is of such a proud and fierce nature that he does not know how to associate with other kinds of wild animals; instead, like a certain king, he disdains...

<summary>This page defines the category of 'beasts' and begins a detailed description of the lion, the 'king of beasts,' focusing on its etymology and physical characteristics.</summary>
<keywords>bestiary, lion, etymology, natural history, animal classification, Isidore of Seville</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the lion, transitioning from its physical characteristics to its symbolic behaviors as described in the Physiologus, a foundational medieval text on animal symbolism. It establishes the three "natures" of the lion as allegories for the life of Christ.</meta>

...consistent with his nature.
The **Physiologus** <note>A Greek text, later translated into Latin, which was the primary source for medieval animal lore and Christian allegory</note> says that the lion has three principal natures. His **first nature** is that he loves to walk along the peaks of the mountains. And if it should happen that he is pursued by hunters, the scent of the hunters reaches him; and with his tail behind his back, he covers his tracks wherever he goes, so that the hunters may not find his den by his tracks and capture him.

Just so our Savior—that is, the spiritual **lion from the tribe of Judah**, the root of Jesse, the son of David—covered the tracks of his divinity in the heavens, until, sent by the Father, he descended into the womb of the Virgin Mary to save the human race which had perished. And the **devil**, that is, the enemy of the human race, was ignorant of this, for he dared to tempt Him as if he were a mere man <note>original: "purum hominem"</note>. And even those angels who were on high, being ignorant of this as He was ascending to the Father, said to those who were ascending with Him:

> Who is this King of Glory? <note>A quotation from Psalm 24 (Psalm 23 in the Vulgate numbering), interpreted here as the angels' surprise at Christ's divinity during the Ascension</note>

His **second nature** is that when he sleeps, he appears to have his eyes open. Just so our Lord, while sleeping physically upon the cross and buried in the tomb, His divinity remained awake, as is said in the **Song of Songs**:

> I sleep, and my heart keeps watch. <note>Song of Songs 5:2; interpreted by medieval scholars as the wakefulness of Christ’s divine nature even during his death</note>

And in the **Psalm**:

> Behold, He who keeps watch over Israel shall neither slumber nor sleep. <note>Psalm 121:4 (Psalm 120 in the Vulgate)</note>

His **third nature** is: when the lioness <note>original: "leena"</note> gives birth to her cubs, she brings them forth dead, and she guards...

<summary>This page describes the first two symbolic 'natures' of the lion: using its tail to hide its tracks (representing the Incarnation) and sleeping with open eyes (representing Christ's divinity remaining awake during his death).</summary>
<keywords>Physiologus, Lion, Christ, Devil, Incarnation, Song of Songs, Psalms, Lioness, Allegory, Medieval Science</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the "third nature" of the lion, illustrating the Resurrection of Christ through the allegory of lion cubs being brought to life by their father's breath. It then transitions into the natural and moral qualities of the lion as understood in the medieval tradition.</meta>

...them for three days, until the father, coming on the **third day**, breathes into their faces and brings them to life. <note>Medieval bestiaries claimed lion cubs were born dead and only "awakened" after three days by their father's breath or roar, a direct parallel to the Resurrection.</note>

Thus the Almighty Father raised our Lord Jesus Christ from the dead on the third day, as Jacob said: "He shall rule like a **lion**, and like a **lion's cub** he shall be raised." <note>original: "Dominabit tanquam leo. et sicut catulus leonis suscitabitur." A reference to the Blessing of Judah in Genesis 49:9.</note> Regarding the nature of lions in relation to man, they cannot be provoked to anger unless they are harmed. Rational men <term>rational men (rationabiles homines): a term distinguishing humans, who possess a soul and reason, from "brute" animals</term> ought to look to this example—those who become angry even when not harmed and oppress the innocent, while the **Christian law** commands that even the guilty be set free.

For the father of lions is known for his **mercy** <term>mercy (misericordia): despite their strength, lions were frequently depicted as noble and merciful to those who submitted</term> through constant examples; for they spare those who prostrate themselves; they permit captives they encounter to return home. They rage against men rather than women. They do not kill infants except in cases of great hunger.

Likewise, they all give birth from abundance <note>original: "a sagina," referring to the state of being well-fed or fattened</note>. First, they take drink on alternating days and food on the others. But frequently, if digestion has not followed, they postpone their usual feeding day, because they are then burdened by having devoured more meat than is right. Then, because they are weighed down by having devoured more meat than is right, they voluntarily pull it out by inserting their **claws** into their mouths. Indeed, they do the same when they must flee while full.

The old age of lions is proven by the loss of their teeth. They mate as adulterers. <note>In this context, "adultery" refers to mating habits that deviated from the perceived "natural" or monogamous order.</note> And not only these, but also **lynxes**, **camels**, **elephants**, **rhinoceroses**, **tigers**, and **lionesses**. In her first birth, she has five cubs.

<summary>This page concludes the Christological allegory of the lion's third nature (the Resurrection) and begins a detailed description of the lion's "moral" behaviors, such as mercy toward the weak and regulated eating habits.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the lion's natural history and temperament, specifically focusing on the reproduction of lionesses and the various ways the "King of Beasts" can be weakened or killed by much smaller creatures.</meta>

...they raise. Then, year by year, they reduce the number of offspring in the following years. And finally, when they have come down to one, the mother's fertility is exhausted and they remain sterile forever. <note>This reflects a medieval belief, often attributed to Herodotus, that a lioness produces five cubs in her first litter, four in the next, and so on, until she becomes barren after her final, single cub.</note>

The **lion** scorns yesterday's food and turns away from the leftovers of its own meal. What wild beast would dare resist it, when such great natural terror resides in its voice? Indeed, many living things that might have escaped its attack through their speed are so overcome and defeated by the mere sound of its roar that they fail as if lifeless.

When a **lion** is sick, it seeks out a **monkey** <note>original: "simiam"</note> to devour so that it might be healed. The lion fears the **cock**, and especially the white one. Although the lion is indeed the king of beasts, it is driven wild by the tiny sting of a **scorpion**, and is killed by the venom of a **serpent**.

We have heard of small beasts called **Leontophones** <term>Leontophones (lion-slayers): legendary small animals whose remains were believed to be toxic to lions</term>. When these are captured, they are burned so that meat polluted with a sprinkling of their ashes may be placed at the crossroads of the paths where lions run. If a lion tastes even a tiny morsel of this, it is killed. For this reason, lions pursue them with a natural hatred; and whenever the opportunity arises, they refrain from biting them, but instead they tear them apart and kill them with the force of their paws. <note>The lion avoids biting the Leontophone to prevent ingesting its poison, choosing instead to crush it to death.</note>

- On the left, two small golden-brown animals (leontophones) are curled inside a pit.
- In the center, a lion has fallen head-first into a pit, with only its hindquarters and tail visible.
- On the right, a small white animal stands on a green rectangular grate or platform placed over a pit that appears to contain fire, depicted with red patterns.
This sequence illustrates the traditional bestiary account of how the leontophone is captured and burned so that its ashes can be used to poison lions.</image-desc>

<keywords>lion, lioness, Leontophones, animal lore, medieval medicine, bestiary, fertility, lion's roar, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical descriptions and legendary behaviors of lions (such as their reaction to Leontophones and roosters) into a moral and allegorical interpretation of the lioness, drawing directly from Saint Gregory the Great's "Moralia in Job." It continues the theme of the lion as a complex symbol of both strength and vulnerability.</meta>

By the name of the **lioness**, the human mind is signified. As is said of **Blessed Job** <note>original: "bti iob"</note>: *And because of pride, you will catch me like a lioness* <note>original: "et ppter supbiam q̃i leenam capies me"; this is a quote from the Book of Job 10:16 in the Latin Vulgate bible</note>. 

**Divine providence** <term>Divine providence (superna dispensatio): the theological concept of God's intervention in and governance of the world</term> therefore permits us, who serve Him with our whole intention, to be tried by the temptations of our flesh. This is done so that our mind does not dare to be puffed up with pride through the presumption of its own security. Instead, while it trembles from being struck by these trials, it may plant its foot more firmly in the help of its Creator alone. 

For this reason, it is well said: *Because of pride, you will catch me like a lioness.* For indeed, when a **lioness** seeks food for her cubs, she rushes open-mouthed into a pit set for her capture. 

It is known, as is reported from certain regions, that long pits are constructed in those lands where livestock is placed as bait. The lioness is provoked to seek the prey there by her hunger. This pit is prepared to be both narrow and deep, so that while she circles it, she may fall in, yet she can by no means escape it by jumping out. 

A deep pit is dug which is not connected to the surface, but it is joined to the part where the cattle are by a trap at the far end. In that spot, a cage is placed. When the lioness comes, she is driven by terrors from above <note>This likely refers to hunters using noise or fire to drive the trapped animal toward the cage</note>; seeking to hide herself as if in a more secret part of the pit, she enters the cage of her own accord. 

Once the cage is lifted, she is no longer feared, because she is raised up enclosed within it. She who fell into the pit of her own will returns to the upper world surrounded by iron bars. In this same way, the **human mind**—

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the lioness as a symbol of the human mind, explaining how God uses temptation to humble the proud. It includes a detailed description of a medieval hunting technique used to trap lions in pits using cages and bait.</summary>
<keywords>Job, lioness, pride, Divine Providence, allegory, hunting traps, Saint Gregory the Great, Moralia in Job, temptation</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Saint Gregory the Great's commentary on the Book of Job. It specifically unpacks the metaphor of the "lioness" falling into a pit, transitioning from the literal description of a hunter's trap on the previous page to a theological explanation of the Fall of Man and the necessity of divine discipline.</meta>

She is the **goat** who, though established in **freedom of choice** <note>original: "libertate arbitrii"; the theological concept that humans have the free will to choose between good and evil</note>, sought the desires of the flesh while being nourished—just as the **lioness** sought food for her cubs. And so, she fell into the pit of her own deception, she who, at the persuasion of the Enemy <note>The "Enemy" refers to the Devil or the serpent in Genesis</note>, extended her hand toward the forbidden food. 

But in that pit, she immediately found a **cage**. Because she came to her death voluntarily, she soon endured the prison of her own **corruption** <term>Corruption (corrupcio): in medieval theology, this refers to the state of human mortality and the tendency toward sin resulting from the Fall of Adam</term>. 

Through the finding of grace, she is led back to the free air. But when she tries to do many things and is not able, she is bound by the obstacles of that same corruption, as if by the bars of a cage. Indeed, she has already escaped that pit of **damnation** into which she had fallen; because she followed the punishment of death but was helped by the hand of the **Redeemer**, she is overcome by returning to mercy. 

Yet, being constrained, she still tolerates the cage. She is distressed by the bonds of heavenly discipline so that she might not wander through the desires of the flesh. Therefore, she who fell into the pit by her own will returns to the free air while still enclosed. This is because she both fell into sin through her **freedom of choice**, and yet, the grace of the Creator restricts her movements and restrains her, even against her will. 

Thus, after the pit, she suffers the cage. Having been snatched from eternal punishment, she is tied back from the movements of a wicked will under the **management of the heavenly Craftsman** <note>original: "celestis artificis dispensacione"</note>. 

Rightly, therefore, is it said: *And because of pride, you will catch me like a lioness* <note>original: "et per superbiam quasi leenam capies me"</note>. For a free man brought death upon himself through food, but having been brought back to mercy, he lives better while enclosed under discipline.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the lioness by explaining that while humanity fell into the "pit" of sin through free will, God’s grace rescues us but keeps us in a "cage" of holy discipline to prevent further spiritual wandering.</summary>
<keywords>Free will, The Fall, original sin, divine discipline, Saint Gregory the Great, Book of Job, redemption, lioness allegory</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on the Book of Job by Saint Gregory the Great. The previous page established the metaphor of a lioness—representing the soul or humanity—falling into a pit of corruption. This page expands on how pride (superbia) leads to this fall and how God uses our own weaknesses as a form of "caged" discipline to lead us back to humility and grace.</meta>

Like a lioness, therefore, she was captured through **pride**; for now, the discipline of her own **corruption** <term>Corruption (corrupcio): the state of human mortality and spiritual frailty resulting from the Fall</term> weighs her down. From this state, not fearing to transgress the commandment, she leaped boldly into the pit. 

But even if we set aside for a moment the way the mind was overwhelmed by the guilt of our **first parent** <note>Referring to Adam and the concept of Original Sin</note>, we still find ourselves daily captured like the lioness through the vice of **exaltation** <term>Exaltation (elatio): a sense of self-importance or pride that lifts a person up in their own estimation</term>. For often, a person who has received **virtues** <note>moral excellences or spiritual gifts</note> is lifted up by the audacity of his own presumption; yet, by a marvelous arrangement of divine mercy, it is ordained that some matter should fall into ruin before his very eyes. And while he desires something for a moment, what else is seen in the pit but his prey? 

He falls into this trap of his own accord, but he lacks the strength to rise again by his own power. When he considers that he is nothing of himself, he learns without a doubt whose help he must seek. Yet, through heavenly compassion, the Lord lifts him out of the pit as if he were a captive, so that, having recognized his own weakness, he may be led back to forgiveness. 

Thus, in the manner of a lioness, he who is puffed up by his virtue is held in the upper parts of the **cage**; having slipped into earthly desires, he is bound in **humility** <note>the spiritual discipline of recognizing one's own limitations and dependence on God</note>. Because he sins through his own presumption, it is brought about by a marvelous mercy that he now lives "enclosed" by the awareness of his own frailty. Because the **holy man** <note>likely referring to the figure of Job, or the author Gregory taking on a persona of penance</note> sees this happen so often to people, he now takes the voice of sin upon himself; thus, when we recognize his weeping, we may learn what things in ourselves we ought to mourn.

<summary>This page explains the allegory of the lioness as a symbol for the human soul falling into the 'pit' of sin through pride. It describes how God allows humans to experience their own weakness and 'imprisonment' in mortality as a tool of discipline to teach humility and the need for divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Humility, Virtue, Original Sin, Divine Discipline, Lioness Allegory, Gregory the Great, Penance</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
# The Tiger is named for its swift flight.

For that is what the Persians, Greeks, and Medes call an arrow <note>The Latin text notes "perse greci & medi sagittam." The word "tiger" likely derives from the Old Persian word *tigra*, meaning pointed or sharp, also used for an arrow.</note>. It is a beast distinguished by various spots, wonderful in its strength and speed. From its name, the **Tigris river** is called, because it is the swiftest of all rivers. These animals are mostly produced in <term>Hyrcania</term> <note>A region located along the southeastern shores of the Caspian Sea, known in the Roman world as a wild, forested frontier.</note>. 

When the tiger finds her lair empty of her stolen offspring, she immediately follows the tracks of the thief. But the hunter, though he is carried on a horse to flee, sees nonetheless that he is almost overtaken by the beast's speed. Seeing that no means of escape remains for him, he contrives a trick of this kind: when he sees her close to him, he throws down a sphere made of glass. 

But she is deceived by her own image <note>original: "jmagine sui luditur"</note> and thinks it is her cub. She checks her pursuit, desiring to collect her young. Delayed again by the empty reflection, she pours all her strength into catching the horseman; driven by the sting of anger, she looms over the fleeing man. Again, the hunter slows his pursuer by throwing a sphere. Yet, the memory of the previous trick does not discourage the mother's diligence. She turns over the hollow image and settles down with it as if she were about to nurse it. And so, deceived by the intensity of her own maternal devotion <note>original: "pietatis sue studio"</note>, she loses both her revenge and her child.

<summary>This page describes the tiger, linking its name to the Persian word for arrow and recounting a legendary hunting method involving glass mirrors. It portrays the tiger as a creature of immense speed whose maternal instincts are used against her by hunters.</summary>

<keywords>tiger, Hyrcania, Tigris river, medieval bestiary, hunting myths, animal etymology, maternal devotion</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Following the description of the tiger and its pursuit by hunters, the manuscript turns to the panther, a creature characterized in bestiaries not as a fierce predator, but as a gentle and fragrant symbol of Christ.</meta>

# The Panther

There is an animal that is called the **panther**, which has a coat of many colors. It is exceedingly beautiful and very gentle. The <term>Physiologus</term> <note>A central text for medieval animal lore, likely written in Alexandria between the 2nd and 4th centuries, which provided the basis for the moralizing tales found in bestiaries.</note> says of it that it has only one enemy: the dragon. 

When the panther has eaten and is satisfied, it hides itself in its cave and sleeps. After three days, it rises from its sleep and immediately emits a great roar. From its mouth comes a most sweet scent, as if it held every kind of spice <note>original: "aromata"</note> within it. 

When the other animals—both those that are near and those that are far off—hear its voice, they all gather together and follow the panther wherever it goes because of the sweetness of its breath. But the dragon, upon hearing the voice, flees in terror into the caverns of the earth. There, unable to bear the scent, it becomes numb within itself and remains motionless as if dead. However, the other [animals...] <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the panther as a beautiful, gentle creature whose sweet-smelling breath attracts all animals except the dragon, which flees in terror. In medieval allegory, the panther represents Christ, and the dragon represents the devil.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical comparison between the panther and Christ, transitioning from the animal's physical behavior to its symbolic fulfillment in the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus. animals follow the panther wherever it goes. In this same way, our Lord Jesus Christ—the true panther—descending from the heavens, rescued us from the power of the devil. Through his incarnation, he joined us to himself as children. He took all things for himself, and "leading captivity captive, he gave gifts to men." <note>A reference to Psalm 68:18 and Ephesians 4:8, signifying Christ's victory over sin and death.</note>

That it is a **multicolored animal** <note>original: "varium animal"</note> is signified by what was said by Solomon concerning Christ, who is the wisdom of God: "a spirit that is pure, intelligent, unique, manifold, true, sweet, ready, merciful, firm, stable, secure, all-powerful, and all-seeing." <note>This list of attributes is drawn from the Book of Wisdom 7:22-23.</note>

That it is a **beautiful animal**, David says of Christ: "Beautiful in form beyond the sons of men." <note>Psalm 45:2.</note>

That it is a **gentle animal**, Isaiah also says: "Rejoice and be glad, daughter of Zion; preach, daughter of Jerusalem, for your king comes to you, gentle." <note>original: "mansuetus." While the manuscript attributes this to Isaiah, the quote is from Zechariah 9:9, a common attribution error in medieval manuscripts.</note>

When he had been filled <note>The text compares the panther's full stomach to Christ being "filled" with the sufferings of the Passion.</note>, he hid himself in the cave amidst mockeries, lashes, slaps, injuries, insults, and spitting; hung on the cross by his hands, fixed with nails, given gall and vinegar to drink, and pierced with a spear. Falling asleep, he rested in the tomb and descended into hell, there binding the great dragon. <note>The "harrowing of hell," where Christ is said to have bound Satan (the dragon) and freed the souls of the righteous.</note>

On the third day, however, he rises from sleep and lets out a great cry, smelling of sweetness, just as David says: "The Lord was awakened as if from sleep, like a mi—" <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "tanq(ua)m po-", likely intended to be "tanquam potens" (like a mighty man) from Psalm 78:65: "Then the Lord awoke as from sleep, like a mighty man refreshed by wine."</note>

<meta>This page concludes the account of the viper's birth and begins the entry for the antelope. While the previous page discussed the Panther as a symbol of Christ, the text has transitioned through the Bestiary's sequence to the Viper (a symbol of parental betrayal) and now to the Antelope.</meta>

...the reason for this is manifest. For when three offspring <note>original: "catuli," literally meaning "pups" or "cubs," here referring to young vipers.</note> have grown together in the mother’s womb and possess mature strength for being born, they hate any delay in time. Therefore, they tear at the womb with their claws as if it were an obstacle to their birth. She pours out her young—or rather, she releases them under the compulsion of pain. Afterwards, because the womb is ruined and scarred <note>original: "cicatricosis"</note>, any seed received cannot take hold but falls away in vain. For Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (AD 23–79), the Roman author of Natural History, who was the primary scientific authority for medieval writers.</note> says that animals with sharp claws are often unable to give birth, for they are apparently injured from within by the movement of their young.

## The Antelope

There is an animal called the antelope <note>original: "autalops"</note> which is exceedingly fierce, so much so that no hunter can approach it. It has long horns like sa... <note>The text likely continues as "serrata" or "saw-like," describing how the antelope uses its horns to cut through trees.</note>

<summary>This page describes the violent birth of vipers, where the young tear through the mother's womb, and introduces the fierce Antelope, noting its long, saw-like horns.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for the Antelope, following the description of its saw-like horns. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how the animal uses its horns to cut through trees.</meta>

...having the shape of saws, so that it can cut through high and great trees and cast them down to the ground. When it becomes thirsty, it goes to the great river Euphrates and drinks. There is a certain shrub there which in Greek is called *heriane* <note>original: "heriane." This likely refers to the *ericina* or heath/heather, though in bestiary tradition, it represents the "thicket of worldliness."</note>, having slender and long twigs. Coming there, the antelope begins to play with its horns against the *heriane*. While it plays, it entangles its horns in the twigs. When, after struggling for a long time, it cannot be freed, it cries out with a loud voice. Hearing its voice, the hunter comes and kills it. 

So also you, O man, who strive to be sober and chaste and to live spiritually: your two horns are the Two Testaments <note>The Old and New Testaments of the Bible, which the medieval reader was expected to use as spiritual tools.</note>, through which you are able to cut back and prune away all corporal and spiritual vices. Beware, therefore, of drunkenness, lest you be entangled by lust and pleasure, and so be killed by the devil. For wine and women make a man turn away from God <note>original: "apostatare," meaning to fall away or desert one's faith. This phrase is a common medieval citation of the biblical book of Ecclesiasticus 19:2.</note>.

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the Antelope—a beast of dual nature (the Two Testaments) that can be ensnared by worldly thickets—to the entry on the Leopard. It begins a new chapter on the "Pard" (spotted cat) and its hybrid relative, the "Leopard."</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

<image-desc>Large ornamental blue initial P at the start of the text, decorated with intricate red penwork flourishes that extend into the left and top margins.</image-desc>

**P**ard <note>original: "Pardus." In medieval lore, the Pard was considered a distinct species of spotted great cat, separate from the Leopard.</note> is a spotted breed, exceedingly swift and headlong in its thirst for blood; it rushes toward death. The **Leopard** is born from the adultery of a lioness and a pard. This creates a third origin, just as **Pliny** says in his *Natural History* <note>Pliny the Elder was a Roman author whose 1st-century encyclopedia was the primary source for medieval science.</note>: that a lion mates with a female pard, or a pard with a lioness, and from both matings "degenerate" offspring are created, just as the mule and the hinny <note>term: <term>burdo</term> (hinny); the offspring of a stallion and a female donkey, whereas a mule comes from a male donkey and a mare.</note> are produced. In a mystical sense, the pard signifies the **Devil**, who is full of many deceits, or any **sinner** whose soul is covered with the spots of crimes and various types of error. This is why the prophet says: 
> *“Can the Ethiopian change his skin, or the leopard its spots?”* <note>original: "ethiops non mutabit pellem. et pardus varietatem suam." From Jeremiah 13:23.</note>

Likewise, the **Antichrist** is said to be like a pard because he is covered in a variety of wickednesses, as it says in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation 13:2.</note>: 
> *“And the beast which rose from the sea was like a leopard.”* 

This refers to those who persist in the darkness of their sins and the variety of their errors. Yet it is written elsewhere: 
> *“The wolf shall dwell with the lamb, and the leopard shall lie down with the young goat.”* <note>Isaiah 11:6. A prophecy of the peace brought by the Messiah.</note>

This was fulfilled at the coming of **Christ**, when those who were previously fierce began to lead their lives alongside the innocent, and those who were once defiled by the spots of error turned to the truth of the faith through repentance.

<summary>This page describes the origins of the Leopard as a hybrid of a Lion and a 'Pard,' drawing on Pliny the Elder's natural history. It interprets the animal's spots as symbols of sin or the Devil, but concludes with the hopeful message of conversion through Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Leopard, Pard, Pliny the Elder, Antichrist, Devil, Jeremiah, Isaiah, prophecy, symbolism, bestiary, conversion</keywords>

The **Unicorn** <note>original: "Unicornis"</note>, which is also called the **rhinoceros** <note>In the Middle Ages, the mythical unicorn and the rhinoceros were often considered the same animal, based on accounts from ancient Greek historians.</note> by the Greeks, has this nature: it is a small animal, resembling a young goat <note>original: "hedo" (kid). Bestiaries often described the unicorn as small and goat-like rather than the large horse-like creature of modern fantasy.</note>, and is exceedingly fierce. It has one horn in the middle of its head, and no hunter is able to capture it. 

However, it is captured by this strategy: A **virgin girl** is led to the place where it dwells and is left there alone in the woods. As soon as the unicorn sees her, it leaps into her lap <note>original: "sinum" (bosom or lap)</note> and embraces her. Thus, while it is sleeping in her lap, it is seized by the hunters <note>original: "exploratoribus" (scouts/hunters)</note> and is put on display in the king’s palace. 

In the same way, our Lord **Jesus Christ** <note>original: "ihc xpc" (abbreviation for Ihesus Christus)</note> is spiritually the unicorn, of whom <note>The text cuts off here, likely leading into a biblical quotation such as Psalm 92:10 or Deuteronomy 33:17 on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the legendary nature of the unicorn and the specific method of its capture using a virgin, serving as an allegory for the Incarnation of Christ.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the entry on the Unicorn, transitioning from the narrative of its capture by a virgin to the Christological interpretation of the beast’s physical traits and behavior. The text on this page correlates the unicorn's single horn and its fierce nature with the unity of the Godhead and the mystery of the Incarnation.</meta>

...[and is call]ed and beloved, just as the "son of the unicorns" <note>original: "filius unicornium." This is a reference to Psalm 29:6. In medieval theology, the unicorn is a common symbol for Christ.</note>. And in another psalm: "My horn shall be exalted like that of a unicorn" <note>Psalm 92:10.</note>. And Zacharias says: "He has raised up a horn of salvation for us in the house of his servant David" <note>Luke 1:69, from the Benedictus.</note>. 

Moreover, that it has one horn on its head signifies what He Himself says: "I and the Father are one" <note>John 10:30.</note>. And according to the Apostle: "The head of Christ is God" <note>1 Corinthians 11:3.</note>. Furthermore, it is said to be "exceedingly fierce" because neither principalities, nor powers, nor thrones, nor dominions <note>These terms refer to the "Celestial Hierarchy," the different ranks or orders of angels. The text suggests that even the highest angels could not fully comprehend the mystery of God becoming man.</note> were able to understand Him; nor could Hell hold Him, nor was the most subtle Devil able to understand or investigate Him. Instead, by the sole will of the Father, He descended into the womb of the Virgin for our salvation. 

It is called a "small animal" because of the humility of His incarnation, as He Himself said: "Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart" <note>Matthew 11:29.</note>. The unicorn is like a kid <note>original: "hedo" (a young goat/kid). This relates to the unicorn's goat-like appearance in medieval art and its role as a sacrifice.</note>, because the Savior Himself was made in the likeness of sinful flesh, and by sin He condemned sin.

<margin>Unicorns are often captured; they are found and laid low by being wounded.</margin>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the Christological interpretation of the unicorn to a new entry concerning the Lynx. It follows the standard medieval bestiary format: first describing the physical and behavioral traits of the animal, and then providing a moral allegory that likens the animal's habits to human virtues or vices.</meta>

The **Lynx** <note>original: "Linx"</note> is so named because it is counted among the genus of wolves: it is a beast whose back is distinguished by spots like a leopard, but it is otherwise similar to a wolf. It is said that the urine of this creature solidifies <note>original: "coniti," likely a scribal variant for "coit" (thickens or solidifies).</note> into the hardness of a precious stone, which is called **ligurius** <note>Also known as "lyncurium" or "lynx-stone." In medieval lore, this gemstone was believed to be formed from the solidified urine of the lynx.</note>. That the lynxes themselves are aware of this is proven by this evidence: for they cover their discharged liquid with sand as much as they are able, out of a certain natural envy, lest such a discharge should pass into human use.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist whose "Natural History" was a primary source for medieval bestiaries.</note> says that the lynx does not produce more than one offspring. This beast represents the type of **envious and deceitful men** who desire to do harm rather than good. Intent on earthly desires, they enviously hoard those things which are superfluous to themselves, even though such things could have been of benefit to others.

To the left, a stylized plant with orange and blue floral elements grows from the ground.</image-desc>

It is called the **Griffin** <note>original: "Gripes," a medieval variant of the Latin *gryphes*.</note> because it is a winged and four-footed animal. This kind of wild beast is born in **Hyperborean** <note>In ancient and medieval geography, the "Hyperboreans" were a mythical people living in the far north, "beyond the North Wind."</note> lands or mountains. In the entire rear part of its body it is like a **lion**, while in its wings and face it is similar to **eagles**. It is violently hostile to horses; for it also tears apart any humans it sees.

<meta>This page continues the description of the Elephant, following the previous page's introductory sentence which broke off mid-word. The text transitions from the elephant's physical appearance and etymology to its legendary reproductive habits and its role in human warfare.</meta>

...the Greeks think they are named from the greatness of their body, because they present the form of a mountain. For in Greek, a mountain is called *eliphio* <note>original: "elephio." Medieval etymologists mistakenly linked the Greek *elephas* (elephant) to words for hills or mountains due to the animal's size.</note>. Among the Indians, however, it is called by the name **barro**. From this comes the name of its voice, the **barritus** <term>barritus: the specific Latin term for the trumpeting sound of an elephant</term>, and its teeth are called **ivory** <note>original: "ebur"</note>. Its snout is called a **proboscis** <note>original: "promuscida"</note>, because it uses it to move fodder to its mouth, and it is similar to a snake. It is fortified by an ivory wall <note>A reference to the elephant's tusks acting as a defensive barrier.</note>. 

No animal is seen that is larger than this. Indeed, Persians and Indians, placed in wooden towers upon them, fight with spears as if from a wall. They flourish with great intelligence and memory. They travel in herds. They flee from mice. They mate back-to-back. They are pregnant for two years, and they do not give birth more than once, nor do they have many offspring, but only one. 

They live for three hundred years. If an elephant wishes to have offspring, it goes to the East near **Paradise** <note>In medieval maps, the Garden of Eden (Paradise) was often located in the far East of the world.</note>, where there is a tree called the **mandrake** <note>original: "mandragora." In medieval lore, the mandrake was a magical plant with a human-shaped root, believed to induce fertility or desire.</note>. It goes there with its female, who first tastes the fruit of that tree and thus seduces the male, persuading him to eat of the same fruit. After they have both eaten, then they couple with one another, and the female immediately conceives. 

When the time for giving birth arrives, she goes out into a pond until the water reaches the mother's breasts. The male elephant, meanwhile, guards her while she gives birth, because the **dragon** is their enemy...

<summary>This page details the elephant's physical characteristics, its use as a mobile fortress in war, and the myth that it reproduces only by eating mandrake fruit near Paradise. It emphasizes the animal's massive size, its fear of mice, and its protective nature during childbirth.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval account of the elephant, concluding the description of its behavior toward serpents and moving into the famous myth of its lack of knee joints and the religious allegory of the Fall of Man.</meta>

...elephant. If it finds a serpent, it kills it; it tramples it until it dies. For although the elephant is terrifying to bulls, it is equally afraid of a mouse. This is its nature: if it falls, it cannot get back up. It falls when it leans against a tree to sleep. For it has no joints in its knees <note>A common medieval misconception; in reality, elephants have knees and can bend them, but their straight-legged stance led early observers to believe they lacked joints.</note>. A hunter <note>original: "uenator"</note>, however, cuts the tree slightly so that when the elephant leans against it, it falls along with the tree. 

Having fallen, it cries out loudly, and immediately a large elephant comes, but cannot lift it. A female approaches and tries to lift it, but cannot. Then they both cry out, and twelve elephants come, yet they cannot lift the one who fell. Then they all cry out, and immediately a tiny elephant comes and puts its mouth and its trunk <note>original: "promuscida"</note> underneath the large elephant and lifts it up. Moreover, the tiny elephant has this property: where its hair and bones have been burned, no evil thing happens, nor can a dragon <note>original: "draco"; in medieval bestiaries, the dragon or serpent was the elephant's mortal enemy, representing the devil.</note> approach. 

The large elephant and its female signify Adam and his wife Eve. For while they were in the flesh, pleasing to God before the time of their transgression <note>original: "preuaricacionem"; referring to the Fall of Man in Genesis.</note>, they did not know sexual union, nor did they have knowledge of sin. But when the woman ate from the tree—that is, the intellectual mandrake <note>The text equates the mandrake from the previous page with the fruit of the Tree of Knowledge.</note>—she gave it to her husband; afterward she became pregnant because of...

<summary>This page describes the legendary medieval belief that elephants lack knee joints and can only be rescued by a 'tiny elephant' (symbolizing Christ), followed by an allegory linking the elephant's mating habits to Adam and Eve.</summary>
<keywords>elephant, medieval bestiary, knee joints, hunting myths, allegory, Adam and Eve, mandrake, dragon, Christ symbol</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the elephant myth, specifically linking the various elephants in the story to figures from Christian salvation history—the Law, the Prophets, and Christ—before returning to the physical and moral traits of the animal.</meta>

...because they went out of paradise. For as long as they were in paradise, Adam did not know his wife <note>original: "non cognovit adam uxorem suam"; a biblical euphemism for sexual intercourse, signifying that in medieval thought, carnal lust was a consequence of the Fall.</note>. And conceiving, she bore her offspring upon the **reproachable waters**, of which the prophet says: "Save me, O God, for the waters have entered even unto my soul" <note>original: "Salvum me fac deus quoniam intraverunt aquae usque ad animam meam"; a quote from Psalm 69:1.</note>. And immediately the **dragon** <note>The dragon or serpent is the traditional enemy of the elephant in bestiaries, representing the Devil.</note> subjected them, and made them strangers to their high stronghold. This means they did not please God.

Then the **large elephant** came—that is, the **Law**—and it did not raise him up, just as the priest did not raise the man who fell among thieves <note>A reference to the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10), where the Priest and Levite represent the old Law failing to save humanity.</note>. Nor did the **twelve elephants** lift him up—that is, the **choir of the prophets**—just as the Levite did not lift that wounded man whom we mentioned. 

But the **spiritual elephant** <note>original: "intelligibilis elephantus"; this refers to the allegorical or higher meaning of the creature.</note>—that is, our **Lord Jesus Christ**—although he is greater than all, was made **tiny** <note>This refers to the "tiny elephant" from the previous page's story. In bestiaries, Christ's humility in the Incarnation is symbolized by the small elephant that succeeds where others fail.</note> because he humbled himself, becoming obedient even unto death, so that he might lift man up. He is the **spiritual Samaritan** who placed the man upon his beast of burden. For he himself, though wounded, took away our infirmities and bore our sins. Moreover, "Samaritan" is interpreted as **guardian** <note>The text draws on a traditional etymology of the Hebrew word for Samaritan, linking it to the verb 'shamar,' meaning to watch or guard.</note>, of whom David says: "The Lord is the guardian of the little ones." 

Where the Lord is present, the devil cannot approach. As for **elephants**, indeed, they break whatever they wrap with their **trunk** <note>original: "promuscida"</note>. With their foot, indeed, they destroy whatever they crush as if by the fall of a massive ruin. They never fight over females, for they know no **adulteries**. There is in them...

<summary>This page concludes a religious allegory where the elephant's rescue represents Christ (the "tiny elephant") saving fallen humanity after the Law and Prophets failed. It also describes the elephant's physical power and its reputation for moral purity and monogamy.</summary>
<keywords>Adam and Eve, Fall of Man, Law of Moses, Prophets, Jesus Christ, Good Samaritan, humility, elephant trunk, fidelity, bestiary allegory</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes the allegorical and moral description of the elephant—specifically its legendary kindness—before transitioning to the entry for the beaver. The previous page ended by describing the "tiny elephant" as a symbol for Christ’s humility.</meta>

...the virtue of **clemency** <note>original: *clemencie*; here referring to the elephant’s legendary mercy and kindness toward humans and other animals.</note>. Indeed, if by chance they see a person wandering lost through the deserts, they offer guidance until they reach familiar paths. Or, if they encounter crowded flocks of livestock, they make a way for themselves with a **gentle hand** <note>original: *blanda manu*; the Latin "hand" refers to the elephant's trunk, which medieval authors often described as a versatile limb.</note> so that no "weapon" <note>referring to their tusks</note> might harm the animal in their path. If they ever happen to engage in a struggle, they show no small care for the injured; for they receive the tired and wounded into the middle of their group.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature with a red and blue border. On the left, three men in medieval attire (tunics, hose, and hoods) and a hunting dog are depicted. There is an animal called the **beaver** <note>Latin: *castor*.</note>, which is exceedingly gentle. Its testicles are highly suitable for medicine. Concerning this animal, the **Physiologus** <note>The *Physiologus* was an ancient text that served as the primary source for medieval bestiaries, providing both natural lore and Christian allegories.</note> says that when the beaver realizes a hunter is pursuing him, he bites off his own testicles with his teeth and throws them into the hunter's face, and thus he escapes by fleeing. If, however... <note>The text likely continues to explain that if another hunter pursues a beaver that has already lost its testicles, the beaver shows its empty groin to prove it is no longer worth hunting.</note>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on the beaver (castor), specifically explaining the moral allegory of why the beaver is said to castrate itself to escape hunters.</meta>

...if it should happen again that another hunter pursues him: he stands up and shows his **private parts** <note>original: *uirilia sua*</note> to the hunter. When the hunter sees that he lacks testicles, he departs from him. In this way, everyone who conducts themselves according to the commandment of God and wishes to live **chastely** <note>original: *caste*</note> cuts away from themselves all vices and all acts of impurity, and throws them from themselves into the face of the devil. Then the devil, seeing that the person possesses nothing that belongs to him, departs from them in confusion. That person truly lives in God and is not captured by the devil, who is said to pursue and seize such people. The **beaver** <note>Latin: *Castor*</note> is so named from the act of **castrating** <note>original: *castrando*. This reflects a common medieval folk etymology where the animal's name was believed to derive from its behavior.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the moralizing account of the beaver to the entries for the ibex and the hyena. It continues the bestiary's tradition of assigning Christian allegorical meanings to animal behaviors, moving from the theme of personal purity to the theme of spiritual balance and the nature of sin.</meta>

There is an animal called the **ibex** <note>original: *ibex*</note> that has two horns. Their strength is so great that if the animal should fall from the high mountains down to the valleys, it supports its entire body on these two horns and remains unhurt.

This animal represents **learned men** <note>original: *eruditos homines*</note> who, through the harmony of the **Two Testaments** <note>A reference to the Old and New Testaments of the Bible.</note>, are accustomed to balance whatever adversity might happen to them, as if with a healthy moderation. Supported as if by two horns, they sustain the good works they perform through the witness of the Old Testament and the reading of the **Gospels** <note>original: *euangelice lectionis*</note>.

There is an animal called the **hyena** <note>original: *hyena*</note> that lives in the **tombs** <note>original: *sepulcris*</note> of the dead and feeds on their corpses.

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the hyena, which began on the previous page. It moves from the physical and behavioral traits of the beast to its allegorical interpretation regarding the people of Israel and the nature of spiritual inconstancy.</meta>

Its nature is such that it is sometimes male and sometimes female, and therefore it is an **unclean animal** <note>original: *immundum animal*; according to Levitical law, animals that were perceived as "doubled" or changing were often labeled unclean.</note>. Since its spine is rigid in one continuous piece, it cannot turn unless it rotates its entire body.

**Solinus** <note>Gaius Julius Solinus, a 3rd-century Roman grammarian whose "Collection of Remarkable Facts" was a primary source for medieval bestiaries.</note> relates many marvels about it: first, that it follows the stalls of shepherds and circles houses at night. By constant listening, it learns names so that it can imitate the human voice, intending to attack a person after they have been called out into the night. It also mimics human vomiting, and by making sounds like false sobbing, it devours the dogs it has lured. If hunting dogs, while following it, happen to touch its shadow, they lose their voices and are unable to bark. This same hyena searches for buried bodies and digs up graves.

The **children of Israel** <note>original: *filii israel*</note> are compared to this beast, for in the beginning they served the living God. Later, devoted to wealth and **luxury** <note>original: *luxurie*; in a medieval moral context, this refers specifically to the sin of lust or excessive indulgence.</note>, they worshipped idols. Therefore, the **Prophet** <note>Jeremiah 12:9.</note> compared the **synagogue** <note>original: *synagogam*</note> to an unclean animal, saying: "My inheritance has become to me like the den of a hyena."

Therefore, whoever among us serves luxury and **avarice** <note>original: *auaricie*; extreme greed for wealth.</note> is compared to this beast, since they are neither men nor women—that is, they are neither faithful nor entirely unfaithful. Rather, they are undoubtedly those of whom **Solomon** <note>While the text cites Solomon, this verse is actually found in the Epistle of James 1:8.</note> says: "A man of double mind is inconstant in all his ways." Of such people, the Lord says: "You cannot serve God and **Mammon** <note>original: *mammone*; a biblical term for wealth, often personified as a false god.</note>." This beast...

<summary>This page describes the mythical traits of the hyena—such as its rigid spine, ability to mimic human speech, and grave-digging—as an allegory for the "double-minded" person. It specifically compares the animal to those who alternate between faith and worldly greed.</summary>

<keywords>hyena, Solinus, children of Israel, luxury, avarice, synagogue, Solomon, Mammon, spiritual inconstancy, double-mindedness</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical and behavioral description of the hyena, focusing on its magical attributes and its hybrid offspring, the crocotta. It then transitions to the entry for the bonnacon.</meta>

...[the hyena] holds a stone in its eyes, called the **hyena-stone** <note>original: *hyenam*; medieval lapidaries (books about gems) claimed the *hyaenia* stone was found in the eye of the beast and possessed divinatory powers.</note>. If anyone holds this stone under their tongue, it is believed they can **predict the future**. Furthermore, any animal that the hyena circles three times is unable to move. For this reason, authorities have declared that it possesses **magical knowledge** <note>original: *magicam scienciam*</note>. 

In parts of **Ethiopia**, it mates with a lioness, from which is born a monster named the **crocotta** <note>original: *crocote*; a mythical creature often described as a combination of a dog and a wolf, or a hyena and a lioness.</note>; it likewise imitates the voices of humans. It never shifts its gaze, and maintains its sight without turning its eyes. In its mouth, there are no gums. It has one continuous tooth which, so that it is never blunted, is naturally encased like a **folding box** <note>original: *capsularum modo*; describing a sheath-like mechanism where the teeth of the upper and lower jaws fit together perfectly to maintain sharpness.</note>.

### The Bonnacon

In Asia, an animal is born which is called the **bonnacon** <note>original: *bonnacon*; a legendary bull-like creature known for its inward-curving horns, which are useless for defense, leading it to protect itself by ejecting caustic dung.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the description of the bonnacon, which began on the previous page, detailing its unusual physical traits and its unique chemical defense. It then begins a new entry for the ape, focusing on its human-like qualities and its reaction to the phases of the moon.</meta>

It has a **bull-like head**, and thereafter its whole body is similar, except for its **horse's mane**. Its horns, however, curve back upon themselves with such a complex twist that if anyone should run into them, they would not be wounded. But whatever protection its forehead denies this monster, its **belly provides** in abundance. For when it turns to flee, it discharges dung from its bowels over a distance of **three acres** <note>original: *trium iugerum*; a *iugerum* was a Roman unit of land area, roughly equivalent to five-eighths of an acre. The text suggests the beast could cover a massive area with its defense.</note>, the heat of which **burns** whatever it touches. Thus, it wards off its pursuers with this harmful discharge.

### The Ape

**Apes** <note>original: *simie*; the Latin term *simia* was used broadly in the Middle Ages to describe monkeys and apes.</note> are so named in the Latin tongue because a great **likeness to human reason** <note>original: *similitudo rationis humane*; medieval scholars loved "folk etymologies," believing the word *simia* (ape) was derived from *similitudo* (likeness).</note> is perceived in them. These creatures, being astute regarding the **elements** <note>original: *elementorum*; this likely refers to their sensitivity to the cycles of nature or celestial bodies.</note>, rejoice at the **new moon**, but are saddened by the middle and the waning moon. The nature of the ape is such that when she has **given birth to twins**...

<meta>This page continues the moralizing description of the Ape (Simia), which began on the previous page with its human-like behavior. It specifically details a famous medieval fable regarding how the ape treats its young and uses the animal's physical traits as an allegory for the Devil. It also introduces subspecies like the Dog-headed ape and the Sphinx.</meta>

...loves one of its young and despises the other. If it should happen at any time that they are pursued by hunters, the mother clasps the one she loves in front of her in her arms, and carries the one she hates clinging to her neck. But when she becomes exhausted from walking on two legs, she drops the one she loves, though she does so unwillingly <note>original: *uolens*; while the manuscript says "willingly," the context of the Bestiary tradition usually implies she is forced by fatigue to drop the favorite, whereas the hated child survives by clinging on its own.</note> <unclear>n</unclear>, and carries the one she hates, even though she does not wish to. 

The **ape has no tail**. In this, it represents the **Devil**, who has a head but indeed has no tail. And although the ape is ugly as a whole, its hind parts are even more foul and horrible than its front. The Devil had a beginning when he was an angel in heaven, but he was a hypocrite and deceitful within, and so he "lost his tail" because he will perish entirely at the end, as the **Apostle** says: "Whom the Lord Jesus shall slay with the breath of his mouth" <note>A quotation from 2 Thessalonians 2:8, used here to explain that the Devil has no "end" or "tail" because he will be destroyed.</note>. 

**Ape** <note>original: *Simia*</note> is a Greek name meaning "flat-nosed" <note>original: *pressis naribus*; likely referring to the Greek *simos*, meaning snub-nosed.</note>. This is why we call them **simians**, because they have flat noses and foul faces with skin that sags in ugly wrinkles. Though goats <note>original: *capellari*</note> may also have flat noses.

**Dog-headed apes** <note>original: *Cynocephali*; from the Greek *kyon* (dog) and *kephale* (head).</note> do have tails; this is the only distinction between them and those mentioned previously. These dog-headed creatures are themselves numbered among the apes. They are very common in the regions of **Ethiopia**, violent in their leaping, and fierce of bite. They are never so tame that they are not more often rabid. Among the apes are also counted the **Sphinxes** <note>original: *sphinges*</note>, which have shaggy limbs and are easily tamed, even to the point of forgetting their natural instincts <note>original: *sensuum obliuionem*; literally "forgetfulness of the senses," suggesting they become docile enough to lose their wild nature.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the classification of apes and monkey-like creatures. There are also those they call **satyrs**, who have a very pleasing face and are restless in their gestures with various movements. The **Callitriches** <note>original: *callitrices*; a name derived from the Greek for "beautiful hair," likely referring to marmosets or similar long-haired monkeys.</note> differ from the others in almost their entire appearance. They have a beard on their face and a broad tail. To capture them is not difficult, but it is rare to bring them away alive <note>original: *pferre rarum*; literally "to bring forth is rare," suggesting they often died in captivity or during transport.</note>. Nor do they live under any other sky than that of **Ethiopia** <note>original: *ethiopico*; in medieval geography, "Ethiopia" was a broad term for much of Sub-Saharan Africa, believed to be the only climate where such exotic beasts could survive.</note>.
