--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on the stag (cervus) which began on the previous page. It explores the etymology of the name, the stag's legendary enmity with snakes, its medicinal knowledge, and its social behaviors, finally transitioning into a Christian allegory where the stag represents members of the Church.</meta>

**Stags** are named from "the horns" <note>original: *ap̄ tou ceraton*, from the Greek *apo tou keraton*</note>; for in Greek, horns are called *kerata* <note>original: *cerata*</note>.

These creatures are the enemies of serpents. When they feel themselves weighed down by infirmity, they draw snakes out of their holes with the breath of their nostrils; and having overcome the deadly nature of the venom, they are restored by eating them as food. They also eat the herb **dittany** <note>original: *dictamnū*; an aromatic plant, *Origanum dictamnus*, traditionally believed to have the power to heal wounds and eject weapons from the body</note>. Having grazed upon this plant, they can shake out any arrows they have received. 

Furthermore, they marvel at the sound of pipes, listening intently with their ears pricked up; yet, when their ears are lowered, they hear nothing. Stags also possess this nature: for the love of another homeland, they change their pastures and support one another. If they must cross immense rivers or seas, they place their heads upon the haunches of those going before them; by succeeding one another in turn, they feel no fatigue from the weight. And if they encounter muddy places, they leap over them very quickly so that they are not defiled by the filth.

They have another nature as well: after eating a serpent, they run to a spring, and by drinking from it, they shed their hair and all their old appearance <note>original: *uenustatem*; literally "beauty," referring here to the shedding of their coat and antlers as a form of rejuvenation</note>. 

These natural traits are seen to correspond by a fitting and competent reason to the members of the **Holy Church**. For while they leave this homeland—that is, the world—for the love of the heavenly homeland, they help one another; that is, the more perfect members advance and support the less perfect through their example and good works. And if they find a place of sin, they immediately leap over it, and afterward...

<summary>This page describes the stag's legendary abilities to heal itself with snakes and dittany, its social behavior of helping others cross water, and its allegorical representation of the faithful supporting one another in the Church.</summary>
<keywords>stag, cervus, snake-eater, dittany, dictamnum, animal allegory, Holy Church, rejuvenation</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the stag, moving from its physical habits to its spiritual significance as an allegory for the Christian faithful, before returning to its natural history, breeding habits, and medicinal uses.</meta>

...the incorporation of the devil, that is, after their sins. For after their **sins**, they run with confession to Christ, who is the true fountain; and drawing from His precepts, they are renewed, having laid down the old age of sin. 

The males of this kind, when the appointed time of **desire** <note>original: *ueneris*; referring here to the mating season associated with the goddess Venus</note> approaches, boil with the rage of lust. The females, though they may be joined with the males earlier, do not conceive before the rising of the star **Arcturus** <note>original: *arum sidus*, likely a contraction of *Arcturum sidus*; a bright star in the constellation Boötes, used by ancient and medieval authors to mark the change of seasons</note>. 

Nor do they bring forth their offspring in just any place, but they hide them with tender care; and having hidden them among the depths of shrubs or thick grasses, they discipline them with a blow of their feet to remain hidden. When their strength has matured for flight, they teach them how to run through exercise, and accustom them to leap across steep places. 

When they hear the barking of dogs, they head downwind, so that their scent departs along with them. They are stunned by all things <note>referring to a state of sensory overload or fixation</note>, and therefore they more easily present themselves to those shooting arrows. 

Of the horns, that which was the **right** one is more effective for healing. If you desire to put snakes to flight, burn whichever of the horns you wish. Their teeth show their old age, for as they grow old, either few teeth are found or none at all. 

To learn of their longevity, **Alexander the Great** put collars on many stags; these were captured a hundred years later and did not yet show signs of old age. **Fawns** <note>original: *hinnuli*</note> are the children of stags, named from "restraining" <note>original: *ab inuincire*; a medieval etymological play suggesting they are 'bound' or restrained by their mother's command</note> because they are hidden at the mother's nod. Against poisons, the **rennet** <note>original: *coagulum*; a substance used to curdle milk, here taken from the stomach of an unborn fawn</note> of a fawn killed in its mother's womb is exceedingly effective. The father never follows or accompanies them.

<summary>This page concludes the Christian allegory of the stag as a repentant sinner before detailing the species' mating cycles, the maternal care and training of fawns, and the stag's legendary longevity as tested by Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>bestiary, stag, allegory, confession, Arcturus, Alexander the Great, fawns, medicinal horns, rennet</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the stag’s physical properties, specifically its longevity and medicinal uses, before transitioning into a lengthy Christological allegory based on the stag's behavior toward serpents and its need for water.</meta>

For this reason, ointments prepared from the **marrow of the temples** <note>original: *medullis temporum*; the marrow or fatty tissue found in the skull/temple area of the stag</note> soothe the fevers of those suffering from illness. We read that many people who were accustomed to eating **venison** <note>original: *cervinam carnem*</note> in the early morning lived to a great age without fevers; this benefit remains as long as they were killed by only a single wound.

For the **stag** signifies holy men who desire the Lord, regarding which the prophet says: *“As the hart longs for the water-springs, so my soul longs for you, O God”* <note>original: *Sicut cervus desiderat ad fontes aquarum: ita desiderat anima mea ad te deus*; a citation of Psalm 42:1</note>. For that animal is, first, harmless; second, very swift; and third, it is parched with a burning thirst. 

It draws out **serpents** from their holes with its nostrils; once it has swallowed them, it is driven by the boiling heat of the venom. It hastens with all possible speed to a **fountain of waters**. For they love to make themselves pure with the sweetest water. 

This beautiful comparison ardently stirs our own desire: so that whenever we drink the venom of the **ancient serpent** <note>a traditional reference to the Devil or original sin</note> and feel the heat in our deepest throats, we may hasten immediately to the fountain of **divine mercy**. Thus, whatever is contracted through the adversity of sin may be overcome by the purity of that sweetest draught. 

Nor is it without meaning that the prophet spoke of the **fountains** of waters rather than just "waters." For the fountain of waters is **Christ the Lord**, from whom flow all things that provide refreshment. For ordinary streams can often dry up, but the fountain of waters is always overflowing. Therefore, it is rightly said that one must hasten to the liquid of that sacred origin, where...

<summary>This page concludes the medicinal lore of the stag before detailing its primary allegorical role: just as a stag eats a serpent and seeks water to neutralize the venom, the Christian must seek Christ to purge the "venom" of sin.</summary>
<keywords>stag, venison, medicinal marrow, Psalm 42, serpent, Christ as fountain, divine mercy, allegory, Bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the stag, moving from its physical thirst to its symbolic representation of the Patriarchs, Apostles, and the faithful. It concludes the section on the stag by explaining how its mountain-dwelling nature relates to the spiritual heights of the prophets.</meta>

...so that our **desire** <note>original: *desiderium nostrum*</note> might never have a cessation <note>original: *reminium*; likely a variant of *remedium* or *remanere*, suggesting an end or a pause in the spiritual longing for God</note>. 

Also, it is written elsewhere: *“The high mountains are for the stags”* <note>original: *montes excelsi cervis*; a reference to Psalm 104:18</note>. Furthermore, the stags represent the **patriarchs** <note>original: *patriarchae*</note>; for this reason, it is said in the **Song of Songs** <note>original: *cantica canticorum*</note> concerning the Savior—who was born of their lineage according to the flesh: *“Flee, my beloved, and be like a roe or a young hart”* <note>original: *Fuge dilecte mi . assimilare capree . hinnuloque cervorum*; Song of Songs 8:14</note>. 

The stags also represent the **apostles**, as in the Psalm: *“The voice of the Lord preparing the stags”* <note>original: *Vox domini praeparantis cervos*; Psalm 29:9</note>. 

The **hind** <note>original: *cerva*; the female deer</note> represents the **flesh of the Lord**. In the writings of **Solomon**: *“A most beloved hind and a most graceful fawn; let her breasts inebriate you”* <note>original: *Cerva karissima & gratissimi hinnulus ubera eius inebriant te*; referring to Proverbs 5:19</note>—this refers to the **evangelical doctrine**. 

The hinds also signify the **apostles** because they give birth spiritually, as it says in **Job**: *“Do you know the time when the wild goats bring forth? Or have you observed the hinds when they calve?”* <note>Job 39:1</note>. 

The **stag** is, as we have said, a devourer of **poisonous serpents**; it leaps over thorny places and is endowed with the greatest agility. It loves to dwell in the highest mountains. To this animal the **faithful** <note>original: *fideles*</note> are rightly compared, for they crush the **devil** <note>original: *diabolum*</note> when they turn his wicked deeds into the praise and glory of the Lord. They pass through the path of this world—as if through **thorns**—by means of their good conduct, and they dwell in the **mountains**, which are the **apostles** and **prophets**. Through their holy preaching, these figures deserved to be the solid peaks of this world.

<summary>This page concludes the stag's entry by applying various biblical verses to the animal's behavior, identifying the stag and hind as symbols for the Patriarchs, Apostles, and the flesh of Christ. The stag's ability to navigate thorns and mountains represents the faithful overcoming the devil's temptations through the guidance of the prophets.</summary>

<keywords>stag, hind, Christology, Patriarchs, Apostles, Song of Songs, Proverbs, Job, spiritual warfare, serpents, mountains</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the bestiary's exploration of deer-like creatures. While the previous page focused on the common stag and its spiritual role as a hunter of serpents, this page introduces the "goat-stag" and the doe, transitioning into the allegorical relationship between these animals and Christ's humility and innocence.</meta>

**Goat-stags** <note>original: *tragelaphi*; a creature from ancient natural history thought to be a cross between a goat and a deer</note> are named by the Greeks <note>original: *agreas*; likely a scribal error for *Graece*</note> because they are of the same species as **stags**, yet they have shaggy shoulders like **he-goats** and chins covered with long, hairy beards. They are found nowhere else except around the **Phasis** <note>original: *phasidem*; the Rioni River in modern-day Georgia, which the ancients considered the boundary between Europe and Asia</note>. **Fawns** <note>original: *hinnuli*</note> are the offspring of stags. The **doe** <note>original: *dammula*; often referring to a fallow deer</note> is so called because it flees from the hand <note>original: *de manu*; a folk etymology linking *damma* (doe) to *manus* (hand)</note>; it is a timid and defenseless animal, about which **Martial** <note>a Roman poet of the 1st century AD</note> wrote:

> The wild boar is feared for its tusks, horns defend the stag.
> We defenseless does—why are we anything but prey?

These animals also signify either **Christ** or **innocent people**. For this reason, the **Bride** says of her **Bridegroom** in the **Song of Songs**:

> My beloved is like a doe or a young stag.

Therefore, **Christ** is likened to the doe in the **assumption of flesh** <note>the Incarnation, when God took on human form</note> and in **humility**; but in the variety of His **virtues** and **innocence**...

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the goat-stag and the doe, using them as allegories for Christ's humility and the vulnerability of the innocent.</summary>
<keywords>goat-stag, doe, fawn, Martial, Christ, Song of Songs, Incarnation, humility, innocence, Phasis River</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical comparison of Christ to various animals. The previous page discussed the doe and the young stag as symbols of Christ’s humility and virtue; this page concludes that thought by connecting Christ’s human lineage to the biblical patriarchs before transitioning to the description of the goat.</meta>

...**young stag** <note>original: *hinnulo ceruorum*</note> of the stags, that is, of the **patriarchs** <note>the biblical ancestors of the faith, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note>: from whom, according to the **flesh** <note>referring to His human lineage</note>, He took His origin.

<image-desc>A framed rectangular illumination from a medieval bestiary. The scene depicts four goats in a stylized landscape against a background divided between a central gold-leaf panel and a deep blue field patterned with small white crosses. On the left, a white goat stands on its hind legs reaching for a tree with pinkish bulbous foliage; beneath it, a light-green goat lies curled on the ground. **There is an animal** which in Latin is called the **he-goat** <note>original: *caper*</note>, because it seeks out <note>original: *captet*</note> sharp and rugged places <note>original: *aspera*; a traditional medieval etymology linking the name of the animal to its habitat</note>. Some call the **wild goat** <note>original: *capream*</note> by that name because of the clattering sound of its hooves <note>original: *a crepita*</note>. These are the wild goats which the Greeks called **dorcas** <note>a Greek term for gazelles or deer, derived from the verb *derkomai*, "to see clearly"</note> because they see so very keenly. They dwell in high mountains and, seeing people approaching from a long distance, they can distinguish whether they are **hunters** or **travelers**. In this same way, our **Lord Jesus Christ** loves the high mountains, that is...

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the stag by linking Christ's human ancestry to the Patriarchs and introduces the goat, whose keen eyesight allows it to distinguish friends from enemies.</summary>
<keywords>patriarchs, lineage of Christ, he-goat, wild goat, keen sight, allegory, discernment</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical discussion of the wild goat (caprea) from the previous page, transitioning from its physical habits to its symbolic meaning as Christ, focusing on divine omniscience and the foreknowledge of the Passion.</meta>

...prophets and apostles. Thus, in the **Song of Songs** <note>original: *Canticis Canticorum*; a book of the Bible interpreted by medieval scholars as a love song between Christ and the Church</note>, it is said: "Behold, my **beloved** <note>original: *fratruelis*; literally "cousin" or "kin," used here to denote the Bridegroom</note> comes like a **wild goat** <note>original: *caprea*; often identifying a roe deer or gazelle in this context</note> leaping over the mountains, bounding across the hills." And just as the wild goat grazes in the valleys, so our **Lord** <note>original: *Dominus*</note> grazes in the **Church** <note>original: *ecclesia*</note>. The good works of **Christians** <note>original: *xpistianorum*</note> are His food—He who says: "I was hungry and you gave me something to eat; I was thirsty and you gave me something to drink."

The valleys of the mountains are understood as the secluded places of the Church. Thus, it is said in the Song of Songs: "Behold, my beloved, turn and be like a wild goat and a **young stag** <note>original: *hinnuloque cervorum*</note>." Because this animal has the sharpest keenness of vision, perceives everything, and recognizes things from a distance, it signifies our Lord, who is the **God of all knowledge** <note>original: *dominus scienciarum deus*</note>. 

And elsewhere it says: "For though the Lord is high, He regards the humble, and the haughty He knows from afar." He created, established, rules, sees, and perceives all things; and before anything arises in our hearts, He foresees and knows it. Finally, just as the wild goat recognizes the arrival of hunters from a distance, so **Christ** <note>original: *xpistus*</note> foreknew the traps of His **betrayer** <note>original: *perditioris*; literally "the lost one," referring to Judas Iscariot</note>, saying: "**Behold, he who betrays me is at hand**" <note>original: *Ecce appropinquat qui me tradet*; quoting the Gospel of Matthew 26:46</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the wild goat, comparing its keen eyesight to Christ's omniscience and its ability to spot hunters to Christ's foreknowledge of Judas's betrayal.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, wild goat, roe deer, Christ, omniscience, Judas Iscariot, Church, good works, allegory, vision</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the wild goat (caprea), moving from its physical traits of keen vision and mountain-climbing to its symbolic role as a model for Christian preachers and a metaphor for Christ’s healing power.</meta>

The **wild goat** <term>caprea</term> <note>Though the Latin *caprea* usually refers to a roe deer, medieval bestiaries often blended the characteristics of goats and deer to symbolize spiritual agility.</note> has these natures: that by grazing, it moves from high places to even higher ground. With the sharpness of its eyes, it picks out good herbs from harmful ones. It "ruminates" <note>chews the cud</note> its herbs. When wounded, it runs to the **dittany** <term>dictannum</term> <note>A medicinal herb, *Origanum dictamnus*, which ancient and medieval writers believed could miraculously expel arrows from the bodies of wounded animals.</note>, and as soon as it touches the plant, it is healed. 

In this same way, good **preachers** <note>original: *predicatores*</note>, feeding upon the law of the Lord and upon good works as if delighting in a rich pasture, ascend from virtue to virtue. With the "eyes of the heart," they distinguish good teachings from evil ones. They "ruminate" on these chosen thoughts—that is, they examine what is good—and once "chewed over," they commit them to memory. When they are wounded by sin, they run back to **Christ** <note>original: *xp̄m*; an abbreviation for *Christum*</note>, confessing their sins, and are quickly healed. Therefore, Christ is fittingly called the "dittany," for just as the dittany plant drives the iron <note>original: *ferrum*; referring to the metal head of an arrow</note> out of a wound...

<summary>This page explains how the wild goat’s habit of climbing mountains and seeking the medicinal herb dittany symbolizes the spiritual ascent of preachers and the healing power of Christ in confession.</summary>
<keywords>wild goat, roe deer, dittany, allegory, preachers, confession, spiritual healing, virtue, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page concludes the spiritual allegory of the wild goat (caprea) and the medicinal herb dittany, transitioning into the description of the monoceros (unicorn), focusing on its formidable physical traits and untamable nature.</meta>

...and **Christ** <note>original: "xp̃c"; a Greek-based abbreviation for "Christus"</note> heals the wound through **confession**; he [drives out] the **devil** <note>original: "diabolum"</note> and forgives the sin.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature with a gold-leaf background framed in blue, white, and red. The illustration depicts a monoceros (unicorn), painted in blue with a tan mane and a single long, straight horn. The creature has a horse-like body and cloven-like feet, standing on stylized yellowish ground. Three ornamental trees with lobed, multicolored foliage (green, white, and orange) surround the animal. The **monoceros** <term>monoceros</term> <note>Literally "one-horn." While the modern "unicorn" is often depicted as a graceful horse, medieval writers followed the descriptions of ancient naturalists like Pliny the Elder, who described it as a composite beast of great power.</note> is a monster with a terrifying bellow. It has the body of a horse, the feet of an **elephant** <note>original: "elephantis"</note>, and a tail very much like that of a **stag** <note>original: "ceruo"</note>. A horn extends from the middle of its forehead with a magnificent shine, reaching a length of four feet. It is so sharp that whatever it attacks is easily pierced by its strike. It does not fall into the power of men while alive; and while it can indeed be killed, it cannot be captured alive.

<summary>This page concludes the lesson on Christ healing the soul through confession and introduces the monoceros, a fierce, multi-featured beast with an incredibly sharp four-foot horn that defies capture.</summary>
<keywords>Christ, confession, monoceros, unicorn, elephant, stag, hunting, animal composite</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the monoceros (unicorn) to the entry on the bear, shifting focus from a beast of capture to a creature defined by its unique method of motherhood and physical development.</meta>

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature set within a decorative border of blue and red. It depicts a large brown bear standing over a small red lump of flesh on a grassy mound, which it is licking. This illustrates the medieval lore that bear cubs are born unformed and licked into shape by their mothers. The background features a dark blue sky with white dots, framed by stylized trees with pink, blue, and gold-trimmed foliage on a gold leaf ground.</image-desc>

The **Bear** <term>ursus</term> <note>original: "Ursus"</note> is said to be so named because it fashions its offspring with its mouth, as if it were "begun" <note>original: "orsus"; the author is using a common medieval etymological pun, suggesting the word for bear comes from the Latin word for a beginning or undertaking</note>. For they say that they give birth to unformed offspring, and that what is born is a certain [lump of] flesh. The mother, by licking this, arranges it into limbs. From this comes the saying: "The tongue shapes the offspring which the she-bear has brought forth." 

But the immaturity of the birth is what causes this. Indeed, the she-bear gives birth on the thirtieth day after conception; thus it happens that this rushed fertility produces unformed offspring. The head of bears is weak, but their strength is great<note>The text here ends abruptly on "max-", likely intended to be "maxima" (greatest or very great).</note>...

<summary>This page describes the medieval belief that bear cubs are born as formless lumps of flesh and must be literally "licked into shape" by their mothers due to their short thirty-day gestation period.</summary>

<keywords>bear, ursus, gestation, animal lore, motherhood, medieval science, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical and behavioral description of the bear, following the discussion of its name and the birth of its unformed young. It focuses on the bear's medicinal knowledge, its human-like mating habits, and the details of its hibernation and gestation.</meta>

...[strength] in the arms and loins, wherefore they sometimes stand upright. They also do not neglect the diligence of healing, for they know how to treat themselves if they are afflicted by a serious ailment or wounded by injuries. They apply their sores to the herb whose name is **mullein** <term>flomus</term> <note>original: "flomus"; from the Greek 'phlomos', likely referring to the Great Mullein plant, historically used for skin conditions</note> (as the Greeks call it), so that they may be cured by touch alone. A sick bear also devours ants. **Numidian bears** <note>These were a North African subspecies, now extinct, famously used in Roman arenas</note> surpass the others, at least in the thickness of their shaggy hair; for their breeding habits are the same regardless of the place where they are born.

They do not mate in the same manner as other four-legged animals, but rather, joined in mutual embraces, they are coupled just like human unions. The desire for love <note>original: "veneris", referring to Venus, the goddess of love and personification of sexual desire</note> is aroused in them by winter. The males respect the privacy of the pregnant females; and although they may share the same dens <term>foveis</term>, they divide themselves into separate sleeping pits for their rest. The moon provides an opportune time for them, for indeed, the thirtieth day releases the womb. 

From this it happens that a rushed fertility <term>fecunditas</term> creates unformed offspring. They bring forth tiny bits of flesh, which are white in color and have no eyes. By licking these with the tongue, they gradually shape them into limbs, and sometimes they warm them against their chests so that, heated by constant brooding, they might draw the **breath of life** <term>animalem spiritum</term> <note>In medieval science, this was the vital force that allowed an animal to move and perceive</note>. 

Meanwhile, there is no food. Truly, during the first fourteen days, the males are so buried in sleep that they cannot be awakened even by wounds. They remain hidden for three months. Soon, having gone out into the open day, they suffer such an unaccustomed intensity of light that you would think...

<summary>This page details the bear's self-healing abilities using herbs and ants, its human-like mating posture, and the specific 30-day gestation that results in unformed cubs which must be licked into shape.</summary>
<keywords>bear, ursus, Numidian bear, mating, gestation, animal healing, hibernation, animal spirit, mullein, medieval zoology</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the bear’s behavior, specifically its diet, its methods of hunting, and its medicinal use of ants, before transitioning into a theological allegory regarding the bear's symbolic meaning in scripture.</meta>

Bound by blindness <note>original: "Obstricta cecitate"; likely referring to the bear's single-minded focus or a physical condition resulting from its diet</note>, they lie in wait for the beehives of bees and especially seek out the honeycombs. They do this for no other reason than to seize the honey. When they have tasted the **mandrake apples** <note>original: "mandragore mala"; the fruit of the mandrake plant was often considered poisonous or narcotic in medieval lore</note>, they die; but they seek a remedy lest the evil prevail in its first stage, and they devour ants to recover their health. 

But when they attack bulls, they know in which parts they may most effectively weaken them; they seek nothing other than the horns or the nostrils. They strike the nostrils so that the pain may be sharper in that tender <term>teniore</term> <note>original: "teniore", a variation of "tenuiore", meaning thinner or more sensitive</note> place. 

The bear, therefore, according to **allegory** <term>allegoriam</term> <note>the practice of interpreting texts or animals to reveal a hidden spiritual or moral meaning</note>, sometimes signifies the devil, the one who lays snares for the flock of the Lord. At other times, however, it signifies fierce and cruel leaders. Wherefore it is written in the **Book of Kings** <note>Referring to 2 Kings 2:23-24</note> that two bears coming out of the forest devoured the boys who were mocking the prophet **Elisha** <term>heliseum</term>. This signifies the two Roman princes, **Vespasian and Titus** <note>Roman Emperors who led the siege and destruction of Jerusalem in 70 AD</note>, who came out from the thicket of the Gentiles to devour the Jewish children who mocked our Savior, crucified at the place of Calvary. And likewise: "A lion and a bear came and took a ram from the flock" <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 17:34, where David describes protecting his sheep</note>.

Inside, a stylized creature resembling a bear or a wolf with a white body and greenish-gray shaded stripes along its neck and back is depicted in a leaping or running pose towards the right. In India is born a beast named the **leucrota** <note>original: "leucrota"; a mythical creature often described in medieval bestiaries as a hybrid of many animals, possibly based on early accounts of the hyena or antelope</note>.
It surpasses all other wild animals in speed. It is
as large as a donkey, with the hindquarters of a deer, the chest
and legs of a lion, the head of a horse, and cloven
hooves. Its mouth gapes open as far as its ears. In the
place of teeth, there is a single continuous bone. This indeed
describes its form, but with its voice, it imitates the sounds
of people speaking <note>original: "voce loquentiū sonos emulat̃"; folklore suggested this beast could mimic human speech to lure people into the wilderness where it could attack them</note>.

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>Continuing the description of the crocodile from the previous page, which detailed its name's origin from the saffron flower and its habitat in the Nile. This page concludes the physical description and transitions into a moral allegory comparing the beast’s habits to the behavior of hypocrites and sinners.</meta>

...armed with vast size <note>original: "immanitate"</note>. It possesses such hardness <note>original: "duricia"</note> of skin that it can withstand the blows of even the strongest stones upon its back without harm. It rests in the waters by night and on the ground by day. The male and female take turns guarding the eggs within. Certain fish, which have a saw-like crest <note>original: "serratam... cristam"</note>, kill the crocodile by cutting into the tender parts of its belly. Moreover, alone among all animals <note>original: "animalibus"</note>, it moves its upper jaw while keeping the lower jaw unmoved.

From its dung <note>original: "stercus"</note> an ointment <term>unguentum</term> is made, which old and wrinkled harlots use to prick and rub their faces until they appear beautiful, continuing until a flowing sweat washes it away. 

Hypocrites <note>original: "hypocrite"</note>, as well as the lustful <note>original: "luxuriosi"</note> and the greedy <note>original: "auari"</note>, carry the likeness of this beast. Although they are internally swollen with the weight of pride, stained by the filth of luxury, and besieged by the disease of greed, they nevertheless present themselves before men as rigid and most holy in their adherence to the justifications <note>original: "iustificationibus"</note> of the law. 

The crocodile rests in the water by night and on the ground by day because hypocrites, though they live luxuriously, nevertheless delight in being described as living holily and justly. Conscious of their own malice, they beat their hearts; yet they are always held by the habit of these things, a custom leading toward sins <note>original: "peccata"</note>. 

The beast does not move the upper part of its mouth because such people display the examples of the holy fathers <note>original: "sanctorum patrum"</note> and an abundance of words to others in their speech, while they show very little of what they say in their own lives. An ointment is made from its dung because the wicked are frequently praised by the impious for their evil sins...

<summary>This page concludes the physical description of the crocodile—noting its impenetrable skin and unique jaw—and provides an extensive moral allegory comparing the beast's dual nature and waste products to the vanity and deception of hypocrites.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, hypocrisy, moral allegory, medieval medicine, vanity, religious law, Nile, bestiary</keywords>

Smeared with ointment <note>original: "Lin[un]tur unguento"</note>, these men are extolled by the favors of this world. But when the strict Judge <note>original: "iudex districtus"</note> raises His hand to strike for their evil sins, then all that beauty of praise shall vanish like smoke <note>original: "velut fumus evanescat"</note>.

In India, a beast is born which is called the manticore <term>manticora; a legendary creature believed to have a human head and a lion's body</term>. It has a triple row of teeth <note>original: "triplici dentium ordine"</note> that meet together in alternating rows. It has the face of a man with gleaming blue-gray eyes <note>original: "glaucis oculis"</note> and is of a blood-red color. It possesses the body of a lion and a tail tipped with a sting like that of a scorpion. Its voice is like a hiss, so much so that it imitates the melodic notes of pipes <term>fistularum; specifically panpipes or flutes</term>. It hungers most greedily for human flesh. Its feet are like those of a stag <note>original: "cers," likely an abbreviation of "cervi"</note>, and it is so powerful in leaping that nothing can delay it nor... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence.</note>

Ethiopia sends a beast by the name of the parandrus <term>parandrus; a mythical creature from medieval bestiaries, often identified with the reindeer or elk</term>, which is the size of an ox: with a cloven hoof-print <note>original: "bisulco uestigio"</note>: with branching horns: with the head of a stag, the color of a bear, and with deep, shaggy hair <note>original: "pilis uillo profundo"</note>. They claim that this parandrus changes its appearance <note>original: "habitum"</note> out of fear, and when it hides, it takes on the likeness of whatever thing it happens to be near—whether that be a white rock, or a green thicket, or whatever other manner <note>original: "quem alium modum"</note> it might present.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of the parandrus to a new entry concerning the fox. While the previous page focused on a creature that changes its color for protection, this entry explores a creature that uses deceit and "feigned death" as a hunting tactic.</meta>

# The Fox

<image-desc>A framed medieval manuscript illumination. The scene is split into two parts. At the top, a reddish-orange fox runs across a stylized golden hill with a bird in its mouth. The background is blue with a white lattice pattern. At the bottom, another fox of the same color lies on its back in a bed of red, wavy ground (symbolizing red earth or blood), with its tongue hanging out to feign death. Several birds—magpies and crows—fly towards or land on the "dead" fox. Two stylized trees with green and red foliage frame the sides of the miniature. The entire scene is enclosed in a decorative red, blue, and gold border.</image-desc>

The fox <term>Vulpis; the Latin name for the fox</term> is so called as if it were "shifty-footed" <note>original: "uolupis"; medieval scholars loved to explain animal names through creative etymology. Here, they link "Vulpis" to "volupis," suggesting someone whose feet are "voluble" or never still</note>. 
For it is nimble-footed and never runs in a straight 
path, but in twisting circuits <note>original: "anfractibz"</note>. It is 
also a deceitful and clever animal. 
When it is hungry and finds nothing to eat, it 
rolls itself in red earth <note>original: "rubea terra"</note> so that it appears 
bloodstained, and it casts itself upon the ground. 
It holds its breath so that it does not seem 
to breathe at all. Indeed, the birds, 
seeing it not breathing and appearing 
bloodied, with its tongue sticking 
out, think it is dead and 
descend to perch upon it. 
But the fox then seizes and devours them. 
The devil possesses the same likeness 
as this animal. For to all those living 
according to the flesh, he pretends to be dead <note>Medieval bestiaries were not just science books; they were moral guides. The fox’s trick represents the way the devil tempts people by appearing harmless or "dead" until he can trap their souls</note>.

<summary>This page describes the deceptive nature of the fox, which feigns death by rolling in red earth to trick birds into landing on it. The text concludes by identifying the fox as an allegory for the devil, who uses similar deceit to trap those living a worldly life.</summary>

And the Psalm <note>Psalm 63:9-10 (Psalm 62 in the Latin Vulgate)</note> says: "They shall go into the lower parts of the earth; they shall be delivered into the hands of the sword; they shall be the portions of foxes."

# The Hare

The Hare <term>Lepus</term> is so called as "light-foot" <note>original: "leupes," a medieval etymological play on the Latin "levipes"</note> because it runs swiftly. Because of this, it is also called <note>original: "parsu laos," likely a phonetic corruption of the Greek word for hare, "lagos"</note> in Greek; for it is a swift animal and quite timid. 

The hare signifies people who fear God, who do not place their trust in themselves, but in their Creator. Hence it is read in Solomon <note>Proverbs 30:26</note>: "The hare is a weak people, who make their home in the rocks" <note>In medieval theology, the "rock" was often interpreted as Christ. Therefore, the timid hare hiding in the rocks represented the humble believer seeking refuge in the strength of God</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the fox as a symbol of the devil's deceit and introduces the hare as a symbol of the humble, God-fearing believer.</summary>
<keywords>fox, devil, flesh vs spirit, Romans 8:13, Psalm 63, hare, Lepus, humility, Proverbs 30, Four Hares motif</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on the hare, specifically interpreting its behavior and biblical mentions as a metaphor for the soul seeking Christ. It then transitions into the description of the hedgehog (erinacius) as a symbol of the sinner seeking protection.</meta>

God as their own. Whence it is said in the Psalm <note>Psalm 104:18; in the Latin Vulgate, this is Psalm 103:18</note>: "The rock is a refuge for hares and hedgehogs." But the rock is Christ. For this reason, it is written concerning Moses that he, the "little hare" of the Lord, stood in the cleft of the rock because he placed the hope of his salvation in the passion of the Redeemer. 

# The Hedgehog

The hedgehog <term>Erinacius</term> <note>often referred to in medieval texts as an "urchin"</note> is indeed what they call an *erinium* <note>original: "erinium," likely a variation of the Greek "echinos"</note>, an animal that is entirely timid by nature, yet by a modest instinct it is always armed. In place of the defenses of other wild beasts, very dense and sharp quills fortify it. However, this natural protection does not suffice for it; instead, lest it be caught by some trickery, it always maintains a refuge among the rocks. To this animal is fittingly compared the person who, bristling with their sins and fearing the coming judgments, is known to have Christ the rock as their most firm refuge.

# The Chameleon

The chameleon <term>Cameleon</term> does not have a single color, but is speckled with a diverse variety of spots, much like a leopard. <note>In this passage, the author conflates the small, color-changing lizard with the "camelopard" or giraffe.</note> In grammar, it is declined as follows: "of this chameleon" <note>original: "huius cameleontis"</note>. Its small body changes into the colors it sees through a very easy transformation; while in other animals the physical frame is fixed, in this creature, the body is easily altered by what it encounters. 

It is called the "chameleon-leopard" <note>original: "Cameleon-pardus"</note> because, while it is like a leopard with white spots sprinkled over it, it has a neck like a horse, feet like an ox, and a head like a camel. Ethiopia produces this creature.

Behind it are stylized trees with pink buds and green foliage. A black and white bird is perched on a branch at the top left. The background features gold leaf and a blue patterned rectangular insert.</image-desc>

# The Yale

There is a beast called the Yale <term>Eale</term> <note>a mythical beast often found in medieval heraldry and bestiaries, sometimes associated with the antelope</note> which is the size of a horse, has the tail of an elephant, is black in color, and possesses the jaws of a wild boar. It carries horns that are exceedingly long, which it can move at will to suit its needs <note>the manuscript cuts off at "motu," referring to the animal's ability to swivel its horns independently to fight</note>.

# The Wolf

The word "Wolf" <note>original: *Lupus*</note> is brought into our language from a Greek derivation. For they call wolves *lykos* <note>original: *licos*</note>. Moreover, they are named *lykos* from the word for "bites" <note>original: *a morsibus*</note>, because in the madness of their greed, they slaughter whatever they find. 

Others say they are called wolves <term>lupus</term> as if they were "lion-footed" <note>original: *leopos*</note>, because their strength lies in their feet just as it does with lions. Because of this, whatever they tread upon does not survive. 

The wolf is named for its rapacity <term>rapacitas</term>. From this, we also call prostitutes "she-wolves" <note>original: *lupas*</note>, because they [devour] the goods of their lovers... <note>This is a common medieval etymological link between the predatory nature of the wolf and the perceived social danger of "loose" women; the Latin word *lupanar* (brothel) shares this root.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the description of the Yale's rotating horns and introduces the wolf, focusing on the Greek and Latin etymologies of its name and its predatory nature.</summary>
<keywords>Yale, wolf, lycos, lion-footed, etymology, Isidore of Seville, bestiary, predator</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for the Wolf (Lupus), picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion regarding the etymology of the word and the predatory nature of the beast.</meta>

...devastate. It is a rapacious beast, hungering for blood. Its strength lies in its chest and mouth, but in its loins <note>original: *renibus*; literally "kidneys," though often used to denote the source of physical vigor or reproductive power</note> there is very little. It is never able to bend its neck backward. It is said sometimes to live on prey, sometimes on earth, and occasionally even on the wind. Furthermore, the she-wolf does not give birth to cubs in any month other than May, when the thunder rolls. 

Its cunning is so great that it does not take prey near its own den <note>original: *munimia*; referring to its fortified or protected lair</note>, but instead hunts far away for its cubs. If it must seek prey by night, it approaches the sheepfold like a tame dog; and so that the dogs might not catch the scent of its breath and the shepherds wake up, it walks against the wind. And if a branch or any object it touches should snap under its foot, it punishes that very foot with a sharp bite <note>This behavioral detail highlights the wolf's legendary discipline and stealth</note>. 

Its eyes shine in the night like lamps. Its nature is such that if it sees a man first, it takes away his voice and looks down upon him as a victor who has stolen his speech. <note>This refers to an ancient and medieval superstition that a human would be struck dumb if a wolf caught their gaze before they noticed the animal.</note> Likewise, if the wolf senses that it has been spotted first, it loses its ferocity and is unable to run. Solinus <note>Gaius Julius Solinus, a 3rd-century Roman scholar whose works on the "wonders of the world" were heavily cited in medieval natural history.</note>, who writes many things concerning the nature of things, reports that there is a very small tuft of hair on this animal's tail which acts as a love charm; the wolf tears it out with its teeth if it fears being captured, for the charm has no power unless it is removed while the wolf is still alive. 

Wolves bear the image of the Devil, who always envies the human race and constantly circles the folds of the Church of the faithful, so that he might slaughter and prey upon their souls. As for the fact that it gives birth [during thunder]...

by his own wit he will be free, just as he was in the beginning. 
**S**piritually, however, this must be understood and spoken of allegorically <note>An "allegory" is a story or image where the characters and events symbolize deeper moral or spiritual meanings.</note> toward a higher sense.

This is so that Cerberus <note>The three-headed dog from Greek mythology who guards the underworld; here used as a symbol for the devil or the mouth of Hell.</note>, rejoicing in our destruction, does not swallow us up in our ignorance. **L**olves do not mate for more than twelve days in the whole year. They endure hunger for a long time, and after long...

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the wolf. ...they devour much after their long fasts. Ethiopia sends forth **wolves with maned necks** <term>maned necks</term> <note>original: *ceruice iubatos*; This likely refers to the Ethiopian wolf or perhaps a creature like the hyena, which medieval naturalists often grouped with wolves.</note>, and they are so varied in pattern that it is said no color is lacking from them. It is characteristic of these Ethiopian wolves that they leap very high when jumping, so that they do not fix themselves to the ground by running so much as they do by their movement through the air. However, they never attack humans. In the **winter** <note>original: *bruma*; the winter solstice or the coldest part of the year.</note> they are shaggy, but in the **summer** they are bare. The Ethiopians call them *theas*.

Therefore, the **wolf** is rarely found to have a positive meaning; it more often has a contrary significance. For it signifies either the **devil**, as it is written in the **Gospel**:
> The wolf snatches and scatters the sheep. <note>original: *Lupus rapit & dispergit oues*; a reference to John 10:12.</note>

Or it signifies **heretics** <note>In this context, a "heretic" is someone who spreads false or divisive religious teachings.</note> or deceitful men, about whom the **Lord** says:
> Beware of false prophets who come to you in sheep’s clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves. <note>original: *Attendite a falsis prophetis qui ueniunt ad uos in uestimentis ouium intrinsecus autem sunt lupi rapaces*; a reference to Matthew 7:15.</note>

And again:
> He sees the wolf coming and leaves the sheep and flees. <note>original: *Videt lupum uenientē & dimittit oues & fugit*; a continuation of the passage from John 10:12 regarding the "hired hand" who abandons the flock.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the wolf to the entry on the dog. While the previous page focused on the predatory and deceitful nature of wolves (linked to the devil and heretics), this text introduces the dog as the wolf's noble counterpart, emphasizing loyalty, intelligence, and domesticity.</meta>

The Latin name for **dog** <term>dog</term> <note>original: *Canis*</note> seems to have a Greek etymology. For in Greek it is called *canos* <note>original: *canos*; a medieval Latin reference to the Greek word *kyōn* (κύων).</note>. However, some think they are named for the **ringing melody** <note>original: *canore*</note> of their barking, because it makes a sound, which is why it is also said to **sing** <note>original: *canere*; the author is making a linguistic connection between the word for dog (*canis*) and the word for singing (*canere*).</note>.

Moreover, nothing is more **sagacious** <term>sagacious</term> <note>Meaning keen-witted or possessing sharp senses.</note> than dogs, for they have more perception than other animals. For they alone recognize their own names and love their masters. There are many types of dogs: some track the wild beasts of the forests in order to capture them; others keep watch and guard flocks of sheep from the attacks of wolves; still others, as guardians of the home, protect the property of their masters so that it is not snatched by thieves in the night. 

They will voluntarily expose themselves to death for their masters; they run with their master toward prey, and they even guard their master's body when he is dead and will not leave him. Finally, it is their nature that they cannot exist apart from humans.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the dog's loyalty, moving from general descriptions of their nature to specific historical and legendary examples of their devotion to their masters, even unto death. This text draws heavily from classical sources, particularly Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*.</meta>

It is written that dogs love their masters. For instance, when King **Garamantes** <note>original: *Garamantem*; a legendary king of the Garamantes, a powerful ancient tribe of the Sahara.</note> was captured by his enemies and held in bondage, two hundred dogs formed a column and led him back from exile through the middle of the enemy battle lines, fighting against all those who resisted them. When **Jason of Lycia** <note>original: *Iasone laconi*; a figure from ancient history whose dog showed legendary devotion.</note> was killed, his dog refused all food and died of starvation. 

The dog of King **Lysimachus** <note>original: *Lisimachi*; one of the generals and successors of Alexander the Great.</note> threw himself into the flames when his master’s funeral pyre was lit and was consumed by the fire alongside him. In the consulship of **Appius Junius** and **Publius Silius** <note>original: *Appio iunio. pictumo*; the OCR 'pictumo' refers to Publius Silius Nerva, who served as consul in 28 AD. This story refers to the execution of Titius Sabinus.</note>, when a man had been condemned, his dog could not be driven away and followed him into prison, and shortly thereafter followed him for a time with piteous howling. And when, out of the pity of the Roman people, <note>The sentence breaks off here, mid-thought. The full story usually describes the dog bringing food to the executed master's mouth and jumping into the Tiber river to try and keep the body afloat.</note>

<summary>This page lists historical anecdotes demonstrating the extreme loyalty of dogs, including stories of dogs rescuing a king, dying of grief, and following their masters into execution and fire.</summary>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the dog of Titius Sabinus, whose execution in 28 AD was a famous example of animal devotion in Roman history, as recorded by Pliny the Elder. It then transitions into a discussion of the dog's innate "logic" during a hunt.</meta>

...pity and food were provided. He brought food to the mouth of the deceased master. When the corpse was then thrown into the nearby **Tiber** <note>original: *tyberim*; the main river in Rome. This refers to the dog of Titius Sabinus, which reportedly tried to keep its master's body from sinking after he was executed and cast into the river.</note>, the dog, swimming alongside, tried to keep it afloat. 

Indeed, when a dog finds the track of a hare or a deer and comes to a fork in the path, or a certain crossroads of ways that is split into many parts, he considers the start of each individual path and silently debates within himself—as if his shrewdness in gathering the scent were emitting a **syllogistic voice** <note>original: *sillogisticam uocē*; a reference to a classical trope (attributed to the philosopher Chrysippus) arguing that dogs possess a form of logic. The dog "reasons" through a process of elimination.</note>. 

"He has either turned in this direction," he says, "or that one; or surely he has taken himself into this winding bend." But since he has entered neither this one nor that one, it remains, therefore, that he has taken himself into this [remaining] part. And thus, having rejected the falsehood, he finds the truth.

<summary>This page concludes the story of a loyal dog attempting to save its master's body from the Tiber and describes the "logic" of hunting dogs, who use a process of elimination to track prey.</summary>
<keywords>dog, Tiber, Titius Sabinus, loyalty, hunting, syllogism, animal intelligence, tracking</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general "logic" of hunting dogs to a specific narrative illustrating the loyalty and moral agency of dogs. It introduces a famous anecdote from the bestiary tradition regarding a murder in Antioch, where a dog serves as the primary witness against a criminal.</meta>

Often, the evidence of a murder has also been brought to light by dogs, who provide the signs needed to convict the guilty; thus, their **mute testimony** <note>original: *muto eorum testimonio*; a recurring theme in medieval literature where animals, though unable to speak, act as instruments of divine justice.</note> is frequently believed. 

They say that in a remote part of the city of **Antioch** <note>original: *Antiochie*; a major Hellenistic and Roman city, now in modern-day Turkey.</note>, a certain man was killed at twilight. He had his dog with him at the time. A certain soldier, driven by a desire for plunder, had been the perpetrator of the murder. Concealed by the murky shadows of the early morning, the killer had slipped away to another part of the city. 

The corpse lay there unburied, and a large crowd of onlookers gathered. The dog stood by, mourning his master’s misfortune with a pitiful whimpering. By chance, the killer himself approached that circle of onlookers. Using the cunning of the human mind—and assuming an air of authority to better assert his "innocence"—he drew near the corpse as if he were full of pity. 

Then the dog, setting aside his mournful cries for a moment, took up the weapons of vengeance—

<summary>This page recounts a story from Antioch where a dog acts as a "mute witness" to his master's murder, mourning over the body until the killer—a soldier—boldly joins the crowd of onlookers.</summary>
<keywords>dog, Antioch, murder, loyalty, justice, witness, soldier, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of the dog from Antioch and transitions into the natural properties and moral allegories of dogs, common in medieval bestiaries. It connects the physical traits of the dog—such as its healing tongue and its behavior in fables—to the roles of preachers and priests.</meta>

...[the dog] seized him and held him fast. Then, whimpering a kind of pitiful lament as a final plea <note>original: *epilogo quodam miserabile carmen*; the text poetically describes the dog's howling as a closing argument in a court case.</note>, he moved everyone present to tears. This gave credit to the evidence, for out of the many people in the crowd, the dog held onto this man alone and would not let him go. Finally, the man became distraught because he could not nullify such manifest evidence of the deed. He could offer no objection of hatred, enmity, envy, or the influence of some bribe. He was unable to refute a crime that could not be washed away. And so, finding it difficult to maintain his defense, he confessed to the act of vengeance <note>original: *ultionem professus est*; here implying he admitted to the killing, perhaps claiming it was an act of retribution, rather than continuing to feign innocence.</note> as best he could. 

The tongue of a dog heals a wound when it licks it. His eyesight is said to be quite moderate. Furthermore, the tongue of a puppy is often used for the health of the internal organs <note>A common medieval belief held that the saliva of dogs, particularly young ones, had medicinal properties for both external wounds and internal ailments.</note>. It is his nature to return to his own vomit and eat it again <note>A reference to Proverbs 26:11 and 2 Peter 2:22, often used in a moral sense to describe a sinner returning to their vices.</note>. 

And when he swims across a river, holding meat or something similar in his mouth, and sees his reflection <note>original: *umbram*; literally "shadow," but referring to the reflection in the water, as in the famous Aesop's fable.</note>, he opens his mouth. While he hopes to seize the other piece of meat, he loses the very thing he was holding. 

In certain respects, dogs represent the figure of **preachers** <term>predicatores; those who publicly proclaim the word of God</term>. By always advising and practicing what is right, they drive away the snares of the devil, lest he carry away the treasure of God—that is, the souls of Christians—by seizing them for himself. 

The tongue of the dog heals when it licks a wound because the wounds of sins, when they are laid bare in **confession**, are cleansed by the correction of **priests**. A dog’s tongue also heals the "intestines" of a man, because preachers often cleanse the secrets of the heart.

<summary>The narrative of the loyal dog of Antioch ends with the murderer's confession, followed by a series of allegories where the dog’s healing tongue represents a priest’s role in confession and its mistakes serve as moral warnings.</summary>
<keywords>loyalty, confession, preachers, bestiary, Aesop's fables, healing, justice, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegories of the dog's nature. It transitions from physical descriptions and fables (like Aesop's dog and its reflection) to the spiritual duties of preachers and the steps of repentance for a sinner.</meta>

...in the work and speech of a teacher <note>original: *opre & sermone doctoris*</note>. A dog’s diet is said to be very meager; likewise, he who leads others must keep watch over the study of wisdom and must avoid excess <note>original: *crapulam*; specifically referring to the sickness or "hangover" caused by over-drinking or gluttony</note> in every way. For in the "fullness of bread," Sodom perished <note>A reference to Ezekiel 16:49, suggesting that idleness and over-eating lead to moral decay.</note>. Finally, there is no entry point through which the enemy <note>the Devil</note> possesses a person as quickly as through a greedy throat. 

That a dog returns to its vomit signifies those who, after making a formal confession, carelessly return to the crimes they have committed. 

That the dog leaves its meat in the river because of the reflection <note>original: *umbram*; literally "shadow"</note> it desired signifies foolish people who, because of their ambition for something unknown, often abandon what is rightfully their own. Thus it happens that while they are unable to obtain what they desire, they discover too late they have lost in vain that which they left behind. 

Lyciscae <note>original: *Licaca*; a term for a wolf-dog hybrid</note> are dogs so called because they are born from wolves and dogs; they are mixed together by chance. It is also common for female dogs to be tied up in the woods at night to be approached by tigers <note>In medieval lore, tigers were often depicted as fierce beasts that could interbreed with dogs to create superior hunters.</note>. From this union, the fiercest dogs are born, so strong that they can strike down lions in their grip. 

Whenever a sinner wishes to please his Creator, it is necessary and useful for him to seek out three spiritual guides <note>original: *conductores*</note>. These three spiritual messengers <note>original: *legatos*</note>, along with three spiritual gifts, should lead him to reconciliation with his Maker. ...true confession. The third is true penance. Their guides <note>original: *conductores*</note> are the love of God, good will, and right work. The spiritual gifts are the purity of body and soul, pure prayer, and perseverance in good work. These legates and guides, together with spiritual houses, thus present themselves before the Trinity. 

Before God the Father, weeping <note>original: *ploratus*; referring to the tears of a contrite heart</note> presents itself with the love of God, bringing with it purity of body and soul. Before the Holy Spirit, true penance presents itself with right work, carrying with it the perseverance of good work. 

Just as potions are necessary for a weakened body to heal its infirmities, so too for a sinful soul a potion is necessary by which its spiritual corruption may be healed. This potion of the soul, however, consists of four ingredients <note>original: *speciebz*; literally "species," used here in the medical sense of components in a compound medicine</note>: that is, the weeping of the heart, true confession, true penance, and right action. This medicine is so suitable for healing the soul's infirmities that as soon as the soul is bathed <note>original: *tingitur*; can also mean "dyed" or "steeped"</note> by it, it is immediately healed of its infirmities. 

But once healed, if the soul were left without an honorable garment, how could it be presented in the heavenly court <note>original: *celesti curia*; imagining heaven as a royal court requiring specific etiquette and dress</note> before its Maker, where it ought to be presented? It is necessary, therefore, that the man who has undertaken to rule and clothe the soul should dress it so honorably and appropriately that he may be able to present it praiseworthily before the angels in heaven.

If man had yielded to these, he would not have lost the heavenly kingdom; but because he did not yield to them, he lost his inheritance.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sheep (ovis) from the previous page, specifically focusing on its name, its behavior in winter, and its various allegorical meanings in Scripture, ranging from Christ and the faithful to the reprobate.</meta>

...some call them "two-toothed" <note>original: *bidentes*; a common medieval etymology derived from having two prominent teeth among eight</note> because among their eight teeth they have two that are taller, which the pagans <note>original: *gentiles*</note> especially used to offer in sacrifice. The sheep, at the approach of winter, is insatiable for food. It ravenously seizes the grass because it senses the coming bitterness of winter, so that it might sate itself on the fodder of the grass before all vegetation fails with the arrival of the frost.

Now, as we have said, the sheep signify the innocent and simple people among the Christian folk <note>original: *xpiano* (christiano); the "x" is the Greek letter 'Chi,' a standard abbreviation for Christ</note>. Furthermore, the sheep also sometimes demonstrates the gentleness and patience of the Lord <note>original: *dñi* (domini)</note> himself in a symbolic way, as in that passage from Isaiah <note>original: *ysaie*</note> concerning the innocent death of the Savior <note>original: *saluatoris*</note>: "He shall be led like a sheep to the slaughter," it says, "and he shall not open his mouth." 

The sheep are the faithful people in the Gospel <note>original: *euuangelio*</note>: "My sheep will hear my voice." And in the Psalm <note>original: *psalmo*</note>: "You have put all things under his feet, both sheep and oxen." 

The two sheep signify the peoples of the nations whom a man—that is, Christ <note>original: *xpc* (christus)</note>—nourishes. Concerning him, it is read in the prophet <note>original: *ppha* (propheta)</note>: "In those days a man shall nourish a young cow and two sheep, and from the abundance of milk he shall eat butter." 

Likewise, the sheep are the reprobate <note>those who are rejected or condemned</note> in the Psalm: "Like sheep they are set in hell; death shall feed upon them"—that is, the devil <note>original: *diabolus*</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of sheep to the specific characteristics and symbolism of the ram (Aries), following the typical structure of a medieval bestiary where animal traits are linked to Christian moral lessons.</meta>

**A**ries <note>the Ram</note> is so named either from "strength" <note>original: *viribus*; Isidore of Seville, a major source for bestiaries, frequently used this etymology</note> because it is stronger than the other sheep, or because it is a "vir"—that is, a male—or perhaps because it has a worm <note>original: *vermem*; it was a common medieval belief that rams had worms in their heads that caused their aggressive behavior</note> in its head. Excited by the itching of these worms, they butt one another and strike with great force when they fight. Thus, the Ram is also named after "Ares" <note>original: *areos*; referring to the Greek god of war, Ares, denoting the animal's combative nature</note>, which means "bitter" <note>original: *amare*; likely a medieval etymological confusion between the name of the god and the Latin word for bitterness</note>, which is why among us the males in the flocks are called "rams" <note>original: *mares*</note>; or else it is named so because this animal was the first male <note>original: *maris*</note> to be sacrificed by the pagans <note>original: *gentilibus*</note>. From this comes the saying: "The ram is slaughtered at the altar <note>original: *aram*; a pun on the similarity between *aries* and *ara*</note>."

Now, the Rams signify the apostles or the leaders of the churches. For this reason, it is read in Isaiah <note>original: *ysaia*</note>: "The rams of Nabaioth shall minister to you," and in the Psalter <note>original: *psalterio*</note>: "Bring to the Lord the offspring of rams." These, like leaders of the flocks, have led the Christian people <note>original: *populum christianum*</note> into the folds of the Lord <note>original: *domini*</note>. Furthermore, by "offering the offspring of rams," the text refers to those whom the apostles have fathered through righteous preaching, rather than those who are proven to be outsiders through wicked dogma.

<summary>This page describes the etymology and physical nature of the ram, emphasizing its strength and sacrificial history, before transitioning into an allegory where rams represent the Apostles leading their "flocks" of faithful Christians.</summary>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the ram, shifting from its physical strength to its symbolic role as a leader of the faith, before introducing the lamb. It maintains the theme of etymological wordplay common in medieval bestiaries.</meta>

Rams, moreover, are rightly compared to the **apostles** <note>original: *aplis*, an abbreviation for *apostolis*</note>, because—as we have said—these animals are exceptionally strong in their foreheads and always knock down any obstacles by butting against them. This is exactly what the apostles did through their preaching: they shattered various superstitions and the most firmly established idols with the "forehead," so to speak, of the heavenly word. For the ram <term>Aries</term> is so named because it "rushes from the front" <note>original: *a fronte ruens*; a medieval etymological explanation linking the name *aries* to its behavior of rushing forward</note>.

In a negative sense, rams represent the leaders of the wicked. It is concerning these that we read in **Ezekiel**: "with goats, lambs, and rams" <note>original: *cum edis & agnis & arietibus*; a reference to Ezekiel 39:18, where the different animals represent different classes or ranks of people</note>.

The **Lamb**, which the Greeks call *Arnon* <note>original: *Arnon*; likely a variation of the Greek *arnos*</note> because it is "holy" <note>original: *agno*; likely referring to the Greek *agnos*, meaning pure or holy, which sounds like the Latin *agnus*</note> and pious. The Latins, however, believe it holds this name because, more than all other living creatures, it **recognizes** <note>original: *agnoscat*; the author links the word *agnus* (lamb) to *agnoscere* (to recognize)</note> its mother; so much so that even if it has wandered into a massive flock, it immediately recognizes the voice of its parent by her bleating.

Lambs are mentioned in the Gospel: "Feed my lambs" <note>John 21:15</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the symbolism of the Lamb to that of the Kid (young goat), following the standard structure of a medieval bestiary where animals are grouped by their biblical and moral associations. It continues the use of scriptural citations to provide a multi-layered allegorical interpretation of the animal.</meta>

**Kids** <term>Haedi</term> are named from "eating" <note>The author follows Isidore of Seville’s etymology, linking the Latin *haedus* (kid) to *edendo* (eating)</note>. For when they are very small, they are quite fatty and possess a pleasant flavor; for this reason, the words for "food" <note>original: *ede*</note> and "eatable" <note>original: *edulium*</note> are derived from the same root.

According to allegory, these animals signify **sinners**, who are to be stationed at the left hand of the Judge at the end of time, just as the just—signified by the sheep—are placed at the right. Whence Truth itself <note>A common medieval title for Christ</note> says in the Gospel: "He shall set the sheep on his right hand, but the goats on the left" <note>original: *Statuet quidem oues a dextris. hedos autem a sinistris*; Matthew 25:33</note>.

Sometimes, however, even the **just** are described by this name on account of their confession of sins, as in the book of **Jeremiah**: "Depart," he says, "from the midst of Babylon; go forth out of the land of the Chaldeans, and be like goats before the flocks" <note>original: *Recedite... de medio babilonis... & estote quasi edi ante greges*; Jeremiah 50:8. In this context, "goats" refers to leaders or those who are first to repent</note>.

The **kid** can also represent **Christ** because of the "likeness of sinful flesh" <note>A reference to Romans 8:3, suggesting that though Christ was without sin, he took on a human body capable of suffering</note>, as it says in **Deuteronomy**: "You shall not boil a kid in its mother’s milk" <note>original: *non coques edum in lacte matris*; Deuteronomy 14:21</note>.

Finally, the kid signifies the **Antichrist**, as in that passage in the Gospel: "You never gave me a young goat so that I might feast with my friends" <note>original: *Nunquam dedisti mihi edum ut cum amicis meis epularer*; Luke 15:29. This is the complaint of the elder brother in the Parable of the Prodigal Son</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of goats, moving from the "kids" mentioned on the previous page to the adult male "he-goat." It maintains the tradition of drawing moral lessons from the animal's physical traits and its role in biblical sacrifice, specifically contrasting the "goat" (sinner) with the "bull" (the righteous or the repentant).</meta>

# The He-Goat

The **He-goat** <term>Hircus</term> is a lustful and impudent animal, always burning for mating. Its eyes look sideways because of its lust; and it is from this characteristic that it takes its name. For the "hirqui" <note>original: *hirci* or *hirqui*. The author is using an etymological play common in medieval texts, linking the name of the goat to the corners of the eyes, which were thought to twitch or look askance during arousal</note> are the corners of the eyes. 

The nature of this animal is so exceedingly hot that its blood alone can dissolve the **diamond** <note>original: *adamantem lapidem*. Medieval bestiaries claimed that the diamond, the hardest of stones, could only be softened by the warm blood of a he-goat</note>, a stone which neither fire nor iron is strong enough to master. 

In the Old Law, the he-goat was offered as a sacrifice for sins, representing the person of **sinners**. By the shedding of its blood—which signifies the tears of repentance—the hardness of sins is dissolved. Therefore, the Church says to the Lord in the **Psalter**: 
> "I will offer to you bulls with goats" <note>original: *offeram tibi boves cum hircis*; Psalm 66:15</note>. 

Goats represent sinners or the **Gentiles** <note>In this context, non-Christians or those yet to be converted</note>. As written in the book of **Daniel**: 
> "Behold, a male goat came from the north" <note>original: *Ecce autem hircus caprarum veniebat ab aquilone*. Though the biblical Daniel 8:5 usually specifies the west, the manuscript indicates the north, perhaps associating it with the direction of evil or coldness in medieval symbolism</note>. 

The "two goats" <note>A reference to the ritual of the Day of Atonement in Leviticus 16, involving one sacrificed goat and one scapegoat</note> represent both peoples—namely the Jews and the Gentiles—who both arise from the root of sin, as mentioned in **Leviticus**. Others, however, have interpreted these two goats as representing **Christ** and **Barabbas**. 

Goats are understood to be those who, devoted to diabolical wickedness, are clothed in "shaggy" vices. For the **He-goat** is so named because it is "shaggy" <note>original: *hirrutus*, related to *hirsutus* or "hairy"</note>. Yet the Church offers them along with the bulls when, having been converted, they enjoy the presence of Christ the Lord. 

For example, that thief who hung on the cross beside Jesus was a "goat" <note>Symbolizing his life as a sinner</note>; but he soon became a "bull" <note>Symbolizing a righteous soul or a holy sacrifice</note> when he looked toward the confession of truth and said: 
> "Lord, remember me when you come into your kingdom" <note>Luke 23:42</note>.

<summary>This page describes the he-goat as a symbol of lust and sin, whose "hot" blood can uniquely dissolve the hard diamond of sin, ultimately illustrating how a sinner (the thief on the cross) can be transformed into a righteous offering through confession.</summary>
<keywords>he-goat, diamond, sacrifice, repentance, Daniel, Leviticus, thief on the cross, etymology, blood</keywords>
