<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of goats to the sow, boar, and pig. It continues the bestiary tradition of using etymological wordplay and biblical dietary laws to provide moral instruction, shifting focus from the lust of the goat to the filth and lack of spiritual "rumination" found in the pig.</meta>

# The Sow

The **Sow** <term>Sus</term> is named because she "upturns" <note>original: *subigat*. The text suggests an etymological link between the name *sus* and the verb *subigo*, meaning to turn up or subdue the earth</note> the pastures; that is, she searches for food in the overturned earth. The **Boar** <term>Verres</term> is so called because he possesses great strength <note>original: *vires*. Another medieval etymological association linking the name of the male pig to his power</note>. The **Pig** <term>Porcus</term> is named as if he were "filthy" <note>original: *spurcus*. This reflects a common linguistic theory in the Middle Ages that the Latin word for pig was derived from the word for uncleanness</note>; for he gorges himself on mire, plunges into the mud, and smears himself with slime. As the poet **Horace** <note>A famous Roman lyric poet from the 1st century BC</note> says: "And the sow is a friend to the mud" <note>original: *et amica luto sus*; Horace, *Epistles* 1.2.26</note>. 

From this animal, we also derive the names for **filthiness** and for those who are **spurious** <note>original: *spurcitiam* and *spurios*. In this context, "spurious" refers to those of low or illegitimate birth, equated here with the baseness of the pig</note>. We call the hairs of pigs "bristles" <term>setae</term>, and the word for bristle is said to come from "sow." From these bristles, **shoemakers** <term>sutores</term> also take their name, because they "sew" <note>original: *suant*</note>—that is, they stitch together—hides using bristles.

Sows signify **sinners** and the unclean, or even **heretics**. Concerning these, it is commanded in the **Law** <note>The dietary laws found in the biblical books of Leviticus and Deuteronomy</note> that because they have a split hoof but do not "chew the cud" <term>ruminant</term>, their flesh must not be eaten by the faithful. These people may accept both the **Old Testament** and the **Gospel**, but because they do not "ruminate" on spiritual food <note>In medieval theology, "chewing the cud" was a metaphor for meditating deeply on scripture to extract its hidden, spiritual meaning</note>, they are unclean. Likewise, sows signify **negligent penitents** who return to those very sins for which they once wept... <note>This sentence is incomplete, breaking off at *sig-* (signify), likely continuing with the biblical proverb of the washed sow returning to the wallow</note>

<summary>This page describes the sow, boar, and pig, using Latin etymologies to link them to filth and physical labor like shoemaking. It concludes with a moral allegory, comparing the pig’s failure to "chew the cud" to people who read scripture without spiritual meditation.</summary>

<keywords>sow, boar, pig, Horace, shoemakers, dietary laws, rumination, heretics, spiritual food, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing description of the sow and the pig, focusing on their behavior as metaphors for backsliding sinners and the insincerity of confession without true repentance. It concludes the section on the pig with various scriptural citations from the Gospels and the Psalms.</meta>

...signify. As **Peter** says in his letter: "The dog returns to his vomit, and the sow, after washing herself, returns to wallowing in the mire" <note>original: *Canis reversus ad vomitum suum. & sus lota in volutabro luti*; 2 Peter 2:22</note>. Therefore, when a dog vomits, he certainly throws up the food that was weighing down his chest. But when he returns to the vomit, he is burdened again by that from which he had been relieved. 

So it is with those who do not lament the sins they have committed: they certainly cast out the wickedness of the mind—with which they were badly satiated and which weighed them down inside—by **confessing** <term>confitendo</term> it; however, when they repeat those sins after confession, they take them up again. 

Truly the **sow** <term>sus</term>, having washed in the wallow of mud, is made filthier while she is being washed. And he who laments a committed sin, yet does not abandon it, subjects himself to a penalty of even heavier guilt. This is because he both despises the very pardon which he was able to obtain by weeping, and involves himself, as it were, in muddy water. Because while he withdraws the purity of life by his tears, he makes those very tears filthy before the eyes of God. 

Likewise, by the "pigs" are meant **unclean** and **luxurious** men in the **Gospel** <term>evangelio</term>: "If you cast us out, send us into the pigs" <note>original: *Si eicias nos mitte in porcos*; Matthew 8:31, referring to the demons known as Legion</note>. Likewise there: "Do not throw your pearls before swine" <note>original: *Ne mittatis margaritas vestras ante porcos*; Matthew 7:6</note>. Pigs appear in the form of the unclean elsewhere in the Gospel: "And he sent him to his farm to feed pigs" <note>original: *et misit illum in villam suam ut pasceret porcos*; Luke 15:15, from the Parable of the Prodigal Son</note>. 

The **pig** <term>porcus</term> likewise signifies the unclean and sinners, of whom it is written in the **Psalm**: "Their belly is filled with your hidden things. They are sated with **swine-flesh** <note>original: *porcina*. While many versions of Psalm 17:14 (Vulgate 16:14) read *filiis* (children), some medieval versions and bestiaries used *porcina* to emphasize the uncleanness of the wicked</note>, and they left what remained for their little ones."

<summary>This page uses the biblical metaphors of the dog returning to its vomit and the sow to the mud to illustrate the danger of insincere confession. It concludes by linking pigs to various New Testament parables and a Psalm, categorizing them as symbols of luxury and uncleanness.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the moralizing discourse on the pig (sus) and transitions into the description of the wild boar (aper). This implies that wisdom or beauty is wasted on those with base, "swinish" natures.</note>. <meta>This page continues the transition from the domestic pig to the wild boar (aper). It shifts from dietary laws to a historical and spiritual allegory based on Psalm 80, interpreting the "boar from the wood" as the Roman leaders who destroyed Jerusalem.</meta>

[The boar] is a wild animal and was previously called "wild" <note>original: *agreste*</note> by an improper use of the term. Others, however, say that the **boar** <term>aper</term> is named for its wildness <note>The author follows Isidore of Seville’s etymology, linking *aper* (boar) to *asperitas* (roughness/harshness)</note>, because it dwells in rough <note>original: *aspris*</note> places. Furthermore, the boar signifies the ferocity of the princes of this world; wherefore it is written in the **Psalm** <term>Psalmus</term> concerning the vineyard of the Lord: "The boar from the wood has uprooted it, and a solitary wild beast has grazed upon it" <note>original: *Exterminauit eam aper de silua: & singularis ferus depast' est eam*; Psalm 80:13. In medieval exegesis, the "vineyard" represents the people of Israel or the Church.</note>.

"He has uprooted" <note>original: *Exterminauit*; literally "to drive beyond the boundaries" (ex terminos)</note>: he dispersed them everywhere outside their borders and their own homeland, which happened to the nation of the **Jews** <term>iudei</term>. Perhaps you may understand the boar to be **Vespasian** <term>Vespasianus</term> <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 69–79 AD) who began the suppression of the Jewish Revolt.</note>, who drove them out and enslaved them. By this name, it is signified that the Jews were contradictory <note>original: *contrarium*; likely implying they were opposed to Christ</note>, for they were seen to hold this animal as unclean among the others. "From the wood" <note>original: *De silua*</note> refers to the **Gentiles** <note>Non-Jewish peoples</note>, who are rightly compared to squalid woods because they were not yet part of the fruitful offspring <note>i.e., they had not yet been "cultivated" by Christian faith</note>. 

Moreover, by "the solitary wild beast," he records his son **Titus** <term>Titus</term> <note>Vespasian's son and successor, who completed the Siege of Jerusalem in 70 AD.</note>, who crushed the remnants of the war with such great destruction, consuming the people and the city like the fodder of grasses with a terrible grazing. For it was necessary for this to happen to the **vineyard** <term>uinea</term> whose wall was seen to have been cast down. 

Spiritually, however, the boar can be understood as the **devil** <term>diabolus</term> because of his ferocity and excessive strength. He says "from the wood" because his thoughts are wild and always exist in a tangled state <note>original: *inuiceque*; literally "mutually" or "in turn," suggesting thoughts as dense and prickly as a thicket</note>.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the wild boar (aper), identifying it with the Roman Emperors Vespasian and Titus in their destruction of Jerusalem, as well as with the devil.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the wild boar (aper) to the bull (taurus) and bullock (iuvencus). While the previous page focused on the boar as a symbol of Roman persecution and spiritual wildness, this page shifts to domestic and exotic cattle, exploring their etymologies and their dual roles as symbols of Christ and worldly pride.</meta>

**Iuvencus** <term>bullock</term> <note>A young bull, typically under two years old, beginning its training for labor.</note> is so named because it begins to **help** <note>original: *iuvare*; the author uses a common medieval etymology linking *iuvencus* (bullock) to *iuvare* (to help).</note> with human needs in tilling the soil. Or [it is so called] because among the **pagans** <note>original: *gentiles*; referring to the pre-Christian Romans and Greeks.</note> a bullock was always and everywhere sacrificed to **Jupiter** <note>original: *Iovi* (Jove), the king of the Roman gods.</note>, and never a full-grown bull. For among sacrificial victims, age was also taken into strict consideration.

**Bull** <term>taurus</term> is a Greek name, as is **ox** <note>original: *bos*; derived from the Greek *boūs*.</note>. In **India**, bulls have a tawny color and exceptional swiftness <note>original: *uolucris precipua*; literally "chiefly winged/flying," a common medieval description for the speed of the legendary Indian bull.</note>. Their hair is turned in the opposite direction [of other animals]. Their mouth gapes wide enough to take in whatever it catches. These bulls can also move their horns with whatever flexibility they desire. Because of the hardness of their hide, they repel every weapon; they are so fortified by their own ferocity that, if captured, they give up their lives out of pure rage.

The bull signifies **Christ**. As it says in **Genesis**: "And in their fury they hamstrung the bull" <note>original: *& furore suo subnervaverunt taurum*; Genesis 49:6. In this context, the "hamstringing" is interpreted as the suffering and immobilization of Christ during the Passion.</note>. Likewise, bulls represent the powers of this world, who toss the humble common people about with the horn of pride <note>The "horn" is a standard biblical metaphor for strength or arrogance.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the bullock and the bull, providing etymological origins and describing a legendary, fierce variety of Indian bull. It concludes with an allegorical interpretation of the bull as both a figure of the suffering Christ and a symbol of arrogant worldly rulers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the bull (taurus), moving from its identification with Christ to its broader symbolic meanings in Scripture. It concludes the section on the bull and begins the entry for the ox (bos), focusing on its etymology and its remarkably social, loyal nature.</meta>

...of whom it is said in **Jeremiah**: "You have bellowed like bulls" <note>original: *mugistis ut tauri*; Jeremiah 50:11. In this context, it refers to the pride of Babylon.</note> and elsewhere, "and bulls with the powerful" <note>original: *& tauri cum potentibz*; Isaiah 34:7.</note>. For **bulls** <term>tauri</term> have a significance in both a good and a bad sense. 

In the **good sense**, it is as in that passage from the **Gospel**: "Behold, I have prepared my banquet; my bulls and fatted calves are slaughtered, and everything is ready" <note>original: *Ecce prandium meum paravi. tauri mei & altilia occisa sunt*; Matthew 22:4. In medieval commentary, these bulls often represent the Apostles or the Patriarchs of the Old Testament.</note>. 

In the **bad sense**, it is as in the **Psalm**: "Fat bulls have surrounded me" <note>original: *Tauri pingues obsederunt me*; Psalm 22:12. This verse was traditionally interpreted as the powerful leaders of the Jews who conspired to crucify Christ.</note>.

<image-desc>A square illumination framed by blue and red borders. It depicts a reddish-brown bull or ox standing on uneven brown earth against a dark blue background decorated with a white grid and cross pattern. The Greeks call the **ox** <term>bos</term> "boetes." The Latins call it *bion* <note>The author uses a traditional medieval etymology linking the Latin *bos* to the Greek *boūs*.</note> because it **treads the earth** <note>original: *terram terat*; the author suggests the word *bos* comes from the act of treading or wearing down the soil during labor.</note>, as if it were a "treader of the earth" <term>terrionem</term>. 

Among oxen, there is an extraordinary **loyalty** <note>original: *pietas*; this implies not just "pity" but a sense of duty and familial love.</note> toward their companions. For one will seek out the other with whom it is accustomed to draw the plow by the neck; and with frequent bellowing, it testifies to its previous affection if the other happens to be missing. 

Oxen know to withdraw to their stalls when rain hinders them. Likewise, when they have gathered by their natural senses that there is a change in the weather, they look outdoors.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ox (bos), moving from its physical characteristics—specifically the dewlap—to its various allegorical meanings in the Bible, ranging from lustful folly to the hard work of the clergy.</meta>

...and they stretch their necks beyond the enclosures, all of one appearance as if to testify that they wish to go forth.

**Neurus**. Here the countryman guides the **ox** <term>Trionem</term> <note>original: *Neurus. Trionem hic moderatur rusticus.* The word *Trionem* refers to a plowing ox, often associated with the constellation *Septentriones* (the "Seven Oxen" or Big Dipper). "Neurus" is likely a truncated or corrupted reference to a classical poet.</note>.

Because the breadth of the chest from the chin to the knee is called the **dewlap** <term>palearia</term>, it is so named from the skin (*pellis*) itself, as if it were "skin-hanging." This is a sign of nobility in an **ox** <term>bos</term>.

In oxen, therefore, sometimes the madness of the lustful is expressed; sometimes the laborious strength of those **preaching** <term>predicantium</term>; and sometimes the humility of the **Israelites** <term>israhelitarum</term>.

For that the madness of the lustful is designated by the name of the ox through comparison, **Solomon** indicates, who, after describing how a man yielded to the petulance of a seductive woman, added: "And immediately he follows her like an **ox led to the slaughter**" <note>original: *Statimque eam sequitur quasi bos ductus ad victimam*; Proverbs 7:22.</note>.

On the other hand, the words of the Law testify that the labor of preaching is expressed by the name of the ox, saying: "You shall not muzzle the mouth of the **ox while it treads out the grain**" <note>original: *non obturabis os boui trituranti*; Deuteronomy 25:4. In the Middle Ages, this verse was commonly used to argue that those who perform spiritual labor—preachers—deserve physical sustenance from their flock.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bovines from page 40. Having previously addressed the domestic ox (bos) and its role as a symbol for both preachers and the lustful, the text now transitions to its wilder relatives: the buffalo and the aurochs.</meta>

**Buffaloes** <term>Bubali</term> are so named by <gloss>by</gloss> a derivation because <gloss>because</gloss> they are similar to oxen. They are so wild that, because of their fierceness, they do not accept the yoke upon their necks <gloss>necks</gloss>. Africa produces these. The **aurochs** <term>Uri</term> <note>The aurochs (*Bos primigenius*) was a large species of wild cattle, now extinct, that once roamed the forests of Europe and Asia.</note> are wild oxen found in Germany, having horns of such great <gloss>great</gloss> weight that drinking vessels <note>original: *gerule*. These were large drinking horns often rimmed with silver or gold.</note> of remarkable capacity are made from them for the tables of kings <gloss>kings</gloss>.

In the buffaloes or the aurochs, however, we may understand proud teachers or rulers of the common people. They bear a resemblance to good teachers in the rank or office which they hold, but they are swollen <gloss>swollen</gloss> with pride and trust in their hearts <gloss>hearts</gloss> more in worldly power than in divine help. And, contrary to the Apostle, they choose to dominate the clergy rather than to become a model for the flock. The Lord threatens these through <gloss>through</gloss> the prophet <gloss>prophet</gloss>, saying:

> Woe to the shepherds of Israel who fed themselves! Should not the flocks be fed by the shepherds? You ate the milk and covered <gloss>covered</gloss> yourselves with the wool, and you killed what was fat. You did not strengthen what was weak, and you did not heal what was sick; you did not bind up what was broken, and you did not bring back what was cast aside; you did not seek what was lost, but you ruled <gloss>ruled</gloss> over them with harshness and with power. <note>original: *Ve pastoribus israel qui pascebant semet ipsos...* This is a quotation from Ezekiel 34:2-4, a classic biblical indictment of corrupt leadership.</note>

<meta>This page continues the bestiary's discussion of bovines, transitioning from the fierce, wild buffaloes and aurochs described on page 41 to the domestic cow. It utilizes the allegorical method to link the physical properties and biblical mentions of the cow to the nature of Christ.</meta>

### The Cow

The **Cow** <term>Vacca</term> is so called as if it were "bo-acca" <note>Medieval scholars often used creative etymologies; here they link *vacca* to *bos* (ox) to show they belong to the same family.</note>. For it is one of those nouns that changes form according to gender, just as the lion <term>leo</term> becomes a lioness <term>leena</term>, or the dragon <term>draco</term> becomes a she-dragon <term>dracena</term>. This animal is sometimes used to draw out a meaning in a good sense, such as that passage in the Law where a **red cow** <note>original: *vacca rufa*. This refers to the ritual of the Red Heifer described in Numbers 19, used for purification.</note> of full age, which has never pulled a yoke, is commanded to be sacrificed for the atonement of the sanctuary of the Lord.

It is common for strength to be designated by the masculine gender, while weakness <note>original: *infirmitas*. In a theological context, this "weakness" refers to human mortality and the ability to feel pain, which Christ assumed during his life on Earth.</note> is designated by the feminine. What, therefore, is represented by the cow if not the weakness of the Lord’s **incarnation** <term>incarnacio</term> accepted for sacrifice? Concerning which it is written: "Because even if he died out of weakness, yet he lives by power" <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 13:4.</note>.

Moreover, this cow is rightly called "red" because his humanity was clearly made red through the **blood of the Passion** <term>cruorem passionis</term>. The age of the Lord's humanity is complete <gloss>undiminished</gloss>, for his every work was perfect; in that humanity, clearly, there was no blemish <gloss>stain</gloss>. For this reason it is truly written of him: "He who committed no sin, nor was deceit found in his mouth" <note>A reference to 1 Peter 2:22.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the etymology of the cow and interprets the biblical Red Heifer as a symbol of Christ’s human nature, specifically his sacrifice and sinlessness.</summary>
<keywords>cow, Red Heifer, Incarnation, Passion of Christ, sacrifice, atonement, medieval etymology</keywords>

### The Calf

The **calf** and the **young heifer** <note>original: *vitulus & vitula*</note> are named for "greenness" <term>viriditas</term>; that is, they are of a green <gloss>youthful</gloss> age, just like a **virgin** <term>virgo</term> <note>The author uses a popular medieval etymological style, linking the words *vitulus* (calf), *viriditas* (greenness/vitality), and *virgo* (virgin) because of their similar sounds, suggesting they all share the quality of youthful purity.</note>. Thus, we call the heifer "small" and one that has not yet given birth; for one that has given birth is called a young cow <term>iuvenca</term> or a full-grown cow <term>vacca</term>.

The **calf** is certainly understood in a positive light when it demonstrates the **innocence of the faithful** or sincere praise born of devotion, regarding which it is written: "We shall offer the **calf of our lips**" <note>A reference to Hosea 14:2. In this context, "offering a calf" means offering words of prayer and praise to God instead of animal sacrifices.</note>. And in the Psalm it says: "Then," He says, "they shall lay **calves** upon your altar" <note>Psalm 51:19. In a Christian context, this was often interpreted as the offering of a pure and contrite heart.</note>. Indeed, he used the term "calves" to represent the innocent, who are in the first stage of life and whose necks are free from the **yoke of another's sin** <note>The "yoke" refers to the weight of sin or the strict requirements of the Old Law; the calf represents a soul liberated through innocence or grace.</note>. For this reason, he used such language to show that the rituals of the **Old Law** were an image of things to come in the future.

Alternatively, these calves depict the **preachers of the Gospel**, whose image **Luke the Evangelist** <note>In Christian art, the four Gospel writers are symbolized by the "Four Living Creatures." Luke is represented by the winged ox or calf because his Gospel emphasizes the sacrificial and priestly nature of Christ.</note> took up in the form of a calf. These preachers would not strike the air with mere bellowing <note>original: *mugitibus*</note>, but would fill the whole world with the preaching of the Lord's faith. Or rather, those calves

...we must observe those who have offered their souls as a **sacrifice of sweetness** <note>original: *hostia suavitatis*. This is a liturgical term for an offering that is pleasing to God.</note> upon the sacred altars. For even **Father Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose works often used animal symbolism to explain the Gospels.</note>, when he was treating the illustrious figures of the Gospel, in a certain place calls the **Lord himself a "calf,"** who offered himself as a sacrifice for the salvation of all.

Wherefore, whether they are understood as the youth, or as preachers, or as martyrs, he was able to send such ones like those very **calves** to the altars of the Lord, which is newly fitting for the Christian religion. **Christ** is the **calf** in the Gospel: "Bring the **fatted calf** and kill it" <note>A reference to the Parable of the Prodigal Son (Luke 15:23). Medieval commentators viewed this calf as a symbol of Christ’s sacrifice on the cross, provided as a feast for the repentant soul.</note>. And in another part: "And he shall break them to pieces like the **calf of Lebanon**" <note>A reference to Psalm 29:6. Here, the "breaking" refers to the power of God over the world.</note>. **Calves** are those growing in faith and having necks free from the **yoke of the Law** <term>iugum legis</term> <note>The "yoke of the Law" refers to the strict legal requirements of the Old Testament; the calf represents the Christian soul liberated from these burdens by grace.</note>. Calves are also seen frolicking, as in the Psalm: "**Many calves have surrounded me; fat bulls have besieged me**" <note>Psalm 22:12. In the context of the Passion of Christ, these "calves" and "bulls" were interpreted as the crowds and leaders who called for Christ's crucifixion.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the theological discussion of calves and the sacrifice of Christ to a descriptive bestiary entry concerning camels. While the previous page focused on spiritual allegories, this page begins an encyclopedic account of the physical nature, origins, and temperaments of camels, a common structure in medieval manuscripts like those of Isidore of Seville.</meta>

The cause gave the name to **Camels** <note>original: *Camelis*. The author is engaging in etymology, a popular medieval practice of explaining an animal's nature through the origins of its name.</note>: either because, when they are loaded, they lie down so that they might become shorter and lower—since in **Greek** *cami* means "low" and "short" <note>original: *cami*. This likely refers to the Greek word *chamai* (χαμαί), meaning "on the ground."</note>—or because the animal has a curved back, and *camur* is a **Greek** word meaning "curved" <note>original: *camur*. Although the text identifies it as Greek, *camur* is actually a Latin term for "crooked" or "curved."</note>. Although other regions send these animals forth, **Arabia** sends the most. **Bactria** <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan.</note> sends the strongest camels, though **Arabia** may produce more of the other kinds. 

Truly, they differ in this: those of **Arabia** have two humps on their back, while those of **Bactria** have only one <note>This is a reversal of biological reality: Arabian camels (Dromedaries) have one hump, while Bactrian camels have two. Medieval scribes occasionally swapped these details during transcription.</note>. Their feet are never worn down, for they have certain fleshy pads as the soles of their feet. For this reason, it is quite a disaster for them when walking if there is no supporting ground to help their effort in stepping <note>The author suggests that the camel’s soft, fleshy feet are specialized but can be a disadvantage on certain terrains where they cannot gain "purchase" or grip.</note>.

These animals are used for a double service. Some are suited for carrying loads; others are swifter <term>perniciores</term> <note>From the Latin *pernix*, meaning nimble or quick.</note>. But the former will not receive weights beyond what is fair, nor do the latter wish to travel more than their accustomed distances. They are unbridled by such a great desire for **mating** <note>original: *venerem*. Literally "Venus," the goddess of love, used here as a term for the mating instinct.</note> that they become savage when they seek it. 

They hate the **horse-kind**. They can also tolerate thirst for three days. However, when the opportunity to drink is given, they fill themselves as much as will satisfy past cravings and provide for a long time in the future. They seek out **muddy waters** and shun pure ones. Indeed, unless the liquids are miry, they themselves stir up the mud by constant trampling, or so it seems. They last for a hundred years. If by chance they are moved to foreign places, the strangeness of the changed air...

<summary>This page describes the etymological origins of the camel's name and details the physical and behavioral differences between Arabian and Bactrian breeds. It highlights their unique physiology, such as their fleshy feet and humps, as well as their temperament regarding labor, mating, and water consumption.</summary>
<keywords>Camel, Arabia, Bactria, anatomy, animal behavior, etymology, medieval science, dromedary</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page concludes the physical description of the camel—specifically its use in warfare and the management of its temperament—before transitioning into a lengthy Christological allegory. In medieval bestiaries, animals were rarely described for scientific interest alone; their physical traits were interpreted as symbols of divine truths.</meta>

...they contract diseases. Female camels are prepared for war, and it has been discovered that their desire for mating should be restrained; for people believe they become stronger if they are kept from sexual intercourse. 

Moreover, the **Camel** <term>Camelus</term> signifies either the humility of **Christ** <note>original: *xpi*, a standard medieval abbreviation for *Christi*.</note>, who carried the heavy burdens of our sins, or the **Gentile** people <note>The "Gentiles" refers to non-Jewish nations who converted to Christianity.</note> who have been converted to the faith of Christ. 

For as the Lord says in the **Gospel** <term>euuangelio</term>: "It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle <term>foramen acus</term> than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of heaven." This shows that it is easier for Christ to suffer for those who love this world than it is for the lovers of the world to be converted to Christ. 

By the name of the **Camel**, he wished himself to be understood as the one who voluntarily took up the burdens of our weakness through his humility. In whom is that which was written more clearly understood than in him? "The greater you are, the more you should humble yourself in all things." <note>This is a quotation from the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 3:18, emphasizing that true greatness is found in humility.</note> 

By the **needle**, he signifies piercings; and by the piercings, the pains undertaken during the **Passion** <term>passio</term> <note>The "Passion" refers to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus.</note>. Therefore, the **eye of the needle** <term>foramen acus</term> refers to the narrow anguish of the Passion. Through this narrow opening, he deigned to mend—that is, to restore—the torn garments of our human nature. Thus, being better reformed after the Fall <note>The "Fall" refers to the original sin of Adam and Eve, which medieval theologians believed "tattered" the original perfection of human nature.</note>, we may rejoice in the testimony of the **Apostle** <term>apostolus</term> <note>Referring to Saint Paul in his letter to the Galatians 3:27.</note> who says: "For as many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ."

<summary>This page moves from the physical management of camels in war to a spiritual allegory where the camel represents Christ's humility and the "eye of the needle" symbolizes the suffering of the Passion.</summary>
<keywords>camel, Christology, humility, Gospel of Matthew, eye of a needle, Passion of Christ, original sin, baptism, Saint Paul</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the transition from the discussion of the Camel to the Donkey. The previous page concluded the allegory of the Camel, focusing on its humility and its representation of Christ or the converted Gentiles. This page begins a new entry in the bestiary, applying the same method of etymological explanation and moral symbolism to the Donkey.</meta>

The **Donkey** <term>asinus</term> and the **little donkey** <term>asellus</term> are so named from the word for "sitting" <note>original: *a sedendo*</note>, as if to say "the sitting-beast" <note>original: *asedus*</note>. But although this name was more fitting for horses, this animal took it for itself because before humans captured horses, they began to rely on the donkey as their primary mount. Indeed, it is a slow animal and one that resists by no means; as soon as a human wished, the donkey placed itself beneath them <note>The author suggests the donkey's natural submissiveness made it the first animal to be domesticated for riding.</note>.

They are called **Arcadian donkeys** <term>archadicos</term> for the reason that they were first brought from Arcadia <note>A mountainous region in Greece famous in antiquity for its livestock.</note>, and they are large and tall. The smaller donkey, however, is more useful than the **wild ass** <term>onagro</term> <note>The *onager* is the wild ancestor of the domestic donkey, often depicted in bestiaries as untamable and fierce.</note> because it tolerates both hard work and neglect; it does not refuse its modest lot. 

By the name of **Donkeys** <note>original: *asinorum* (masculine) or *asinarum* (feminine)</note>, sometimes the gentleness of simple folk is designated; at other times, it signifies the foolishness of the Gentile people. For the **Donkey**, mystically speaking, because it is a brute and lustful animal, signifies either the Gentile people—as we have said—over... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely continuing to describe how the "lustful" nature of the donkey represents the state of people before they found the Christian faith.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the donkey, explaining its name through a folk etymology related to sitting and its historical role as a mount before the horse. It concludes by shifting into a moral allegory where the donkey represents both simple, gentle people and the "foolish" or "lustful" nature of non-believers.</summary>

Or, it signifies a foolish man and one who follows only the lusts of this world.

Hence, Jacob said to his son Issachar <note>original: *Yfachar*</note>—whose name is interpreted as "reward"—"Issachar is a strong donkey lying down between the borders; he saw that rest was good, and the land was excellent, and he put his shoulder to the burden, and he was made a servant to tribute" <note>A translation of Genesis 49:14–15. The Gentile <term>Gentilis</term> person, whom the Lord purchased with the price of His own blood, was previously like a brute and lustful animal, using no reason at all. Hence he is also "made a servant to tribute"; that is, offering to the King and His Christ the good gifts of faith and the rewards of good works.

Again, the donkey signifies a foolish man. In Deuteronomy <note>Deut. 22:10</note>, it says: "You shall not plow with an ox and a donkey together"; that is, you should not pair a fool with a wise man in the preaching of the Word.

The donkey signifies the Synagogue of the Jews in Genesis <note>Gen. 49:11</note>: "Binding his foal to the vine, and his she-ass to the vineyard, O my son."

The donkey signifies the human body. Whence it is written in the Book of Judges <note>Judges 5:10</note>: "You who ride upon shining donkeys." Likewise there, it is said of Achsah <note>original: *Axa*</note>, the daughter of Caleb—who represents the image of the soul—that "she sighed while sitting upon a donkey" <note>Refers to Judges 1:14. Achsah's position on the donkey was interpreted as the soul's mastery over the physical body and its desires.</note>.

Fearing this, the mothers hide them in secret places. <meta>This page continues the bestiary's entry on the onager (wild ass), moving from its physical description and reproductive habits to its symbolic behavior during the spring equinox and its allegorical connection to the Devil.</meta>

On the twenty-fifth day of the month of March <note>original: *vicesimo quinto die mensis martii*</note>, the animal brays twelve times in the night and likewise during the day. From this, it is known that it is the equinox <note>the time of year when day and night are of equal length</note>. The number of hours of the day or night can be known from the braying of the <term>onager</term> <note>the wild ass</note> as it sounds for each individual hour.

The Devil bears the likeness of this creature. When he knows that night and day are made equal—that is, when those who walked in darkness turn to the Lord and are made equal to the faith of the righteous, just as night is made equal with day—he therefore roars <note>original: *rugit*; although a donkey "brays," the text uses a word that also means "roar" to connect the donkey's sound to the lion's roar in the upcoming scripture</note> by night and by day through the individual hours, seeking his food.

For the wild ass does not bray unless he is longing for food, just as **Job** says: "Does the wild ass cry out unless he is longing for food?" <note>Job 6:5</note> Whence also the Apostle says: "Our adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion seeking someone to devour." <note>1 Peter 5:8</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the onager (wild ass) to the dromedary and the horse. It continues the bestiary's habit of defining animals through their physical traits and the Greek or Latin origins of their names, often drawing from Isidore of Seville’s *Etymologies*.</meta>

### The Dromedary

The dromedary is a type of camel of smaller stature indeed, but of greater speed—from which also it has its name. For in Greek, *dromos* <note>original: *dromos*</note> is the word for "running" and "swiftness." For it is accustomed to travel a hundred and more miles in a single day. This animal <term>ruminates</term> <note>chews the cud</note> just as the sheep, the ox, and the camel do. Rumination <note>original: *ruminatio*</note> is named from the *rumen*, a prominent part of the throat through which swallowed food is recalled <note>brought back up to be chewed again</note> by certain animals.

Behind it and to the right, several other animal heads emerge, including a tan dromedary or camel and two other horse-like heads. At the bottom, a white quadruped lies curled up. To the left of the animals are three stylized trees with large, round, colorful lobes in orange, blue, and green. The frame consists of a thick orange-red outer border and a thinner inner blue border.</image-desc>

### The Horse

Horses <term>equi</term> are so called because when they were joined in <term>quadrigae</term> <note>four-horse chariots</note> they were matched—made equal—and coupled together, equal in form and similar in speed <note>The author is using a wordplay: the Latin word for horse, *equus*, sounds like the word for equal, *aequus*</note>. The common horse <term>caballus</term> is named from the "hollow foot" <note>original: *a pede cavo*; a medieval folk etymology linking the word *caballus* to *cavus*, meaning hollow, referring to the shape of the hoof</note>, because as the hoof, in stepping...

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the horse from the previous page, moving from the etymology of "caballus" (common horse) to the physical characteristics, temperament, and famous historical examples of equine loyalty.</meta>

The pressed hoof hollows out the earth, a trait which other animals do not have. From this, the horse is also called "sounding-foot" <note>original: *sonipes*</note> because it makes a sound with its feet. The liveliness of horses is great; for they exult in the fields, they scent the approach of war, they are excited to battle by the sound of the trumpet, and they are spurred to run by the sound of a human voice. They grieve when they have been defeated and exult when they have won. Some sense their enemies in war to such an extent that they attack their adversaries with bites. Some even recognize their own masters, and if their masters are changed, they forget their former gentleness. 

Some will receive no one upon their backs except their own master. Of this we shall give an example: the horse of Alexander the Great, called Bucephalus <note>original: *Buceuola*; literally "ox-head"</note>. He was named this either from the grimness of his gaze, or because he had the mark of a bull's head branded on his shoulder, or because certain horn-like bumps protruded from his forehead. Although at other times he might allow himself to be mounted gently, once he had received the royal saddle-cloth, he never deigned to carry anyone except his master. There are many proofs of his deeds in battles, in which he carried Alexander safely out of the most cruel struggles by his own aid. 

The horse of Gaius Caesar <note>Julius Caesar</note> received no one on its back except Caesar. When a king of the Scythians <note>original: *scitarum*</note> was killed in single combat and the victorious adversary wished to strip his body of armor, he was kicked and bitten to pieces by the fallen king’s horse. When King Nicomedes was killed, his horse ended its own life through starvation. When Antiochus had subdued the Galatians in battle, he leaped upon a certain horse named Centaur which had been killed in the line of battle, intending to fight. And so, they are possessed of spirits so unbridled that...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of equine nature, following the accounts of historical horses like Bucephalus. It transitions from stories of individual loyalty to general observations on the lifespan, breeding habits, and physical ideals of the horse, largely drawing from the encyclopedic tradition of Isidore of Seville.</meta>

...intentionally withheld from the teat <note>original: *ruma*; an archaic term for the breast or udder</note>, it produces distress and afflicts both itself and the rider. In this kind of animal, the life span is longer for males. We have indeed read that a horse lived to seventy years. We have also noted that a horse named Opontes remained active for communal breeding <note>original: *gregarum venerem*</note> up to forty years. The sexual desire of mares is extinguished if their manes are shorn. 

At their birth, a love-charm <note>original: *veneficium*; often used to mean a potion or magical substance</note> is produced, which they carry on their foreheads; it is a piece of flesh of a yellowish color similar to a dried fig, and it is called <term>hippomanes</term> <note>literally "horse-madness"; a legendary substance used in antiquity for love philtres</note>. If this is snatched away immediately, the mother will by no means offer her teats to the foals for suckling. 

Through leisure, love arises in horses; and the greater the love, the more deeply they plunge their nostrils while neighing. When their masters are slain or dying, horses shed tears. For they say that the horse is the only animal near to man that weeps and feels the emotion of grief. Hence, in the **centaurs** <note>mythological creatures with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse</note>, the nature of horses and men is mixed. For men are accustomed to gather the outcome of a future battle from the horse, based on its sadness or its eagerness. 

It is a common opinion that in noble horses, as the ancients say, four things are looked for: **build**, **beauty**, **merit**, and **color**. 

**Build** <note>original: *forma*</note> requires that the body be strong and solid, with a height appropriate to its strength; its flanks long and its belly tucked up; the haunches very large and round; the chest broad and wide; the whole body knotted with a density of muscles <note>The OCR reads *macularum* (spots), but the context of physical perfection in Isidore suggests *musculorum* (muscles)</note>; the hoof dry and strengthened with a hollow horn. 

**Beauty** <note>original: *pulchritudo*</note> requires that the head be small and lean, with the skin almost...

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the ideal horse, concluding the list of four essential qualities (shape, beauty, merit, and color) begun on the previous page. The text is a direct excerpt from Isidore of Seville's "Etymologies," Book XII, which served as the standard zoological reference for the Middle Ages.</meta>

...clinging to the bones. The ears should be short and keen; the eyes large; the nostrils flared; and the neck erect. The mane and tail should be dense, and the hooves should have a firm, fixed roundness in their solidity. Its **merit** <note>original: *meritum*; referring to the horse's quality or character</note> is that it be bold in spirit, swift of foot, and have trembling limbs—which is an indication of strength—and be one that is easily stirred from a state of total rest, yet once excited to haste, is not difficult to restrain. A horse's disposition is understood by its ears, and its excellence by its trembling limbs.

The **colors** especially to be looked for are: bay <note>original: *badius*</note>, golden, rose-colored, myrtle-colored, deer-colored, dun <note>original: *gilvus*</note>, blue-grey <note>original: *glaucus*</note>, dapple-grey <note>original: *scutulatus*</note>, hoary, shining white, dull white, speckled, and black. Next in order is the variegated horse, distinguished by black and bay; any other variegated color is considered the worst.

The ancients used to call the bay horse *vadium*, because among all other animals it goes <note>original: *vadat*; a pun on *badius* and the verb *vadere*, to go</note> more powerfully. This is the same as the <term>spadix</term> <note>chestnut or date-brown</note>, which some call "phoenician-red" <note>original: *phoenicatum*</note>, named after the color of the palm branch, which the Syrians call a *spadix*. 

A <term>glaucus</term> horse, however, is one that has eyes as if they were painted, suffused with a certain brilliance. A <term>gilvus</term> horse—which is a better color—is somewhat whitish. The speckled <note>original: *guttatus*</note> horse is marked by black spots like falling drops. 

Shining white <note>original: *candidus*</note> and dull white <note>original: *albus*</note> differ from one another. For *albus* is a white with a certain paleness, while *candidus* is snowy and suffused with pure light. It is called **hoary** <note>original: *canus*</note> because it is composed of both shining white and black. It is called **dappled** <note>original: *scutulatus*</note> because of the white circles it has among its purple <note>original: *purpuras*; used here to denote dark or reddish-brown patches</note> markings. The variegated horse...

The <term>dosinus</term> is so called because its color is like that of an ass <note>original: *asino*</note>; it is the same as ash-colored. There are also those born from a wild breed, which we call <term>equiferos</term> <note>wild horses</note>; and for that reason, they cannot pass into a state of "urban dignity" <note>meaning they cannot be domesticated for use in the city or by the nobility</note>. <term>Mauron</term> is black, for the Greeks call black *maurōn*. A <term>mannus</term> is a shorter horse, which the common people call a <term>brunnus</term> <note>a pony or small pack-horse; the root of the word "brown"</note>. 

The ancients used the term <term>veredos</term> <note>the transcription reads *teredos*, likely a scribal error for *veredos*, which were fast post-horses</note> because they carried <term>redas</term> <note>four-wheeled traveling carriages</note>—that is, they led them or ran along the public roads upon which carriages were accustomed to travel. 

There are three kinds of horses:
1. One is noble, fit for battles and all [noble] tasks.
2. The second is common and for the herd, fit for transport but not for riding.
3. The third is born from a mixture of different kinds, which is also called "hybrid" <note>original: *bigenerum*</note>, because it is born of different parents, such as the mule. 

In truth, the term "horse" sometimes turns toward a good significance and sometimes toward a bad one. In a good sense, it is as the prophet Habakkuk says: 

> "Ascending," he says, "upon your horses, and your riding is salvation." <note>Habakkuk 3:8</note> 

And in the book of Job, the divine voice says: 

> "Will you provide strength for the horse, or will you surround his neck with neighing?" <note>Job 39:19</note> 

And so forth. Here, the horses are understood to be the holy preachers, whose rider is the Lord, who [gives] them strength, that is...

<meta>This page continues from a physical description of horse breeds and colors into a spiritual and allegorical interpretation of the horse in Christian theology. On the contrary, however, it is written regarding another species of horses in the Song of Exodus <note>original: *cantico exodi*; referring to the Song of Moses in Exodus 15</note>: "The horse and its rider he has thrown into the sea." And again: "Pharaoh’s chariots and his army he has thrown into the sea." 

There are horses which the Lord mounts and they walk through all the earth, concerning which it is said: "And your riding is salvation" <note>original: *equitatus tuus salus*; a reference to Habakkuk 3:8</note>. There are, however, horses that have the devil and his angels as their rider. 

Judas was a horse, but as long as he had the Lord as his rider, he was part of the <term>cavalry of salvation</term> <note>original: *equitatu salutis*</note>; sent out with the others and with the apostles, he provided health to the sick and healing to the weak. But when he withdrew himself to the devil—for after the morsel <note>original: *buccellam*; refers to the piece of bread Jesus gave Judas at the Last Supper, after which "Satan entered him"</note>, Satan entered into him—Satan became his rider. Having control of him, he began to ride against our Lord and Savior. 

For all who persecute the saints are "neighing horses"; but they have as riders the evil angels by whom they are driven, and for that reason they are fierce. The Lord, therefore, "has thrown the horse and the rider into the sea," and he has become my salvation.

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual and allegorical symbolism of horses discussed on the previous page to a natural history of the mule. While the previous page focused on the horse as a vessel for the soul (either divine or demonic), this page draws from Isidore of Seville’s "Etymologies" to explain the origins of the mule as a man-made hybrid, referencing both Greek etymology and Biblical history.</meta>

**The Mule**

The mule, however, has a name derived from the Greek <note>original: *mulus ... ex greco*; likely referring to the Greek *myle*, meaning mill</note>. For in Greek, it is named for the fact that, having been subjected to the yoke of the bakers, it leads the slow millstones in a circle while grinding. 

The Jews assert that Ana, the great-grandson of Esau, was the first to cause herds of horses to be mounted by donkeys in the desert, so that from there new animals—mules—might be born, contrary to nature <note>This refers to a traditional interpretation of Genesis 36:24, where Ana is said to have "found the hot springs" or, in other translations, "found the mules" in the wilderness</note>. It is also said that <term>onagers</term> <note>original: *onagros*; wild donkeys</note> were admitted to female donkeys for this reason, and that he [Ana] discovered this type of mating so that the swiftest donkeys might be born from them. 

Human ingenuity also forced together animals of different species in mating, and thus through an "adulterous" mixture <note>original: *adulterina commixtione*; here meaning a hybrid or unnatural crossing</note>, another kind was produced. Similarly, Jacob prepared similarities of colors contrary to nature. For his sheep conceived such offspring as they gazed upon the shadows of the rams mounting from above, reflected in the mirror of the waters <note>A reference to Genesis 30, where Jacob used peeled branches to influence the markings of his flock’s offspring</note>.

<summary>This page describes the mule as an unnatural hybrid created through human intervention and selective breeding, citing both Greek etymology and the biblical figure Ana. It also touches on Jacob’s use of visual influence to manipulate the breeding of his flocks.</summary>
<keywords>mule, Isidore of Seville, Ana, Esau, Jacob, hybridization, onager, biblical history, natural history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "maternal impression"—the belief that what a parent sees during conception influences the appearance of the offspring. It moves from the biblical example of Jacob's sheep (on the previous page) to general animal husbandry, human pregnancy, and a classification of various hybrid animals, concluding with the symbolic meaning of the mule in the story of Absalom.</meta>

Finally, it is said that this very same practice occurs among herds of mares: that they place noble horses before the eyes of those conceiving, so that they might be able to conceive and create offspring similar to them. For even lovers of the dove species place paintings of the most beautiful doves in the same places where they frequent, so that by catching their sight, they might generate similar ones. 

Hence it is that some people wish pregnant women to look upon no very ugly animals—such as <term>cynocephali</term> <note>original: *cynocefalos*; literally "dog-heads," a term often used for baboons</note> and apes—lest, occurring to their sight, they give birth to similar offspring. For this is the nature of women: that whatever they have looked upon or conceived in their mind at the height of the heat of pleasure <note>original: *in extimo voluptatis estu*</note> when they conceive, such offspring they procreate. Indeed, the animal in the act of mating <note>original: *in usu uenerio*; literally "in the use of Venus"</note> transmits forms seen from the outside; and being filled with their impressions, it seizes their likeness into its own quality.

Among living creatures, they are called <term>hybrids</term> <note>original: *bigenera*; literally "of two kinds"</note> which are born from different species: a mule from a mare and a donkey; a <term>hinny</term> <note>original: *burdo*</note> from a horse and a female donkey; <term>hybrids</term> <note>original: *Iuride*; likely a corruption of the Latin *hybridae*</note> from wild boars and pigs; <term>tityri</term> <note>original: *titiros*; a legendary cross between sheep and goats</note> from a sheep and a goat; and the <term>musmon</term> <note>original: *musmo*; a mouflon or wild sheep</note> from a female goat and a ram. (The ram, moreover, is the leader of the flock.) A <term>lycaisca</term> <note>original: *licaissa*</note> is born from a wolf and a female dog. 

The mule, because it is a beast of burden, signifies the followers of folly. Absalom, who followed this folly by sitting upon a mule and raising a rebellion against his father, deservedly died <note>A reference to 2 Samuel 18, where Absalom’s hair was caught in a tree while he rode a mule, leading to his death</note>. Accordingly, the Psalm...

<summary>This page explains the medieval theory of maternal impression, where visual stimuli during conception shape the offspring, and provides a list of hybrid animals including the symbolic "folly" of the mule.</summary>
<keywords>maternal impression, hybrids, cynocephali, animal husbandry, Absalom, mule, hinny, wolf-dog, tityrus</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical classification of hybrid animals (such as the mule and hinny) discussed on the previous page to a moral and allegorical interpretation of these creatures. It focuses on a passage from the Psalms that uses the horse and mule as metaphors for human stubbornness and the need for spiritual discipline through fasting.</meta>

The Psalmist restrains his listeners, saying:
> Do not become like the horse and the mule, in which there
> is no understanding. With bit and bridle <note>original: *freno & chamo*</note> bind
> their jaws.

He therefore forbids men of this kind—who are settled in diabolical deceits—from being weighed down by the burdens of vices, lest by obeying poorly they be more fully surrendered to the ranks of pride. But what does he say should be done for such people? Clearly, the same as for senseless animals. For by these comparisons, he invites foolish men to be subjected to the truth. 

For what he says regarding the **bit** <note>original: *freno*</note>: this pertains to the horse. For "bit" <note>original: *frenum*</note> is said to be named from "holding the wild" <note>original: *a fero tenendo*; a traditional medieval etymology attempting to explain the linguistic root of the word</note>. Indeed, the ancients called the horse "the wild one" <note>original: *ferum*</note>. Regarding the **bridle** <note>original: *chamo*; a muzzle or spiked collar used for restraint</note>: this refers to the mule. Therefore, these two aforementioned animals are held back by these restraints so that they may walk at the will of the driver, lest they run wild according to their own desires. 

The jaws <note>original: *maxille*</note> are indeed the instruments of animals by which food is chewed so that the life of the body is provided for. He says, therefore, through an **allegorical figure**, that the jaws of the disobedient ought to be constricted—that is, that small portions of food should be given—so that, hemmed in by the necessity of **fasting** <note>original: *ieiuniorum*</note>, they may be subjected to the command of the Creator.

<summary>This page provides a moral commentary on Psalm 31, using the horse and mule as allegories for stubborn humans who require the "restraint" of fasting and divine discipline to curb their pride.</summary>
<keywords>psalmista, allegory, horse, mule, fasting, etymology, spiritual discipline, pride</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bestiary’s examination of animal natures and behaviors. While the previous page used the horse and mule as allegories for human discipline and the necessity of restraint (the bit and bridle), this page transitions to the specific natural history and legendary habits of the badger. It focuses on the badger's industriousness and a unique, though mythical, cooperative behavior used for digging and transporting earth.</meta>

# The Badger

There is the badger, which is also called the *melota* <note>original: *melota*; a term derived from the Greek *meles*</note>. This animal is biting and unclean <note>The classification of "unclean" often relates to the animal's diet or its habit of living underground in the dirt</note>, frequenting mountainous and rocky places. By scraping and digging with its feet, it shapes underground pits for itself to serve as places of refuge and fortifications. 

Indeed, some among them—prepared to serve the command of nature—clasp earth that has been dug by others to their upturned bellies with all four feet. With a piece of wood placed crosswise in the mouth, and with other badgers gripping the wood from either side with their teeth and pulling backwards, they are dragged along—not without the amazement of those watching <note>This passage describes a famous medieval myth where badgers cooperate by using one individual as a living sled to haul away dirt during the construction of their setts</note>.

<summary>This page describes the physical habits and legendary cooperative labor of the badger, specifically the myth that they use one another as living sleds to transport excavated soil.</summary>
<keywords>badger, taxus, melota, animal behavior, cooperative labor, medieval bestiary, burrowing, nature myths</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the larger forest animals like the badger to common household and field creatures: the cat, the mouse, and the shrew. It continues the medieval tradition of explaining an animal's nature through the presumed origins of its name (etymology).</meta>

# The Cat

<image-desc>A large rectangular miniature with an orange and white border. The background is dark blue, decorated with gold stars and crescent moons. On the left, a dark grey cat stands on its hind legs, reaching for a gold-domed birdcage containing a small bird. Below the cage, a grey cat is curled up. In the center, a light-grey cat stands on its hind legs, holding a black mouse in its front paws. To the right, a simple wooden table holds four white, round objects, likely bread or cheese. The ground is depicted as wavy mounds of brown earth.</image-desc>

The cat is called *musio* <note>original: *musio*</note> because it is an enemy to mice <note>original: *muribus infestus*</note>. The common people call it a *cat* <note>original: *catum*</note> from the word for "capture" <note>original: *captura*</note>. Others say it is named because it "catches," which is to say it "sees" <note>The author is linking the Latin *captat* (he catches/aims for) with the cat’s sharp eyesight</note>. For it sees so keenly that with the brilliance of its eyes it overcomes the darkness of the night. From this, the Greek term for "cat" is derived, meaning clever or ingenious <note>original: *apo tou kaiesthai*; literally "from the burning," referring to the glowing, fire-like quality of a cat's eyes at night</note>.

# The Mouse

The mouse <note>original: *mus*</note> is a tiny animal; its name is Greek in origin <note>original: *mys*</note>, but it has become entirely Latinized.

<image-desc>A smaller rectangular miniature inset into the text with an orange and blue border and a gold background. It depicts a dark grey mouse or shrew standing on a mound of earth, gnawing on one of two white, round objects.</image-desc>

Others say that mice are named so because they are born from the moisture of the earth <note>This refers to the ancient theory of "spontaneous generation," where small creatures were thought to arise directly from inanimate matter like damp soil</note>; for the word *mus* signifies "earth," from which the word for soil, *humus*, is also derived. In this creature, the liver grows larger during the full moon, just as certain sea-dwellers increase in size, which then diminish again as the moon wanes <note>Medieval naturalists believed many biological processes were governed by the phases of the moon</note>.

The shrew-mouse is called *sorex* in Latin because it gnaws <note>original: *rodat*</note>, and it brings forth its young in the manner of a saw <note>original: *in modum serre pariat*; this likely refers to the repetitive, "sawing" motion of its teeth or perhaps its large litters produced in succession</note>. The ancients used to call the shrew *faurex* <note>original: *faurecem*</note>.

<summary>This page explores the etymologies and legendary traits of cats and mice, specifically focusing on the cat's nocturnal vision and the mouse's mythical connection to the earth and lunar cycles.</summary>

<keywords>cat, mouse, shrew, musio, catus, night vision, spontaneous generation, moon phases, animal etymology, medieval bestiary</keywords>

...signifies, and from "limping" <note>original: *claudum*</note> comes "clod" <note>original: *clodum*</note>. Mystically, however, mice signify people who, likewise gasping with earthly desire <note>original: *cupiditate terrenis inhiantes*</note>, snatch prey from the substance of others.

# The Weasel

The weasel <note>original: *mustela*</note> is so named as if it were a "long mouse" <note>original: *mus longus*</note>, for the Greeks call something long *thelon* <note>original: *thelon*; likely a corruption of the Greek *dolichon*</note>. This animal is deceitful by nature. In the house where it lives, once it has given birth to its young, it transfers them from place to place and settles them in a new location. It also hunts snakes and mice.

There are two kinds of weasels. One is a wild forest-dweller, distinct in its size, which the Greeks call *ictidas* <note>original: *ictidas*; likely referring to a marten or ferret</note>; the other wanders about in houses. Some say that they conceive through the ear and give birth through the mouth, and vice-versa. Others say they conceive through the ear and give birth through the ear. It is also said that they possess knowledge of medicine, such that if their offspring happen to be killed, the parents can bring them back to life if they are able to find them.

They signify those people who indeed listen willingly to the seed of the Divine Word, but held back by the love of earthly things, they neglect and ignore what they have heard. The weasel is a figure of thieves, as is shown in the book of Leviticus <note>Leviticus 11:29 lists the weasel among the "unclean" animals that crawl on the ground</note>.

It shows a cross-section of the earth with a dark mole on the surface and another mole moving through a wavy burrow underground. The background is gold, and the miniature is enclosed in a red frame with white ornamentation.</image-desc>

The mole, indeed, which is condemned to perpetual blindness, signifies either idols—which are blind, deaf, and mute—or even idolaters themselves, who always wander in the darkness of ignorance and foolishness. Concerning them, it is written in Isaiah <note>original: *ysaia*; referencing Isaiah 2:20</note> that they should "worship moles and bats," which is to say that the blind should worship the blind. Regarding these people, scripture says elsewhere: "Let those who make them become like them, and all who trust in them" <note>Psalm 115:8</note>.

The mole also demonstrates a prefiguration <note>original: *p̄figurā*</note> of heretics or false Christians. For just as the mole, without eyes, always digs the earth, turns up soil, and eats the roots under the crops, so too are these people deprived of the light of true knowledge. They are entangled in earthly deeds and serve the desires of the flesh and the allurements of pleasure. They attempt to eat away and undermine the roots of good works <note>original: *bonor̄ opum*</note> by which they might live, using the tooth of malice.

The **hedgehog** <note>original: *hericius*; also known in Middle English as an urchin</note> is an animal covered with spines, and it is said to be named from this fact: because it raises itself up <note>original: *erigat se*; the author suggests an etymological link between *hericius* and *erigo* (to raise up)</note> when it is enclosed in its spines, by which it is protected on all sides against snares. --- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the hedgehog's physical behaviors and transitions into its two-fold allegorical meaning: first as a symbol of cunning sinners and then as a symbol of the penitent seeking Christ.</meta>

raises itself up and, having turned into a ball, withdraws into its own armor. There is a certain wisdom in this creature. For when it sees a grape fallen from a vine, it rolls itself onto its back over it and thus presents it to its offspring <note>Medieval bestiaries often depicted hedgehogs carrying grapes or fruit stuck to their quills to feed their young, a behavior not observed in reality.</note>. It is also called an **echinus** <note>original: *echinus*; a Latin term derived from the Greek *echinos* for hedgehog</note>. This same *echinus*, foreseeing the future, prepares two openings for breathing; so that when it perceives the North Wind <note>original: *boream*</note> is about to blow, it blocks the northern one. When it perceives the South Wind <note>original: *nothum*</note> is clearing the clouds of the air, it moves to the northern side, so that it might avoid the winds blowing against it and those that are harmful to its area.

For the **hedgehog** <note>original: *hericius*</note> mystically signifies sinners full of mature vices and hardened in the cunning of wickedness, frauds, and thefts—those who, through deception, seek sustenance for themselves from the labor of others. Of these it is written: *There the hedgehog has its lair and nourishes its young* <note>original: *Ibi habet foueam hericius...*; a reference to the prophecy of desolation in Isaiah 34:15</note>.

Likewise, there is that which is written in the Psalm: *The rock is a refuge for the hedgehogs* <note>original: *petra refugium erinaciis*; Psalm 104:18 (Vulgate 103:18)</note>. The *erinacius* is the same as the *hericius*. It is an entirely timid animal, yet by the foresight of nature, it is always armed; its quills protect it like very sharp and densely packed stakes or bolts. Yet its natural defenses are not sufficient; lest it be caught by some trickery, it always takes refuge among the rocks. This is fittingly applied to the person who is "prickly" with their sins and, fearing the coming judgment, is known to have the **Rock, Christ** <note>original: *petram xp̄m* (Christum)</note>, as a most steadfast refuge.

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual significance of the hedgehog to the description of the ant (formica), focusing on its industry and the legendary "gold-digging" ants of Ethiopia.</meta>

### The Ant

The **ant** <note>original: *formica*</note> is so named because it carries crumbs <note>original: *micas*</note> of grain <note>original: *farris*</note>. It is a creature of great industry; for it provides for the future and prepares in the summer what it may eat in the winter. During the harvest, it chooses **wheat** <note>original: *triticum*</note> and does not touch **barley**. When it rains upon their grain, the whole store was <note>The text likely implies that when grain gets wet, the ants bring it out to dry to prevent rot, a common observation in medieval bestiaries.</note>.

They say that in **Ethiopia** there are ants shaped like dogs, which dig up golden sands with their feet and guard them so that no one may take them away; if anyone tries to seize the gold, the ants pursue them to their death.

The ant has three natures. The **first nature** is that they walk in an orderly fashion, and each one of them carries a grain in its mouth. Those that are empty-handed do not say to the others, "Give us some of your grains," but instead they follow the tracks of those who went before until they find the place where the grain is, and they carry the grain back to their nesting-place. Let these things suffice as a sign of **prudence** <note>In the medieval mind, *prudentia* (prudence) was the ability to judge between right and wrong and plan for the future.</note>; and just as the ants gather together, so too are they rewarded in the future.

The **second nature** is that when it stores the grains in its nesting-place, it divides them in two, lest by chance the rains soak them in winter and the grains germinate, causing the ant to die of hunger. In the same way, you, O man, [should divide] the words of the **Old and New Testaments**... <note>The text breaks off here; the allegory typically compares the "dividing" of the grain to distinguishing between the literal and spiritual meanings of the Bible.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical habits and legendary varieties of the ant, using its industry and "division" of grain as an allegory for Christian prudence and the interpretation of Scripture.</summary>

<keywords>ant, formica, Ethiopia, gold-digging ants, wheat, barley, Old Testament, New Testament, prudence, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the ant’s industry and meteorological foresight before transitioning to the entry for the frog. It links the behavior of the ant to themes of "pious industry" and the frog to the "vain chatter" of heretics.</meta>

The prey is gathered in a black column through the fields; the paths seethe with the company of servants <note>original: *mancipium*; here referring to the ants as a collective workforce</note>, and those things which cannot be held in a narrow mouth—the grains—are carried on their shoulders. The master of the harvest watches this and blushes to deny such small profits to such pious industry. 

The ant <term>formica</term> <note>the Latin name is traditionally derived from *ferre micas*, "to carry crumbs"</note> also knows how to seek out the time of fair weather. For when it happens that its fruits become damp with soaking rain, having diligently examined the air to see when it might maintain a lasting calm, it brings out its heaps. It carries them out from its burrows on its own shoulders, intending to dry the grains <term>frumenta</term> themselves in the constant sun. Finally, in all those days, you will hardly ever see rain falling from the clouds, for the ant will have already recalled its crops back to its own granaries.

[A rectangular framed miniature with a thick red border enclosing an inner orange frame. It depicts a body of water with wavy blue and white lines. Frogs <note>original: *Ranae*</note> are named for their chatter <note>original: *garrulitate*</note> because they make a noise around the marshy places where they are born, and they echo with the sound of their voice in troublesome clamors. Frogs represent demons in the Apocalypse <note>original: *apocalipsi*; the Book of Revelation</note>: "I saw coming out of the mouth of the dragon... unclean spirits <term>spiritus</term> like frogs" <note>Revelation 16:13</note>. Moreover, the frogs of the demons are heretics <term>heretici</term>, who, dwelling in the mire of the vilest senses, do not cease to bark with vain chatter, as seen in Exodus <note>original: *exodo*; referring to the plague of frogs in Egypt</note>.

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the frog to the entry for the salamander. It moves from the negative chatter of the frog (representing demons) to the miraculous, fire-resistant qualities of the salamander, which is used as a metaphor for the faith of the righteous.</meta>

[An illuminated miniature set within a rectangular frame. Three salamanders, depicted as long, serpent-like creatures with gray and white stripes, are shown emerging from or resting within a mass of tall, stylized orange flames. The background of the miniature is split: the left side is burnished gold, and the right side is dark blue decorated with small white stars or dots. The frame consists of an inner green border and an outer red border.]

There is an animal that is called a **salamander** <note>original: *salamandra*</note> in Greek, but in Latin it is called a **starred lizard** <term>stellio</term> <note>This name likely refers to the "star-like" spots often found on European salamanders and lizards.</note>. This creature is similar to a small lizard of varied color. Concerning this, Solomon says: "Like a starred lizard dwelling in the houses of kings." <note>original: *Sicut stellio habitans in domibz regum*; a variation of Proverbs 30:28</note>. The **Physiologus** <note>An influential ancient and medieval text that provided moral and Christian interpretations for the characteristics of animals.</note> says of it that if by chance or in any way it enters a chimney of burning fire, or an oven, or any conflagration, it immediately extinguishes the fire. 

In this way, they are as wondrous to all men as were Ananias, Azarias, and Misael <note>The three Hebrew youths in the Book of Daniel who were cast into a "fiery furnace" by King Nebuchadnezzar but were protected by God.</note> in the furnace of burning fire; the fire did not touch them at all. Daniel clearly declares that they emerged from the furnace of fire untouched and undefiled. 

And the Apostle Paul bears witness, saying: "We suffer tribulations, but we are not distressed; we are brought low, but we are not destroyed." <note>original: *Tribulationes patimur sʒ ñ angustiamur*; referencing 2 Corinthians 4:8-9</note> Also: "They shut the mouths of lions, they extinguished the fury of fire." <note>referencing Hebrews 11:33-34</note> So too, whoever believes in God with all their faith and perseveres in good works shall pass through the **Gehenna** <term>gehennam</term> <note>a term for hell or a place of fiery torment/trial</note> of fire, and the flames of...

...to which **Isaiah the prophet** <note>original: *ysaïas ppha*</note> bears witness: "If you pass through the fire, the flame shall not burn you." <note>original: *Si trāsieris p ignem flāmma nū uret te*; from Isaiah 43:2. In the Middle Ages, this verse was often used to explain how the righteous, like the salamander, could survive the "fires" of temptation.</note>

Again, concerning the same: The **salamander** <note>original: *Salamandra*</note> is so named because it prevails against fire. Among all venomous creatures, its power is the greatest. For while other venomous animals strike individuals, this one kills many at once. For if it crawls into a tree, it infects all the fruit with poison and kills those who eat of that fruit. If it should even fall into a well, the strength of its poison kills those who drink from it. This creature, resisting fire, is the only animal that extinguishes flames; for it lives in the midst of flames without pain or being consumed, and not only is it not burned, but it even puts out the fire.

**Birds** <note>original: *auium*</note> have but a single name, yet the group is divided. For just as they differ from one another in appearance, so too do they differ in the diversity of their natures. For some are simple, like **doves** <note>original: *columbe*</note>. Others are clever, like the **partridge** <note>original: *perdix*</note>. Some submit themselves to the hand [of man], like **hawks** <note>original: *accipitres*</note>; others shrink away in fear, like the **garamantes** <note>original: *garamantes*; likely referring to a wild bird associated with the Garamantes, a North African tribe known in antiquity for living in remote, harsh environments.</note>. Some take delight in the company of humans, like the **swallow** <note>original: *hirundo*</note>; others love a secret life in the desert, like the **turtledove** <note>original: *turtur*</note>. Some feed only on gathered seeds, like the **goose** <note>original: *anser*</note>; others eat flesh and focus on plunder, like the **kite** <note>original: *miluus*</note>. Some are social—that is, flying in flocks—like **starlings** <note>original: *sturni*</note> and **quails** <note>original: *coturnices*</note>. Others are solitary—that is, alone—for the purpose of predatory ambush, like the **eagle** <note>original: *aquila*</note>. And it...
