...[prey] and whichever are of that kind. Some birds make a noise with their voices, like the **swallow** <note>original: *hirundo*</note>; others produce the most sweet songs, like the **swan** <note>original: *cygnus*</note> and the **blackbird** <note>original: *merula*</note>. Others imitate the words and voices of humans, like the **parrot** <note>original: *psitacus*</note> and the **magpie** <note>original: *pica*</note>. 

But there are other kinds, and they are also innumerable in their habits. For who is able to find out how many kinds of winged creatures there are? For no one has been able to penetrate all the deserts of **Scythia** <note>An ancient region in central Eurasia</note> and **India** or **Ethiopia** so as to know their kind or their differences. 

**Birds** <note>original: *Aves*</note> are so named because they do not have straight paths but rather wander through trackless places <note>original: *auia* (avia). The author is using a classic medieval etymology, linking the word for bird (*avis*) to the word for "trackless" or "off the road" (*avius*), because birds fly through the air where there are no paths.</note>. 

**High-fliers** <note>original: *Alites*</note> are named because they aim for the heights with high flight <note>original: *altis alta*</note>, and they ascend to the heavens by the rowing of their wings. 

**Winged creatures** <note>original: *Volucres*</note> are named from flying <note>original: *uolando*</note>. For wherever "to fly" (*volare*) comes from, from that same source we also say "to walk" <note>original: *ambulare*</note>. For the middle part of the foot or the hand is called the **palm** <note>original: *Vola*</note>, and in birds, it is the middle part of the wings by whose movement the feathers are shaken; hence they are called *volucres*. 

**Chicks** <note>original: *Pulli*</note> is the name given to the young of all birds. But the young of four-footed animals are also called *pulli*, and even a small human is called a *pullus*. Therefore, the newly born are called *pulli* because they are "soiled" <note>original: *polluti*. The author suggests a linguistic link between the young (*pullus*) and being "soiled" or "dark" (*pollutus*) at birth, perhaps referring to the birth-fluids or the downy covering.</note>; from this, a black garment is also called *pulla*. 

There are **wings** <note>original: *Alie* (alae)</note> in which feathers are arranged in order for the use of flying. They are called *alae* because with these, birds "nourish" <note>original: *alant*</note> and cherish their chicks in an embrace. 

A **feather** <note>original: *Penna*</note> is named from "hanging" <note>original: *pendendo*</note>, that is, from flying; hence also "to hang" <note>original: *pendere*</note>. For winged creatures are moved by the help of feathers when they commit themselves to the air. 

A **down-feather** <note>original: *Pluma*</note> is as if...

<meta>This page continues the medieval encyclopedic tradition of Rabanus Maurus, moving from the physical descriptions and etymologies of birds to their allegorical and spiritual interpretations in Christian scripture.</meta>

...feathers <note>original: *pluma*</note>. For just as hair is on the body of four-footed animals, so are feathers on birds. Many names for birds are derived and composed from the sounds of their voices, such as the **crane** <note>original: *grus*</note>, **raven** <note>original: *corvus*</note>, **swan** <note>original: *cygnus*</note>, **owl** <note>original: *bubo*</note>, **kite** <note>original: *milvus*</note>, **screech-owl** <note>original: *ulula*</note>, **cuckoo** <note>original: *ciculla*</note>, and **jackdaw** <note>original: *graculus*</note>, among others. **Winged creatures** <note>original: *Volucres*</note> signify angels; as in the Book of **Job**, regarding the origin of wisdom, it is said: *“the birds of heaven have nests.”* <note>The author appears to be blending Job 28:21, which discusses wisdom being hidden from birds, with Matthew 8:20, which mentions birds having nests, to argue that birds represent heavenly messengers.</note> **Winged creatures** can also represent proud men, as written in the **Psalter**: *“All sheep and oxen, and moreover the beasts of the field, the birds of heaven and the fish of the sea.”* <note>Psalm 8:7-8</note>

**Flight** <note>original: *Volatus*</note> represents the ascent of the saints to God, or the understanding of the Holy Scriptures; as in the **Psalter**: *“I will fly away and be at rest.”* <note>Psalm 55:6</note>

Furthermore, **birds** <note>original: *aves*</note> and **winged creatures** hold diverse symbolic meanings. For sometimes they signify angels, as in the passage: *“The birds of heaven shall carry your voice.”* <note>Ecclesiastes 10:20</note> At other times, they represent holy men, as in the Lord’s testimony regarding the **mustard seed**: though it is indeed the smallest of all herbs, it grows and becomes a tree, so that *“the birds come and settle in its branches.”* <note>Matthew 13:31-32</note> The preaching of the **Gospel** is the least of all disciplines in the eyes of the wise of this world, but it grows into a tree, such that the birds of heaven...

### The Eagle

**A**he **Eagle** <note>original: *Aquila*</note> is so named because of the sharpness <note>original: *acumine*</note> of its eyes. For its vision is said to be so keen that while it is carried over the seas on motionless wings, it remains hidden from human sight; and yet, from such a great height, the fish... <note>The text cuts off at "pis", likely the beginning of *pisciculos* (small fish) or *pisces* (fish). In the medieval bestiary tradition, this passage describes the eagle spotting fish from the clouds and diving to catch them, symbolizing Christ’s ability to see and rescue souls from the "waves" of the world.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the eagle, explaining the medieval etymology of its name based on its "sharp" eyesight and its ability to spot prey from incredible heights.</summary>
<keywords>eagle, Aquila, vision, bestiary, etymology, natural history, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the eagle's hunting habits before moving into its legendary renewal and the "sun test" for its young. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought from the previous page regarding how the eagle spots fish from the sky.</meta>

...sees [the fish] swimming and, descending like a bolt <note>original: *tormenti instar* - literally "like a siege engine" or missile</note>, carries the seized prey in its wings to the shore. 

When, however, the eagle has grown old, its wings become heavy and its eyes are covered with mist. Then it seeks a spring, and flies opposite it high up into the region of the sun; and there it sets its wings on fire. Likewise, it burns away the mist of its eyes in the rays of the sun. Then at last, descending into the spring, it plunges itself three times; and immediately it is renewed with great strength of wings and brightness of eyes. 

So too you, O man, who have an old garment and a mist over the eye of your heart: seek the spiritual spring <note>original: *spiritualem fontem*</note> of the Lord and lift the eyes of your mind to the Lord, who is the spring of justice <note>original: *fons iusticie*</note>; and then your youth shall be renewed like the eagle's. 

It is asserted <note>This refers to the *Physiologus*, an ancient Greek text that is the basis for most medieval bestiaries.</note> that the eagle presents its own chicks to the rays of the sun. It suspends them by a claw in the middle of the air; and if any chick, when the light of the sun is reflected, keeps the intrepid gaze of its eyes fixed with unyielding vigor, it is proven that it has demonstrated the truth of its nature. But the one who turns its eyes away from the sun’s ray is held as degenerate and unworthy of such a parent; nor is it considered worthy of being raised if it was unworthy of being acknowledged. Therefore, the eagle condemns not with natural bitterness, but with the integrity of judgment. It does not renounce the chick as its own, but rejects it as if it were a stranger. However, this harshness of the royal bird, as it seems to some, the mercy of a common bird <note>In the bestiary tradition, this "common bird" is often the coot or the sea-eagle, which was said to adopt the chicks the eagle rejected.</note> excuses.

<summary>This page details the eagle's legendary ability to rejuvenate itself by flying toward the sun and bathing in a spring, providing a moral allegory for spiritual renewal. It also describes the "sun test" used to determine the legitimacy of its offspring.</summary>
<keywords>eagle, renewal, spiritual spring, sun test, Physiologus, allegory, baptism, justice</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the Eagle to the entry on the Barnacle Goose. The text on the previous page concluded the Eagle's moralization. This page begins a new section describing the mythical origins of geese in Ireland.</meta>

There are in Ireland <note>original: *ybernia*</note> many birds called barnacles <note>original: *bernace*</note>, which nature produces in a marvelous manner, contrary to the usual laws of nature. They are indeed similar to marsh geese <note>original: *aucae*</note> but smaller in size. They are first born like gum from fir-wood logs <note>original: *lignis abietinis*</note> that have been tossed about by the sea. Then, enclosed in certain shells <note>original: *conchilibus*</note> as if from seaweed clinging to the wood, they hang by their beaks until they have reached a more fully developed form. And so, when in the course of time they have put on a firm covering of feathers, they either fall into the waters or fly away into the freedom of the air. They receive their nourishment and growth simultaneously from the juices of the wood and the sea, through a hidden and most wonderful process of germination <note>original: *seminis ratione* — literally the "reasoning" or "logic" of seed; the author is explaining how they grow without traditional eggs</note>. Many times I have seen with my own eyes more than a thousand tiny bodies of these birds on the seashore, hanging from a single piece of wood...

<summary>This page describes the medieval belief that barnacle geese were not hatched from eggs but spontaneously generated from driftwood in the sea. The author, likely Giraldus Cambrensis, claims to have personally witnessed thousands of these developing "bird bodies" clinging to logs.</summary>
<keywords>barnacle goose, Ireland, spontaneous generation, nature, driftwood, medieval biology, maritime legends</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues Gerald of Wales's account of the Barnacle Goose from the previous page. Having described their physical origin from driftwood, he now discusses the theological and dietary implications of their "fleshless" birth, using them as a polemical argument against those who doubt the Virgin Birth.</meta>

...hanging, enclosed in shells, and thus fully formed. They do not produce eggs through birth as is customary. Furthermore, no bird ever broods upon eggs for their procreation. In no corner of the earth are they seen either to indulge in lust or to build nests. Hence, in certain parts of Ireland, bishops and religious men are accustomed to eat these birds without hesitation during times of fasting <note>During the Middle Ages, meat from land animals was forbidden on fast days. Because these geese were believed to be "born of the sea" and not of "flesh," they were sometimes classified similarly to fish.</note>, because they are not born of flesh, but are as if they were not made of meat. 

Look back, unhappy Jew <note>The author uses a common medieval polemical style, addressing a hypothetical Jewish skeptic to argue for Christian doctrines using "natural" evidence.</note>, look back even if it is late! You do not dare to deny the **first generation** of man from mud without male or female <note>Referring to the creation of Adam from the earth.</note> out of reverence for the Law. The **second generation**, from a male without a female <note>Referring to the creation of Eve from Adam's rib.</note>, you also do not dare to deny. The **third generation** alone—namely from both male and female—you approve and affirm with a stiff neck <note>original: *dura ceruice*; a biblical idiom for being stubborn.</note> because it is common. 

But the **fourth generation**, in which alone is salvation—namely from a female without a male <note>Referring to the Virgin Birth of Jesus.</note>—you detest with obstinate malice to your own destruction. Blush, wretch, blush! At least return to nature, which, for the proof of our faith and for our instruction, daily procreates and produces new animals without any male or female. 

Therefore, the first generation was from mud, and this last is from wood. The first, because it proceeded from the Lord of nature as a one-time event, was truly astounding. This one <note>the barnacle goose</note>, though no less marvelous, is less wondered at because nature provides it frequently by imitation. For human nature is so composed that nothing except the unusual or the rarely occurring produces wonder. At the rising and setting of the sun...

<summary>Gerald of Wales uses the mythical "wood-born" origin of barnacle geese to argue for the possibility of the Virgin Birth, rebuking skeptics by pointing to wonders in the natural world. He also notes that Irish clergy sometimes ate these birds during fasts, viewing them as non-flesh creatures.</summary>

## Of the birds of a dual nature

There are many birds there of a dual nature, which they call **Aunfrigios** <note>original: *Aunfrigios*; likely a variant of *Anfregii*, referring to the Osprey or Sea-eagle.</note>. They are indeed smaller than an **Eagle**, but larger than a **Hawk**. To these birds, the admirable work of a "playful nature" <note>original: *naturae ludentis*; a medieval concept suggesting that Nature expresses creativity through the biological variety of "monstrous" or unusual creatures.</note> has granted one foot armed with talons, fit for grasping and predatory, and the other foot closed and peaceful, suitable only for swimming. 

A marvel concerning these birds, which I have very often seen with my own eyes: they remain at the very height of the air above the waves of the sea, so that they may fix the sharp gaze of their eyes most intently <note>The OCR reads *ubinimia*, likely a transcription error for *intentissime*.</note> [downward]. There, they hold themselves still and support themselves by the rowing of their wings. And when they see fish hiding in the deep of the waves through such a great [distance]...

<summary>Gerald finishes his theological defense of the Virgin Birth and begins describing the Osprey, a bird he claims has one raptor's foot and one webbed foot.</summary>

<keywords>Gerald of Wales, Osprey, Aunfrigios, dual nature, playful nature, medieval zoology, Ireland, maritime birds</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the osprey (Aunfrigio) and its moral allegory before introducing the kingfisher (Martineta). It maintains the theme of Gerald of Wales using Irish fauna to illustrate theological truths.</meta>

...through the turbulent space between air and water with sharp sight; with the speed of a wing, they cast themselves downward. And while they dive and emerge: they steer themselves in the waters with their swimming foot; but as victors, they extract and carry away their prey with their predatory foot.

In this way, the **ancient enemy** <note>original: *hostis antiquus*; a common medieval epithet for Satan.</note> beholds us in the turbulent waves of this world, no matter how hidden we think our actions are, with a sharp gaze. And while he enters gently into the successes of worldly affairs as if with a "peaceful foot": at last, like a predator, he bloodies, seizes, and scatters wretched souls as if with a "predatory foot."

There are many small birds in **Ireland** <note>original: *Hibernia*</note> that they call **kingfishers** <note>original: *martinetas*; Gerald is likely referring to the Common Kingfisher, *Alcedo atthis*.</note>, which are smaller than a **blackbird** <note>original: *merula*</note>. They are common there, though elsewhere they are rare; they are river-birds, like a **quail** <note>original: *coturnix*</note> in manner, and they [feed] on tiny fish...

<meta>This page concludes the natural history and moral allegory of the kingfisher (Martineta) begun on the previous page. dipping the **quills** <note>original: *caulos*; here referring to the shafts of the feathers or perhaps the bird's extremities as it feeds.</note> on which they feed into the water. 

And although they retain the same nature of feathers as other birds, they vary in color alone. For they are conspicuous and beautiful, with a white belly and a black back, and others gleaming with a pale greenness in the manner of a **parrot** <note>original: *psitaci*</note> or **peacock** <note>original: *pauonis*</note>. Indeed, if they are preserved in a dry place after death, they do not know how to rot; and when placed among other garments or any other things, they keep them safe from the **moth** <note>original: *tinea*</note> and make them fragrant. 

And what is far more worthy of wonder: when suspended by their beaks in a dry place, they are changed by a renewed restoration of their feathers every year, as if a vital spirit still thrived from the surviving parts, with the eyes still remaining. 

In this way, the **saints** who are dead to the world, kindled by the fire of **charity** <note>original: *caritas*</note> and placed, as it were, in a dry place, put on the incorruptibility of character. They purge and refine themselves and those joined to them from the **moth of vices** <note>original: *uitiorum tinea*</note> through the study of good deeds, and they make them praiseworthy with the sweet scent of virtues. And while they are suspended on high by the total intention of the mind, they are renewed for the better from day to day and changed by the casting off of old things and the new increases of virtues—entirely stripping off the **old man** and putting on the **new** <note>A reference to the Pauline epistles (Ephesians 4:22–24), a common medieval motif for spiritual conversion and growth.</note>. For it is surely the greatest progress of praise whenever former things are overcome by those that follow.

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the kingfisher (Martineta) to the nature of the coot. The previous page ended with a moralizing comparison of the kingfisher to saints; this page continues the theme of avian behavior as a model for or contrast to human morality.</meta>

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature within a rectangular frame. Two long-necked birds, likely coots (fulica), swim on green-and-white wavy water. The background is gold leaf. The scene is enclosed in a border of red with pink circles containing crosses, all within a blue outer frame.</image-desc>

There is a bird whose name is the **coot** <note>original: *fulica*</note>, which in Greek is called the **phene** <note>original: *difene*; likely a Latinization of the Greek *phēnē* (φήνη), a bird often identified as the osprey or sea-eagle in classical texts, but here associated with the coot's behavior.</note>. It takes up the **eagle’s chick** <note>original: *aquile pullum*</note>—whether it has been cast off, renounced, or simply not recognized by its parent—and joins it with its own brood. Mixing the foundling with its own young, the coot feeds and protects it with the same duty of maternal diligence and with an equal provision of nourishment as those it fosters.

Therefore, the **phene** <note>original: *fene*</note> nourishes the children of others; we, however, treat our own with the cruelty of an enemy. For the **eagle** <note>original: *aquila*</note>, when she gives birth, does not truly give birth to the chick as her own; instead, she refuses to recognize it, as if it were of a degenerate or inferior stock <note>This refers to the medieval belief that eagles tested their young by forcing them to look into the sun; those that blinked were cast out of the nest as "illegitimate."</note>. We, what is even worse, renounce and cast off those whom we actually recognize as our own.

---

<meta>This page transitions from the behavior of the coot and eagle to the nature of the vulture. <image-desc>A large, ornate Romanesque decorative initial 'V' begins the text. The letter is red with white geometric patterns inside, set against a background of blue penwork filigree that extends into the left margin and fills the space between the first few lines of text.</image-desc>

The **vulture** <note>original: *vultur*</note> is thought to be named for its **slow flight** <note>original: *volatu tardo*. Medieval scholars like Isidore of Seville often sought the meaning of an animal's name in its behavior; they believed the vulture's heavy body prevented rapid flight.</note>. Because of its great size, its body does not allow for swift or frequent flight. However, vultures—and even eagles—can sense corpses even across the seas. Indeed, they fly quite high and are often hidden by the shadows of many mountains, yet from that great height, they look down and see them.

For men say that vultures do not indulge in **intercourse** <note>original: *concubitui*</note>, nor are they joined by the marital custom of a wedding bond. Rather, the females are said to conceive without any male effort and give birth without union. The offspring born from these are said to reach a great age, such that the span of their lives is extended up to a hundred years; the end of a short day does not easily overtake them.

What do they say now, those who are accustomed to mock our **mysteries** <note>A reference to the central tenets of Christian faith, specifically the Incarnation.</note> when they hear that a virgin gave birth? Why do they think a birth is impossible for an unmarried woman whose modesty was never violated by any familiarity with a man? That which is not denied as being possible for vultures is deemed impossible for the **Mother of God**. A bird gives birth without a male and no one refutes it; yet because the betrothed Virgin Mary gave birth, her modesty is called into question. Do we not notice that the Lord establishes the truth even through the laws of nature?

Vultures are also accustomed to announce the death of men by certain signs. Men have been so taught and instructed by this evidence that when a **lamentable war** <note>original: *bellum lacrimabile*</note> approaches and opposing battle lines are drawn up, the aforementioned birds [gather] in great...

<summary>This page describes the vulture, focusing on its keen sight and the medieval belief in its spontaneous or virginal conception, which the author uses to argue for the possibility of the Virgin Birth of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, vultur, Virgin Birth, nature as witness, longevity, animal behavior, Christian symbolism, St. Ambrose</keywords>

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature painting with a red border decorated with white vine-like flourishes. The scene depicts four cranes with blue feathers and long, slender necks. **Cranes** <note>original: *Grues*. Medieval bestiaries often begin an animal's entry by explaining its name, a practice popularized by Isidore of Seville.</note> take their name from their own voice, for they make a sound like a **whirring murmur** <note>original: *susurrant*. The text suggests the name "Grus" is onomatopoeic, mimicking the bird's guttural call.</note>. These birds take turns caring for one another as they direct their **expeditions** <note>original: *expeditiones*. The author uses military language to describe bird migration.</note>. They travel under a certain military-like signal. Furthermore, lest the force of the winds blow them back from their intended destination due to their lightness, they **swallow sand**; they also pick up **small pebbles** to weigh themselves down to a manageable heaviness. 

Then, they strive to reach the highest altitudes so that, from a loftier **vantage point** <note>original: *specula*, literally a watchtower or lookout post.</note>, they may see which lands they should seek. Moreover, while they hasten along, they follow a single leader in a **literary order** <note>original: *ordine litterato*. This refers to the "V" or "Y" shape of their flight formation, which ancient and medieval scholars believed resembled written letters.</note>. As the poet **Lucan** <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet. This quote is from his epic poem *Pharsalia*.</note> wrote: *“And the letter is lost, confused by scattered wings.”* One bird, confident in its path, goes before the flocks. It flies...

<summary>This page describes the migration of cranes, noting their military-like organization, their use of sand and stones as ballast against the wind, and their "V" formation which was thought to resemble written letters.</summary>
<keywords>cranes, migration, Lucan, flight formation, animal ballast, bestiary, vigilance</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the crane (Grus), focusing on their highly organized social structure, their communal care for the weary, and their famous methods of remaining vigilant while guarding the flock at night.</meta>

...punishes sloth and compels the column with its voice. And when that voice has become hoarse, another bird takes its place. There is a shared concern among them all for those who are weary, so much so that if any should falter, they all come together to support the exhausted birds until their strength is restored by rest.

Cranes maintain a careful watch during the night. You may see sentries stationed while the other members of the flock rest. Others circle and scout to ensure no ambush is attempted from any side, providing total protection through their tireless vigor.

Once the time for the watch has been completed, those who have performed their duty settle down to sleep, first letting out a cry to wake the sleeper who is to take over the shift. That bird willingly accepts its lot; unlike us, it does not reluctantly give up the security of sleep, but briskly leaves its bed to perform its turn. It returns the favor it received with equal care and devotion. Thus, there is no desertion because their devotion is natural; the guard is secure because their will is free.

Furthermore, cranes divide their night watches into shifts and cycles, holding a **small stone** <note>original: *lapillo*. This is a famous image in medieval art: the crane holds a stone in its foot so that if it falls asleep, the stone will drop and wake it.</note> in their raised claws to keep sleep at bay. A cry signals when there is something to fear. Their color reveals their age, for they **turn dark** <note>original: *ingresant*, likely a variation of *nigrescunt*. Medieval observers noted that the plumage of certain cranes changed as they aged.</note> in their old age.

year of age, it learns what is shown to it
both more quickly and retains it more firmly;
but when it is a little older, it is

The **Caladrius** <note>original: *Caladrius*. A legendary white bird of medieval lore, often identified with the stone-curlew, believed to possess prophetic healing powers.</note>, as the **Physiologus** <note>original: *phisiologus*. An influential Greek text from the 2nd century AD that served as the primary source for medieval animal lore and Christian symbolism.</note> says, is

Its internal **waste** <note>original: *fimus*. This refers to cataracts or a clouding of vision.</note>.
This bird is found in the **courts of kings**. If someone
is in a state of sickness, it is known by this Caladrius
whether they shall live or die. If, therefore, the man's
illness is **unto death**: as soon as it sees the sick man,
it **turns its face away** from him; and all

<meta>This page continues the description of the Stork (Ciconia), following the introductory sentence on the previous page regarding the sound they make with their beaks. ...[their beaks], they fly to Asia, proceeding in a gathered troop. It is said that these birds have no tongues. Storks precede them as leaders <note>original: *Ciconias duces*; in many medieval bestiaries, it is actually crows (*cornices*) that are said to lead the storks during migration, but this scribe identifies the leaders as storks.</note>, and they follow like an army. Their devotion <note>original: *pietas*; referring to the Roman concept of duty toward family.</note> toward their children is extreme. For they tend to their nests so devotedly that they lose their feathers from constant flying. However much time they spend in rearing their elders, just as much are they themselves nourished in turn by their own chicks <note>This refers to the "antipelargosis" legend, where young storks were believed to carry their elderly parents on their wings and feed them when they could no longer fly.</note>. 

The **stork**, therefore, signifies pure men and cautious servants of God in their way of life, who pursue serpents <note>In medieval symbolism, the serpent almost always represents the Devil or temptation.</note> and count their poisons as nothing; they pursue and reduce to nothing the evil spirits with their poisonous suggestions. Of these, **Jeremiah** says: "The turtledove and the swallow and the stork have known the time of their coming" <note>Jeremiah 8:7</note>. This is the time when the **Holy Church** <note>original: *sc̃a ecclia*</note> was to be gathered into one at the coming of the Savior.

The **Heron** <note>original: *Ardea*</note> is so called because it is **Lofty** <note>original: *Ardua*; a typical medieval etymology linking the bird's name to its high flight.</note> on account of its high flights. Whence **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet (39–65 AD) often cited in medieval texts.</note> says: "The heron flies high, fearing [storms]" <note>original: *Metuens alta uolant ardea*; from Lucan's Pharsalia, book 5.</note>. For it dreads rainstorms and [flies] above the clouds...

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on birds, concluding a point about the heron's flight and providing a full allegorical analysis of its behavior and appearance. It moves from the physical signs of weather to the spiritual symbolism of the "just man" and the monastic life.</meta>

...[loftiness] flies so high that it cannot feel the storms of rain. When it flies even higher, however, it signifies a coming storm. Many call this bird a **tantalum** <note>original: *tantalum*; a name used in medieval bestiaries for the heron, sometimes confused with the ibis or a mythical bird.</note>. 

This bird can signify the souls of the saints or the elect who, fearing the disturbances of this world lest they be caught up in the storms of persecution at the devil’s instigation, raise their intention above all temporal things. They lift their minds to the serenity of the heavenly homeland where the face of God is seen. 

Although the **heron** <note>original: *ardea*</note> seeks its food in the waters, it nevertheless builds its nest in the woods and in the trees. This is because the just man, although he makes use of transitory things <note>Meaning temporary, worldly needs like food and shelter.</note>, his soul delights in eternal things. The heron strives to defend its chicks in the nest with its beak, so that they are not snatched away by other birds. In the same way, the just man, with a strong rebuke <note>original: *inuectione*; a "strong invective" or verbal correction used by a leader to protect their flock.</note>, prevents those he knows to be perverse from deceiving his subjects. 

Indeed, some of these birds have a white color, while others are grey <note>original: *cinereum*; literally "ash-colored."</note>. Both colors, however, are interpreted in a positive light: if purity <note>original: *mundicia*</note> is represented by white, then repentance <note>original: *penitencia*</note> is represented by grey. For those who repent and those who live purely are part of the same family. Therefore, the color of the heron and its way of life provide an example of salvation to those in religious orders <note>original: *religiosis*; this refers to people who have taken monastic vows.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the heron (ardea) as an allegory for the "just man" or the monk, interpreting its high flight, nesting habits, and plumage as symbols of spiritual focus, protection of the vulnerable, and the virtues of purity and repentance.</summary>
<keywords>heron, ardea, tantalum, allegory, purity, repentance, monastic life, storms, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the heron (ardea) to a new chapter regarding the swan. It begins with the etymological origins of the bird's names in Greek and Latin, describes its physical characteristics and musical reputation, and concludes with a moral allegory about hypocrisy.</meta>

# The Swan

**O**lor <note>original: *Olor*; the Latin term for swan, often used interchangeably with *cygnus* in medieval texts.</note> is the bird that the Greeks call the **swan** <note>original: *cignus*</note>. It is called an *olor* because it is entirely white in its feathers; indeed, no one recalls ever seeing a black swan. For in Greek, the word for "whole" or "entire" is *olon* <note>original: *Olon*; referring to the Greek *holos* (ὅλος), meaning whole/all.</note>. 

The term **swan** <note>original: *Cignus*</note>, however, is derived from "singing" <note>original: *a canendo*; a common medieval etymology linking *cygnus* to the Latin *canere* (to sing).</note>, because it pours forth the sweetness of song in modulated tones. It is said to sing so sweetly because it possesses a long and curved neck; it is necessary that the voice, struggling through a long and winding path, should yield various musical modulations. 

It is told that in the **Hyperborean** <note>original: *hisperoreis*; a misspelling of *Hyperboreis*, referring to the mythical far-northern regions.</note> regions, when the **cithara-players** <note>original: *cytharedis*; performers on the cithara, a stringed instrument like a lyre or harp.</note> begin to sing, many swans fly toward them and join in the music, singing along in perfect time. 

Now, *olor* is the Latin name, while the Greeks say *cygnus*. Sailors claim that this bird provides them with a good **omen** <note>original: *pnosticon*; a corruption of the Greek-derived *prognosticon*, a sign or prediction.</note>. As the poet **Aemilianus** <note>Likely referring to a classical or late antique poet; bestiaries often cited authorities like Isidore of Seville or various poets to lend weight to their descriptions.</note> says: 

-> "The swan is always the most joyful bird in omens; sailors desire it because it does not drown in the waves." <-

**The Moral Meaning**
In a moral sense, the swan, snowy in its feathers, signifies the pretense of **hypocrisy** <note>original: *simulationis*; the act of feigning or pretending to be something one is not.</note>, by which the "black flesh" <note>A common medieval metaphor for the sinful nature or the corrupted body hidden beneath a holy exterior.</note> is covered. Thus, it hides the sin of the flesh through a fair outward appearance.

<summary>This page describes the swan (olor/cygnus), explaining its name through its white feathers and its legendary singing voice. It concludes with a moral warning, comparing the swan's white feathers over dark skin to a hypocrite hiding sin behind a virtuous appearance.</summary>

<keywords>swan, olor, cygnus, bird, singing, music, Hyperborea, omens, sailors, hypocrisy, allegory, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral and allegorical interpretation of the swan's behavior, transitioning from its physical description to its symbolic meaning regarding pride, earthly pleasures, and the final judgment of the wealthy.</meta>

While the **swan** <note>original: *Cignus*</note> swims in the river, it carries its neck and head erect, which represents how the proud are drawn along by **transitory things** <note>original: *reb[us] tr[a]nsitoriis*; referring to worldly goods that do not last.</note>. He even rejoices <note>original: *glat[ur]*, likely a contraction for *gloriatur*</note> for a time in the possession of these fleeting things. 

It is said that in the **Hyperborean** <note>original: *ypboreis*; referring to a mythical land of perpetual sunshine in the far north.</note> regions, when the **cithara-players** <note>original: *cytharedis*; performers on an ancient stringed instrument.</note> sing, many swans fly there and sing along skillfully in the same manner. By these are meant those who gape after pleasures with all their desires, and who, flying toward them, harmonize with the voluptuous. 

But it is also claimed that in its final moments, when the swan **is dying** <note>original: *morit[ur]*</note>, it sings with a very sweet sound. In the same way, the proud man departs from this life; he is still delighted by the sweetness of the present world. And as he dies, he brings back to memory the evil things he has done. 

When, however, the snowy plumage of the swan is stripped away and it is placed upon a **spit** <note>original: *ueru*; a metal or wooden rod used for roasting meat over a fire.</note>, it is roasted at the fire. Likewise, when the proud rich man dies and is stripped of his **worldly glory** <note>original: *mūdana gl[ori]a*</note>, he will be tortured by torments as he descends into the flames of **hell** <note>original: *inferni*</note>. That which he was accustomed to seek while alive now fails him; as he descends into the abyss, he becomes **food for the fire** <note>original: *cibus ignis*</note>.

<summary>This page uses the swan’s upright posture and its legendary "swan song" as allegories for human pride and the transition from worldly pleasure to eternal punishment. It concludes with a vivid warning that the wealthy and proud will be stripped of their "plumage" and roasted in the fires of hell.</summary>

<keywords>swan, pride, Hyperboreans, swan song, worldly glory, hell, judgment, allegory, cithara</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the swan to describe the ibis, contrasting its lowly, scavenging nature with the spiritual duties of a Christian, before concluding with the importance of the cross and prayer, using the biblical figure of Moses as an example.</meta>

There is a bird called the **ibis** <note>original: *ybis*</note>, which cleans out its bowels with its own beak <note>This refers to a common medieval belief that the ibis gave itself enemas with water using its long, curved beak.</note>. This bird feeds on the eggs of serpents and on dead carcasses, and from these it carries back a most pleasing meal to its chicks. For it is afraid to enter the water, as it does not know the nature of swimming; instead, it wanders along the shore day and night, searching for either small dead fish or any carcasses that have been cast out from the water. 

This represents carnal men who consume **deadly deeds** <note>original: *mortiferis opibus*; literally "death-bearing works" or "riches," suggesting those who find sustenance in sin or worldly vanity.</note> as if they were food; through these, their wretched souls are sent to punishment. 

You, however, O Christian, who have been born of water and the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: *spiritu sancto*</note>, should enter into the spiritual waters of God’s mysteries. From there, take for yourself the purest foods, which the **Apostle** <note>Referring to St. Paul in the Book of Galatians 5:22-23.</note> enumerates, saying: “But the fruit of the Spirit is **charity** <note>original: *caritas*; the highest form of Christian love.</note>, joy, peace, patience, long-suffering,” and the rest. 

Unless the sun and moon extend their rays, they do not shine. Unless birds extend their wings, they cannot fly. So it is with you, O man: if you do not fortify yourself with the **sign of the cross** <note>original: *signo crucis*</note> and extend the twin wings of love <note>A common medieval trope referring to the "double love" of God and neighbor.</note>, you will be unable to cross through the storms of this world to the peaceful harbor of the heavenly homeland. Finally, when **Moses** <note>original: *Moyses*; referencing the battle against the Amalekites in Exodus 17.</note> raised his hands, **Israel** prevailed; but when he let down his hands, **Amalek** prevailed.

<summary>This page describes the ibis as a scavenger that fears the water, using it as an allegory for "carnal men" who avoid spiritual depth, while urging Christians to embrace the "wings of love" and the sign of the cross.</summary>
<keywords>ibis, allegory, charity, Moses, Israel, Amalek, Holy Spirit, Bestiary, Sign of the Cross</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the moralizing discussion on the ibis (a scavenger representing carnal humans) to a new chapter describing the ostrich, focusing on its physical appearance and its unique nesting habits linked to the movement of the stars.</meta>

There is an animal which is called the **ostrich** <note>original: *assida*; likely derived from the Hebrew word *hasidah*, which can refer to a stork or an ostrich.</note>, which the Greeks call the **sparrow-camel** <note>original: *stratocamelon*; a Latinization of the Greek *strouthiokamelos*, reflecting the bird's massive size and camel-like features.</note>. The Latins, however, call it the **ostrich** <note>original: *struccionem*</note>. It indeed has wings, but it does not fly; its feet are like those of a camel. 

When the time comes for it to lay its eggs, it lifts its eyes toward the heavens and watches to see if that star which is called **Virgilia** <note>original: *uirgilia*; the Pleiades star cluster, whose rising signaled the start of summer to ancient and medieval observers.</note> appears. For it does not lay its eggs until that star rises. When, around the month of **June**, it sees that star, it digs into the earth to a place where it may lay its eggs, and covers them with sand. When it leaves that place, it immediately forgets them and never returns to its eggs. Indeed, the calmness and temperature of the air seem to provide for this, so that in the summer, the heated sand hatches the eggs and brings forth the chicks. If, therefore, the ostrich recognizes 

<summary>This page introduces the ostrich, describing it as a flightless bird with camel-like feet that waits for the rising of the Pleiades in June to lay its eggs in the sand. The text highlights the medieval belief that the ostrich forgets its offspring, leaving the summer heat to incubate them.</summary>
<keywords>ostrich, assida, sparrow-camel, Pleiades, Virgilia, nesting habits, June, animal behavior, Bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralization of the ostrich—which leaves its eggs to follow the stars—and transitions into the entry for the coot (fulica), a bird praised for its stability and refusal to wander.</meta>

... its time and forgets its offspring, and setting aside earthly things, follows heavenly things. How much more, O man, must you strive toward the prize <note>original: *brauium*; a term derived from the Greek *brabeion*, referring to the prize awarded to a winner in an athletic contest, used here in a spiritual sense regarding salvation.</note> of the heavenly calling, for whose sake God was made man, so that He might snatch you from the power of darkness and place you with the princes of His people in the kingdom of His glory.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature within a thick red border depicting a fulica (coot). The bird is dark grey with a prominent white beak and frontal shield, perched on stylized green and turquoise foliage. The background consists of a blue diaper-patterned field with silver/white quatrefoil motifs.</image-desc>

The **coot** <term>fulica: a water bird, likely the Eurasian coot (*Fulica atra*), known for its dark plumage and distinctive white "shield" on its forehead.</term> is a bird that is quite intelligent and a most prudent animal. It feeds on carcasses <note>The Latin here reads *cadauerē uescitur* (feeds on carrion), although in the broader Bestiary tradition, the coot is usually praised for *not* eating carrion, contrasting it with the "unclean" ibis. The scribe may have omitted a "not" (*non*).</note>, nor does it fly off to other places or wander about; rather, it stays and remains in one place until the end, and there it finds its food and takes its rest. 

In this same way, let every faithful soul preserve themselves and live. Let them not fly about, wandering here and there through various paths as **heretics** do. Let them not be delighted by worldly desires and pleasures, but let them always keep themselves in one place and find rest in the **Catholic Church** <note>original: *ecclia catholica*</note>, where the Lord makes those of one mind dwell together in a house <note>A reference to Psalm 68:6: "God sets the lonely in families" or "makes the solitary to dwell in a house."</note>. There, let them have their daily sustenance—that is, the **bread of immortality** and for drink the **precious blood of Christ** <note>original: *xpi*; a standard medieval abbreviation for *Christi* using the Greek letters Chi and Rho.</note>—refreshing themselves with the word of the Lord <note>original: *eloquū dñi*</note>, which is sweeter than honey and the honeycomb.

<summary>This page concludes the entry on the ostrich as a model for seeking heavenly things and introduces the coot as a symbol of the faithful Christian who remains steadfast within the Church.</summary>
<keywords>ostrich, coot, fulica, stability, Catholic Church, Eucharist, bread of immortality, heretics, animal symbolism, Bestiary</keywords>

### The Jay

The **jay** <term>graculus: a bird typically identified in medieval bestiaries as a jackdaw, chough, or jay, known for its loud, mimicking call.</term> is named for its **chattering** <note>original: *garrulitate*; the author is using a common medieval etymological argument, linking the name *graculus* to the word for talkativeness.</note>, and not, as some would have it, because they fly in **flocks** <note>original: *gregatim*; a play on words where some suggested *graculus* came from *grege* (flock).</note>. For it is manifest that they are named for the former reason. 

Indeed, it is a most loquacious species, and annoying with its many voices. This can signify either the vain loquacity of **philosophers** or the harmful wordiness of **heretics**. 

Furthermore, another thing can be said regarding the nature of the jay. For the jay signifies the talkative and the **gluttonous**. Those who are devoted to gluttony freely spread rumors after their meals and lend their ears to detraction <note>original: *detractioni*; in this context, it refers specifically to slandering or "tearing down" the reputation of others through gossip.</note>. 

The jay lives in the woods, passing from one tree to another with its chattering. Just so, a talkative man does not cease to tell others the shameful things he knows about those with whom he lives. When the jay perceives that someone is aware of something, it chatters; and when it discovers people in hiding, it acts similarly. This is because a talkative man does not only slander those in the secular world, but also those whom the places of **religion** <note>original: *religionis*; likely referring to those in "religious life," such as monks or nuns living in cloistered or "hidden" places.</note> keep concealed. The jay, once caught, is shut in a snare and kept so that it might [learn] articulate voi... <note>The text breaks off here at *articulata vo-* (likely *voce*), referring to the bird's ability to mimic human speech when caged.</note>

<summary>This page describes the jay, using its noisy and imitative nature as a metaphor for the empty talk of philosophers, the dangerous rhetoric of heretics, and the social malice of gossips and gluttons.</summary>
<keywords>jay, graculus, gossip, heretics, gluttony, garrulity, medieval etymology, moral allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moralization of the jay (graculus). The previous page ended with the idea that the talkative man "does not cease to tell" stories. This page draws a parallel between training a bird to speak and the training of a layman entering the monastery, while warning against certain professions that struggle with the monastic vow of stability.</meta>

...should be taught to speak. Similarly, when any **secular** <term>secularis: a person living in the "world" rather than under a monastic rule.</term> person comes to **conversion** <note>original: *conversionem*; here meaning the specific act of entering the religious life of a monastery.</note>, he should learn the words of religion, so that the tongue of the bird might speak the words of a man. He who was accustomed to speaking in a disordered way should thereafter become accustomed to speaking in an **orderly** fashion. 

However, when it happens that a jay which was kept caged escapes, the bird that was previously a jay cries out even more after its departure. In the same way, a talkative man, when he embraces the religious life, abandons his [former] tongue. But if by chance, having cast off the **habit** <note>original: *habitu*; the distinctive clothing of a monk, representing his vows.</note>, he should go back out into the world, he turns the good language of religion toward a bad end by slandering and chattering. 

Therefore, let the **teacher** <note>original: *doctor*; likely referring to the Master of Novices or the Abbot who instructs newcomers.</note> take heed when he must receive someone: at least let him first test him through **living together** <note>original: *cohabitacione*; a period of probation or trial to see if the candidate is fit for the community.</note>. I learned from a certain religious and discreet man that there are certain types of men who can hardly be kept in an orderly fashion within religious life. 

If you ask who these are so that they may be avoided, they are: **painters**, **physicians**, **performers** <note>original: *ioculatores*; entertainers such as jesters, minstrels, or acrobats.</note>, and certain others who are accustomed to wandering through various regions. Men of this kind can hardly be **stable** <term>stabilitas: the monastic "vow of stability," requiring a monk to remain in his specific monastery for life.</term>. 

The art of the painter is particularly **detestable** <note>original: *detestabilis*; in this context, the author does not mean the art itself is evil, but rather "avoidable" or "problematic" because it encourages a monk to travel and break his vow of stability.</note>. For when a painter has painted the church, the **chapter house** <note>original: *capitulum*; the room where the monks meet for daily business and reading the Rule.</note>, the **refectory** <note>original: *refectorium*; the dining hall.</note>, or other workshops, if it is granted to him and he is requested by someone, he moves on to another monastery for the sake of painting. While he paints works on a wall, his vain mind holds them in...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of training a bird to speak to describe the discipline required of new monks, while warning that certain professions—like painters and doctors—are naturally prone to wandering and struggle with monastic stability.</summary>
<keywords>monastic conversion, stability, jay, painters, physicians, performers, discipline, gossip</keywords>

But it is not profitable for a religious man to speak things that are not true. He promises profit to his church if he goes to the sick man, but he is silent about the scandal and the damage to his own soul.

The **halcyon** <term>halcyon: a legendary bird, often identified with the kingfisher, said to nest at sea during the winter solstice.</term>

brood is achieved in so few days.

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Halcyon bird's miraculous nesting period before transitioning into the description and symbolism of the Phoenix. It connects the natural (or legendary) behavior of animals to Christian theology, a hallmark of the medieval Bestiary tradition.</meta>

Sailors observe these days of the greatest serenity, which they also call the **halcyon days** <note>original: *alcionidas*</note>; during this time, no motion of a stormy tempest is feared.

->[Illustration: A Phoenix, a large gray-brown bird, stands upon a funeral pyre made of bundled twigs. It is surrounded by stylized green and blue trees against a blue and gold background.]<-

The **Phoenix** is a bird of Arabia, so named because it has a Phoenician <term>Phoenician: a deep purple-red color, derived from the Greek *phoinix*</term> color, or because it is singular and unique in the whole world. Living for over five hundred years, when it perceives that it has grown old, it builds a funeral pyre for itself from gathered twigs of aromatic spices. Turning toward the rays of the sun, it fans a voluntary fire for itself with the flapping of its wings. It burns itself, and afterward, on the third day, a new bird rises from its ashes.

Our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: *the xpc*; the scribe uses a Greek-derived abbreviation for 'God Christ'</note> bears the likeness of this bird, He who says: "I have the power to lay down my life and to take it up again" <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 10:18</note>. If, therefore, the phoenix has the power to put itself to death and bring itself back to life, why should foolish men be angry at the Word of God? He is the true Son of God, who says, "I have the power," etc. For our Savior descended from heaven; He filled His wings with the sweet scents of the New and Old Testaments, and in...

<summary>This page concludes the description of the calm "halcyon days" and introduces the Phoenix, describing its mythical self-immolation and rebirth after 500 years. The text explicitly links the Phoenix to the Resurrection of Christ, using the bird's ability to "take up its life again" as a defense of Christian doctrine.</summary>

<keywords>Halcyon days, Phoenix, Arabia, Resurrection, Jesus Christ, aromatic spices, funeral pyre, symbolism, Bestiary</keywords>

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature painting with a double border of blue and red, set against a background of burnished gold leaf. The bird called the **Phoenix** is said to live in the regions of Arabia, and to reach a great age, living for up to five hundred years. When it perceives that the end of its life is approaching, it makes for itself a casket <note>original: *thecam*; here referring to a funerary chest or nest</note> of frankincense and myrrh and other aromatic spices, into which, once the time of its life is fulfilled, it enters and dies. From the moisture of its flesh a worm <note>original: *vermis*; in medieval lore, the new phoenix often began as a small larva or worm within the remains of the old bird</note> arises, which gradually grows and, in the course of the appointed time, puts on the plumage of wings and is restored to the appearance and form of the previous bird. Therefore, let this bird teach us, by its own example, to believe in the resurrection; for without a previous example and without the instruction of precedents, it restores for itself the signs of resurrection. And this unique bird...

<summary>This page concludes the comparison of Christ's sacrifice to the Phoenix and describes the bird's unique lifecycle, where it dies in a nest of spices and is reborn from a worm to its original form.</summary>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegory of the phoenix, transitioning from the physical description of the bird's death in the previous page to an exhortation for the reader to build their own spiritual "casket" of faith and virtue, using Saint Paul as the ultimate example of a "good phoenix."</meta>

...are for the sake of humans, and not humans for the sake of the bird. Let it, therefore, be an example to us that the author and creator of birds, not allowing His own works <note>original: *impetuum*; likely a scribal variation of *impetus* or *opera*, referring to God's creative acts</note> to perish, willed for the bird to be restored by rising again from its own seed. Who, then, announces the day of death to this bird, so that it might make for itself a casket <note>original: *thecam*; here a funerary nest or chest</note> and fill it with good scents, and enter into it, and die there, so that by these pleasing odors the stench of death might be abolished? You also, O man, make for yourself a casket; and stripping off the "old man" with his deeds, put on the "new man" <note>A reference to the Pauline doctrine in Colossians 3:9-10 regarding spiritual renewal</note>. Your casket and your sheath is Christ, who covers you and hides you in the evil day. Do you wish to know the value of this casket? "In my quiver," He says, "I have protected him" <note>original: *pharetra... protexi*; a reference to Isaiah 49:2</note>. 

Therefore, your casket is faith; fill it with the good scents of your virtues—that is, of chastity, mercy, and justice—and enter safely into those very depths of faith, which are fragrant with the sweet odor of excellent deeds. May the end of this life find you clothed in that faith, so that your bones may grow strong <note>original: *pinguescere*; literally "to grow fat" or "be enriched"</note> and be like a well-watered garden <note>original: *ortus ebrius*; literally a "drunken garden," a common medieval idiom for a lush, overflowing garden</note> whose seeds quickly spring up. Know, therefore, the day of your death, just as Paul knew it, who said:

> I have fought the good 
> fight, I have finished the race, I have 
> kept the faith. There is laid up for 
> me a crown of righteousness. <note>2 Timothy 4:7-8</note>

He entered, therefore, into his casket like a good **phoenix**, because he was filled with the good scent of martyrdom.

<meta>This page continues the bestiary's descriptions of avian nature, transitioning from the glowing birds of the Hercynian Forest to the hoopoe—a symbol of filial devotion—and beginning the entry on the pelican.</meta>

<margin>Note well <note>original: "n. v.", likely an abbreviation for *nota vale* or *nota vide*.</note></margin>
The bird called the **hoopoe** <note>original: *epopus*; modern scientific name *Upupa epops*.</note> when it sees that its parents have grown old and their eyes have become dim: it plucks out their feathers and licks their eyes and warms them, and its parents are rejuvenated. It is as if it is saying to its parents: "Just as you labored while nourishing me, I do the same for you." 

If, however, irrational animals do this for one another, how much more should rational humans return the mutual care to their parents. And the Law says: "Whoever curses father or mother, let him die the death" <note>A reference to the biblical commandment found in Exodus 21:17 and Leviticus 20:9.</note> and he is as one who kills his father <term>parricide<gloss>original: *paricida*</gloss></term> or his mother <term>matricide<gloss>original: *matricida*</gloss></term>.

The **pelican** is an Egyptian bird living in the solitude of the Nile River, from which it also took its name. For in Egypt it is called *canapos* <note>The author attempts an etymological link between the bird and the Egyptian city or region of Canopus.</note>.

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on the pelican, moving from its geographical origins in Egypt to its famous—and gruesome—allegorical behavior toward its young. It then introduces the owl, beginning with the smaller night-owl.</meta>

It is an exceeding lover of its children. For when it has fathered chicks and they have begun to grow, they strike their parents in the face. But the parents, striking back, kill them. On the third day, however, their mother, out of love <note>original: *p[er] am-ent*, likely a contraction for *per amorem* (through love)</note>, opens her side and lies over her chicks, and pours out blood over the bodies of the dead, and thus she raises them from the dead. 

So our Lord Jesus Christ, the author and founder of every creature, gave birth to us, and when we did not exist, He made us. We, on the contrary, struck Him in the face, as He says through the prophet Isaiah: "I have brought up and exalted sons, but they have despised me" <note>original: *filios genui et exaltavi. ipsi autem spreverunt me*. A reference to Isaiah 1:2. The manuscript uses *genui* (begot/bore) where the standard Vulgate uses *enutrivi* (nourished).</note>. Then we struck Him when we served the creature rather than the Creator. For this reason, He ascended into the heights of the cross, and having suffered, from His side came forth blood and water for our salvation and eternal life.

# The Night-Owl

The **night-owl** <note>original: *noctua*; referring to the Little Owl or a similar small owl species.</note> is so called because it flies at night with a cry <note>original: *cantu*, literally "song" or "chant."</note> and by day it cannot see. For once the brightness of the sun has risen, its vision is dulled. 

The night-owl, however, is not the same as the **eagle-owl** <note>original: *bubo*; a larger species of owl.</note>, for the eagle-owl is larger. The **night-raven** <note>original: *nicticorax*; from the Greek for "night crow," often used in medieval texts to describe owls.</note> is this same night-owl because it loves the night. For it is a light-shunning bird and does not endure seeing the sun.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the pelican, which symbolizes Christ’s sacrifice and resurrection, and introduces the owl as a bird that shuns the light of day.</summary>
<keywords>Pelican, Owl, Jesus Christ, Isaiah, Sacrifice, Resurrection, Night-Owl, Eagle-Owl, Night-Raven, Allegory</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing allegory of the night-owl (noctua) begun on the previous page, transitioning from its rejection of light to a new entry on the larger Eagle-owl (bubo).</meta>

This bird <note>The night-owl (noctua), described on the preceding page.</note> bears the figure of the Jews who, when the Lord Savior came to save them, rejected Him, saying: "We have no king but Caesar" <note>original: *Non habemus regem nisi cesarem* (John 19:15)</note>; and they loved darkness more than light. Then the Lord turned Himself toward us Gentiles and enlightened us as we sat in darkness and the shadow of death <note>original: *sedentes in tenebris & umb[ris] mortis* (Luke 1:79)</note>. Concerning us, it is said: "A people whom I have not known has served me" <note>original: *Pop[ulu]s quem n[on] cognovi servivit m[ihi]* (Psalm 18:43)</note>. And in another prophet: "I will call my people 'not my people'" <note>original: *Vocabo plebem mea[m] n[on] plebem mea[m]* (Hosea 2:23)</note>. And regarding the Jewish people: "But the children [of the kingdom shall be cast out]" and so forth <note>original: *filii autem. & cetera.* (referring to Matthew 8:12).</note>.

<image-desc>A rectangular illuminated miniature with a thick red border containing a gold inner frame. The background is a deep blue, decorated with a regular pattern of small white and gold quatrefoil flowers. In the center, a large owl (Bubo) is depicted in shades of brown and tan, facing forward with large, detailed eyes. It perches on a stylized branch with green and blue foliage. Two smaller birds are present: one on the left in flight toward the owl, and another on the right, resembling a magpie, perched on a branch and looking back at the owl. This scene illustrates the "mobbing" behavior of smaller birds toward owls, a common motif in medieval bestiaries.</image-desc>

# The Eagle-Owl

The **Eagle-owl** <note>original: *Bubo*. In medieval bestiaries, the *bubo* (large eagle-owl) was usually distinguished from the *noctua* (small night-owl).</note> has a name composed from the sound of its voice. It is a wild bird, heavily laden with feathers and always gripped by a heavy laziness. It stays in tombs day and night, always dwelling in caverns. Regarding this bird, Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso, the Roman poet. Bestiaries frequently cited classical authors to provide authority to their descriptions.</note> says:

-> ...and let it be a foul bird, <-
-> a messenger of coming grief. <-
-> The sluggish owl, <-
-> a dire omen for mortals. <-
<note>original: *fedamq[ue] sit volucris venturi nuncia luctus. Ignavus bubo dirum mortalib[u]z omen.* From Ovid's Metamorphoses, Book 5, lines 549–550.</note>

Finally, among the augurs <term>Augurs: Ancient Roman religious officials who observed the flight and cries of birds to interpret the will of the gods.</term>, it is said to portend evil. The **Eagle-owl** signifies those devoted to the darkness of sin and fleeing from the light of righteousness. For this reason, among the unclean... <note>The text here begins to refer to the Mosaic Law in Leviticus 11, which classifies the owl as an "unclean" animal forbidden for consumption.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a Christian allegory comparing the night-owl's preference for darkness to the Jewish rejection of Christ, then introduces the Eagle-owl (bubo) as a symbol of sloth and sin, citing the poet Ovid.</summary>
<keywords>Eagle-owl, Bubo, Ovid, Metamorphoses, Allegory, Jews and Gentiles, Medieval Bestiary, Augury, Sin, Darkness vs Light</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing entry for the Eagle-owl (bubo) from the previous page. The text explains why this bird is considered "unclean" in biblical law and how its physical habits represent the behavior of sinners.</meta>

is counted among the animals in Leviticus <note>original: *animalia in leuitico deputatur*. Referring to the list of "unclean" birds in Leviticus 11:16.</note>. From this, we can understand any sinner through the **eagle-owl**. The **eagle-owl** <note>original: *bubo*</note> is named from the sound of its voice: because "out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks" <note>original: *ex habundantia cordis os loquitur* (Matthew 12:34)</note>. For what it thinks in its mind, it brings forth with its mouth. It is said to be a foul bird because the place where it lives is defiled with dung: because the sinner dishonors those with whom he lives by the example of his wicked deeds.

The bird is weighed down with feathers, that is, by the excess of the flesh and the levity of the mind, and is indeed held back by heavy sloth. Sinners are rightly held by heavy sloth, because they <unclear>are</unclear> idle and lazy in doing good. Day and night they dwell in the tomb, because sinners delight in sin, which is the stench of human flesh. It also lives in caves and does not come out through confession, but hates the light of truth. 

When seen by other birds, it is betrayed by their loud cries and is harassed by great attacks. For if a sinner comes into the light of recognition, he provides a great source of mockery for those who do well. And when a sinner is clearly caught, he hears rebukes from others. They pluck his feathers and tear at him with their beaks because those who do well rebuke the carnal acts of sinners and condemn their excess. Unhappy, therefore, is the **eagle-owl** who performs those things we have described.
