<meta>The previous page concluded the entry on the Eagle-owl (bubo), interpreting its nocturnal and "unclean" habits as symbols of a sinner's behavior. This page begins a new entry on the Sirens, shifting from a bird of the night to creatures of the sea that lead men to their doom.</meta>

**Sirens**

**Sirens**, says the *Physiologus* <note>original: *phisiologus*. An ancient Greek text, later translated into Latin, which provided the foundation for medieval bestiaries by combining animal lore with moral lessons.</note>, are monstrous creatures <note>original: *monstrifera sunt animalia*</note> that have the appearance of humans from the head down to the navel. Their lower parts, however, down to the feet, they have the form of birds <note>original: *uolantium*, literally "flying things". Although the text describes them as bird-like—following the classical tradition of Harpies—the accompanying illustration depicts them as fish-tailed mermaids, a common visual variation in the Middle Ages.</note>. Through their musical nature, they produce a song of the sweetest melody, so that by the sweetness of their voice, they soothe the hearing of men sailing from afar and draw them toward themselves. With the excessive sweetness of their rhythm <note>original: *modulatione*</note>, they strike the ears and senses of the sailors and lull them into sleep. 

Then at last, when they see the men in a deep sleep, they suddenly attack and tear their flesh to pieces. In this way, by the persuasion of their voice, they deceive and kill ignorant and unwary men. In this way also they dece... <note>The text breaks off mid-word (*decipiunt*) at the end of the page; the following page likely begins the moral allegory, comparing the Sirens' song to the worldly temptations that lure the soul into spiritual slumber.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance and deceptive nature of Sirens, who use beautiful music to lure sailors into a deadly sleep. It follows the medieval bestiary tradition of using "natural history" to warn against the dangers of sensory temptation.</summary>

<keywords>Sirens, Physiologus, monsters, music, temptation, sailors, deception, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegory of the Sirens from the previous page before introducing a new entry on the Partridge.</meta>

...ceived <note>original: *[deci]piuntur*. This completes the word from the previous page, referring to the victims of the Sirens.</note> are those who delight in luxuries, pomps, and pleasures; being unstrung by various musical melodies of comedies and tragedies, they lose all the vigor of their mind as if in a heavy sleep. Then, they are suddenly made the most daring prey of their enemies.

<margin>The **Partridge** <note>original: *perdix*. In medieval etymology, the name was believed to be derived from the sound of its cry.</note> takes its name from its voice. It is a deceitful and unclean bird. For the male rises up against the male, and headlong lust forgets its sex. So much so...</margin>

...moreover, it is fraudulent: so much so that one bird steals the eggs of another. However, fraud bears no fruit. For when the chicks <note>original: *pulli*</note> hear the voice of the mother who truly produced them, through a certain natural instinct, they leave the one who brooded over them and return to her who bore them.

In this, the **devil** <note>original: *diabolus*</note> has imitated this example, as he strives to snatch away the offspring of the eternal Creator; and if he can gather together any who are foolish and lacking in the strength of mental vigor, he nurtures them with bodily temptations. But when the voice of **Christ** <note>original: *xp̃i*; a common medieval abbreviation using the Greek letters Chi and Rho</note> is heard by them, they take up spiritual wings, providently fly away, and entrust themselves to Christ.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the Sirens as symbols of worldly temptation and introduces the Partridge, whose habit of stealing eggs is compared to the Devil stealing souls that eventually return to Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Sirens, Partridge, perdix, Devil, Christ, spiritual awakening, medieval etymology, animal allegory</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Partridge's natural habits, following the moral allegory on the previous page which compared the bird's deceitful nature to that of the Devil.</meta>

Partridges build their nests <note>original: *nidificantur*</note> with clever fortification. For they line their shelters with thorny shrubs so that they may ward off animals that would attack them.

<image-desc>A rectangular medieval miniature set within an orange-red border decorated with a white wavy line. The background features a blue field with a fine white lattice or grid pattern. In the center is a smaller rectangular gold panel. Within this panel, a bird (a partridge) with dark grey and brown plumage is perched on the rim of its nest. The nest is brown and rounded, containing five white eggs, and is situated on top of stylized green leaves.</image-desc>

She stealthily hides her eggs <note>original: *oua sua*</note> in the dust of the fields and returns to them secretly so there is no sign of the location. Frequent human company tires her out. Often, the females hide their brood so they might deceive the males, who very often harm the young while being too impatient in their mating <note>original: *adulantes*; here referring to aggressive or over-eager courting behavior that could crush eggs or chicks</note>. They fight over mating, and it is believed that the losers must submit to the role of the female. Their desire is so easily stirred that if the wind blows from the direction of the males, the females become pregnant by the scent. 

Then, if any person approaches them in remote countrysides, the mothers come out and offer themselves voluntarily to those coming; with a simulated weakness of either their feet or their wings—as if they could be captured immediately—they feign a slower pace <note>original: *gressus*</note>. With this deception <note>original: *mendacio*</note>, they trick those they meet so that they might lead them further away from their chicks. Nor is their diligent care in guarding themselves unrewarded. When they sense they have been spotted, lying on their backs, they lift up clods of earth <note>original: *glebulas*</note> with their feet, by the cover of which they so cleverly protect...

<summary>This page details the natural (and mythological) behaviors of the partridge, including its defensive nesting habits, its ability to conceive via the wind, and its famous "broken-wing" display used to lure predators away from its young.</summary>
<keywords>Partridge, perdix, nesting, animal behavior, medieval mythology, deception, parental care</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page concludes the entry on the Partridge and begins the entries for the Magpie (Pica) and the Woodpecker (Picus). It continues the theme of animal behaviors being used as allegories for human speech and religious error.</meta>

...they struggle <note>original: *gunant*, likely a fragment of *pugnant* (they fight)</note> so that they might remain hidden when detected.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature set within a square frame with an orange border decorated with white patterns. The miniature depicts a magpie (pica) with black and white feathers and a long tail, perched on a stylized green vine. The background of the miniature is divided: the left side is burnished gold leaf, and the right side is blue with delicate gold scrolling patterns.</image-desc>

The **Magpie** <note>original: *Pica*</note> is so called because it expresses words with a distinctness of voice <note>original: *in discrimine uocis*</note> just like a human. For, hanging from the branches of trees, they sound out with a persistent chatter <note>original: *importuna garrulitate*</note>; and even if they cannot unfurl their tongues into actual speech, they nevertheless imitate the sound of the human voice. Concerning this bird, someone appropriately said:

> A chattering magpie, I surely greet you, master, with my voice. If you did not see me, you would deny that I was a bird. <note>This couplet is from the Roman poet Martial's *Epigrams* (14.76). Medieval authors often cited classical poets to authorize their natural histories.</note>

The **Woodpecker** <term>Picus<note>The Latin name for the woodpecker; in Roman mythology, Picus was a king and an augur who was turned into a bird by the sorceress Circe.</note></term> took its name from Picus, the son of Saturn, because he used this bird in taking auspices <note>original: *in auspiciis*; the ancient Roman practice of divining the future by observing the flight or calls of birds.</note>. For they say that this bird possesses something divine, evidenced by this: that in whatever tree it has built its nest, a nail or any other object driven into the wood cannot remain there for long, but must immediately fall out when the bird settles there.

But let everyone think what they wish about this matter. For the sound of its voice can signify the talkativeness of **heretics** or of **philosophers**, just as we said above regarding the **jackdaw** <note>original: *graculo*; earlier in the bestiary, the jackdaw is often a symbol of empty, worldly vanity or deceptive rhetoric.</note>.

<vocab>Pica: Magpie, Picus: Woodpecker, Saturni filio: Son of Saturn, hereticorum: of heretics (those holding beliefs contrary to the Church), philosophonum: of philosophers (here used to mean those who rely on worldly logic rather than faith), graculo: jackdaw</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the magpie's ability to mimic human speech and the woodpecker's legendary association with Roman augury and the power to dislodge nails from trees. Both birds are used as allegories for the "empty chatter" of heretics and worldly philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>magpie, woodpecker, mimicry, Roman mythology, Picus, Saturn, heretics, philosophers, symbolism, augury</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the Woodpecker (Picus) to a new section on the Hawk (Accipiter), continuing the medieval tradition of using animal behavior to illustrate moral and spiritual lessons.</meta>

<image-desc>A framed rectangular miniature at the top of the page depicts a scene of falconry and bird training. On the left, a woman in a long blue gown and white wimple holds a large hawk perched on her gloved hand. In the center, a man in a blue tunic and hood and a small child (perhaps a young apprentice) walk toward a stylized green tree and a body of water where three dark ducks or hawks are situated. The man holds a circular disk and a stick. The background is composed of a reddish panel decorated with white floral or stellar dots, set against a gold-leaf ground. The entire scene is enclosed in a decorative red, white, and blue border.</image-desc>

The **Hawk** <term>Accipiter<note>The general Latin term for a bird of prey, particularly hawks or falcons. The text emphasizes its small size but fierce nature.</note></term> is a bird armed more by its spirit than by its claws, carrying a strength greater than its small body would suggest. It took its name from "taking" <note>original: *accipiendo*</note>, that is, from "seizing" <note>original: *capiendo*</note>. It is a bird greedy for snatching other birds. Thus it is called a hawk—that is, a "raptor" <note>original: *raptor*, meaning one who seizes or plunders; the root of the modern word 'raptor' used for birds of prey</note>.

Wherefore Saint Paul says: "For you endure it if someone brings you into slavery, if someone devours you" <note>original: *si quis accipit*; quoting 2 Corinthians 11:20. The author is drawing a linguistic parallel between the hawk (accipiter) and the greedy person who "takes" (accipit) from others.</note>. For just as he says "if someone snatches," he also says "if someone takes." 

It is said, however, that the hawk acts "impiously" toward its own chicks. For when it sees that they are able to attempt flight, it provides them with no food; instead, it beats them with its wings and drives them from the nest. It compels the young ones to hunt for prey so that they do not become lazy when they are grown. The hawk takes care lest they grow sluggish in their tender age, lest they be softened by comforts, or wither away in idleness. It ensures they do not learn to expect food rather than to seek it out, and that they do not cast aside the vigor of their nature; thus, the parents diminish their own efforts in feeding them.

<summary>This page describes the hawk (Accipiter), deriving its name from its predatory nature and detailing its "tough love" parenting style, where chicks are forced from the nest to ensure they become strong hunters.</summary>
<keywords>hawk, accipiter, falconry, Saint Paul, animal behavior, parenting, raptor, medieval bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hawk (Accipiter), transitioning from its natural behaviors to its symbolic meaning in Christian theology. It specifically moves from the hawk’s treatment of its young to its role as a metaphor for the "holy man" and the monk in the cloister.</meta>

...so that they are compelled to dare the effort of seizing prey.

The **hawk** <note>original: *accipiter*</note> signifies the **holy man**, as one who is "seizing" the kingdom of God <note>A reference to Matthew 11:12, "the kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent take it by force."</note>. Concerning this, it is written in the Book of **Job**: "Does the hawk grow feathers by your wisdom, spreading its wings toward the south?" <note>Job 39:26. In medieval exegesis, the "south" represents the warmth of the Holy Spirit.</note> That is to say: Have you bestowed understanding upon any chosen person so that, with the **Holy Spirit** blowing, he may expand the wings of his thoughts? Whereby he may cast off the weights of his **old way of life** <term>conversationis<note>In a monastic context, *conversatio* refers to one's conduct, way of life, or the process of monastic conversion.</note></term> and take up the feathers of virtues for a new flight? By this hawk, the **renewed Gentiles** <note>Refers to non-Jewish people who have converted to Christianity, shedding their "old feathers" of paganism.</note> can also be designated.

### On Domestic Hawks
In order that they may grow their feathers better, quiet and fresh places are required. <margin><unclear>note regarding a choice in [illegible]</unclear></margin> These quiet places are the **cloisters** <note>original: *claustra*; the enclosed living quarters of a monastery</note>, in which the wild hawk is placed so that it may become domestic and is kept enclosed. There, it loses its old feathers and takes on new ones. Likewise, any person in a cloister, when stripped of the vices of sin, is adorned with the virtues of the **new man**. 

Nor is the hawk taken out of its enclosure unless it is first strengthened by its recently grown feathers. Once it is firm in its flight and stands upright, it comes out to the trainer's hand. Similarly, if any **convert** <term>conversus<note>In the Middle Ages, a *conversus* was often someone who joined a monastery later in life, or a lay brother.</note></term> goes out from the cloister, it is necessary that he approach the **hand of good work**. Having been sent forth from there, he flies so that he may lift himself with the whole effort of his mind toward the desired heavenly things.

<summary>This page uses the hawk’s molting process (shedding old feathers for new ones in a quiet place) as an allegory for a monk shedding sins and gaining virtues within the quiet of a cloister.</summary>
<keywords>hawk, Job, cloister, Holy Spirit, virtues, monastic life, molting, conversion, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the hawk (accipiter) as a model for the Christian soul and the monk. It expands on the previous page's discussion of the cloister by explaining the specific tools of falconry—the hands of the handler, the perch, and the jesses—as symbols of spiritual discipline and the balance between worldly and eternal concerns.</meta>

The **hawk** <note>original: *accipiter*</note> is accustomed to be carried on the **left hand** so that, when released to catch something, it may fly toward the **right**. "His left hand," it says, "is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me." <note>A quotation from the Song of Solomon 2:6, frequently interpreted by medieval theologians as the soul's relationship with God.</note> The **left** signifies temporal goods <note>Earthly or worldly possessions that are fleeting and "light."</note>; the **right**, truly, signifies eternal things. He who presides over light things—that is, temporal ones—sits on the left. But he who desires eternal things with the full affection of his mind flies toward the right. There, the hawk will catch the **dove**—that is, anyone changed for the better will receive the grace of the **Holy Spirit**.

### The Perch of Rectitude
The hawk’s **perch** <note>original: *pertica*</note> designates for us the rectitude of the **religious life** <term>vita regularis<note>A life governed by a monastic "rule" (regula), such as the Rule of Saint Benedict.</note></term>. This perch is suspended far from the earth, because a life of this kind is separated far from earthly desires. He who firmly holds the statutes of the religious life sits bound upon this perch.

The perch is said to be attached to **two walls**, by which it is supported on either side. These two walls that support the perch are the **active** and **contemplative** lives <note>The "active life" refers to physical work and charity; the "contemplative life" refers to prayer and meditation. In medieval thought, a healthy monastery required both.</note>, which piously and mutually uphold the rectitude of life.

### The Shackles of Discipline
The hawk is accustomed to have **shackles** <note>original: *compedes*; in falconry, these refer to the leather straps or jesses used to restrain the bird.</note> upon its feet, so that it might not fly away whenever it wishes when any opportunity arises. He humbles or binds the feet of the hawk in shackles who suppresses the inclinations of the mind through fear of judgment and the pain of punishment.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of falconry equipment, casting the perch as the monastic rule supported by active and contemplative life, and the jesses as the fear of judgment that restrains sinful impulses.</summary>
<keywords>hawk, allegory, monastic life, active and contemplative life, Holy Spirit, discipline, Song of Solomon, temporal vs eternal</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of falconry equipment as symbols of monastic discipline. The previous page discussed the perch as a symbol of the religious life; this page transitions from the "shackles" of the soul to the "strap" or leash of the hawk, which represents the mortification of the flesh. It then begins a new entry in the bestiary concerning the bat.</meta>

"And they humbled," he said, "his **feet in shackles**." <note>original: *in compedibus*; a reference to Psalm 105:18 regarding the imprisonment of Joseph in Egypt.</note> He humbles the feet of **Joseph** in shackles; for he who brings to mind his present misery and eternal punishment binds the affections of his soul, lest they move forward toward adopted desires.

The **strap** <note>original: *corrigia*; in the context of falconry, this refers to the jesses or leash used to restrain the hawk.</note> by which the bird takes its impulse and is bound is the **mortification of the flesh** <term>mortificatio carnis<note>The practice of self-discipline and abstaining from physical pleasures to strengthen the spirit.</note></term>, through which any convert is held within the **religious life** <note>original: *regulari vita*; a life lived according to a monastic Rule.</note>. Indeed, a strap made from the hide of a dead animal signifies the mortification of the flesh. This strap is not broken, but is only given slack so that the bird may take flight to catch something. Similarly, if any **brother** goes out for the sake of temporal gain <note>Referring to a monk leaving the cloister for necessary business or labor to support the community.</note>, his holy purpose is not broken. But when he returns, he is seen to be rebound more firmly afterward by the same strap with which he was previously bound.

![A square illumination depicting a bat with its dark, membranous wings fully extended. The animal is rendered in grey and black with fine line detailing. It is set against a solid gold leaf background. The miniature is framed by a narrow blue inner border with white highlights and a wider, orange-red outer border.]

The **bat** <note>original: *vespertilio*</note> is a lowly animal that takes its name from the **evening** <note>The Latin *vesper* means evening; medieval authors often used etymology to explain an animal's nature.</note>. It is, however, a winged creature, and likewise a **four-footed animal**; it uses **teeth**, which you would not usually find in other birds. It gives birth like the quadrupeds, not to eggs, but to **living young**. It flies, however, not by any flight of feathers, but supported by the rowing motion of its **membrane**, as if suspended...

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page concludes the entry on the bat, which began on the previous page as a symbol of both lowly nature and communal bond. It then transitions to the nightingale (Lucinia), using its name and habits as an allegory for diligence and maternal care.</meta>

[The bat] is carried and borne about by the flight of its wings. This lowly **animal** <note>original: *animal*</note> also has this habit: they cling to one another and hang from a certain place like a **cluster of grapes** <note>original: *botoris*; a variation of *botrus*, meaning a bunch of grapes</note>, and if the lowest one lets go, they all fall apart. This represents a certain gift of **charity** <term>caritas<note>In a medieval context, this refers to the highest form of Christian love and communal bond.</note></term>, which is difficult to find among men of this kind.

# The Nightingale

**L** <note>The large ornamental initial "L" mentioned in the image description begins the word *Lucinia*.</note> ucinia—the **nightingale**—takes its name from its song, as it is accustomed to signal the rising dawn of the day like a **lamp** <note>original: *lucerna*; the text suggests a false but poetic etymology connecting the bird to light.</note>. It is the same as the **acredula** <note>a classical name for a songbird, often appearing in Roman poetry to signal changes in weather.</note>. Hence, the *acredula* performs its morning songs from the **maple tree** <note>original: *acero*</note> as a sign of what is to come. 

For the bird is a watchful guardian; while it warms its eggs in the fold and lap of its body, it consoles the labor of the long night's sleeplessness with the sweetness of its song. Its greatest intention is to see to it that it might animate its offspring and warm the eggs, if not by the heat of its body, then by its sweet melodies. 

That **poor but modest woman** <note>original: *tenuis illa mulier sed pudica*; a common medieval comparison used to illustrate hard work and maternal love.</note> imitates this bird; as she pulls the heavy, hateful **millstone** <note>original: *inuisum mole lapidem*; referring to the grueling manual labor of grinding grain by hand.</note> with her arm so that she can provide the nourishment of **bread** <note>original: *panis*</note> for her little ones if it is lacking, she soothes the sad burden of poverty with a night-time song. And although she cannot match the sweetness of the nightingale, she imitates [its devotion].

<vocab>Lucinia: Nightingale, acredula: a songbird, animal: living creature, caritas: charity/love, cantus: song, diei: of the day, uigil: watchful, fetus: offspring, mulier: woman, panis: bread, sompnem: sleep/sleeplessness, botoris: cluster of grapes</vocab>

# The Raven

The **Raven** <note>original: *Corvus*</note> or **crow** <note>original: *corax*</note> takes its name from the sound of its throat, because it "croaks" with its voice. <note>original: *corvitet*; a medieval Latin term for the specific sound a raven makes.</note> It is said that this bird does not fully provide food to its hatched chicks until it recognizes in them a likeness of its own color through the blackness of their feathers. But after it has seen them with dark plumage, it recognizes them fully and feeds them more abundantly. This bird first seeks out the eye in corpses.

The **Raven** signifies the blackness of sinners or of the devil, as is written in the words of **Solomon**: "Let the raven of the valleys pick it out." <note>original: *effodiat eum corvus de convallibus*; a reference to Proverbs 30:17, often interpreted as a warning against disobedience.</note> Likewise, the raven signifies the Gentile people, as is written in **Job**: "Who prepares food for the raven when its chicks cry out to the Lord, wandering because they have no food?" <note>original: *Quis parat corvo escam suam...*; a reference to Job 38:41. In medieval exegesis, the "hungry chicks" represent the non-Jewish world waiting for spiritual nourishment.</note> 

Indeed, food is given to the chicks of ravens—namely, the children of the Gentiles—when their desire is restored through our **commission** <unclear>comissione</unclear>. <note>This likely refers to the apostolic mission to preach to all nations.</note> On the other hand, it is used in a positive sense, as in the **Song of Songs** regarding the bridegroom: "His hair is like the branches of palms, black as a raven." <note>original: *come ei sicut elate palmarum nigre quasi corvus*; Song of Solomon 5:11. Here, the raven's blackness represents divine mystery or youthful beauty.</note> 

The raven can represent the blackness of sin or of infidelity, as in the **Psalter**: "And to the chicks of the ravens that call upon him." <note>Psalm 147:9.</note> Finally, the raven represents the sinner's delay in **penitence** <term>penitentiam<note>Medieval writers often mocked the raven's cry "cras, cras" because it sounds like the Latin word for "tomorrow," symbolizing a sinner who always promises to repent tomorrow but never today.</note></term>.

<summary>This page describes the raven, detailing its supposed habit of only feeding its chicks once they turn black and exploring its varied symbolic meanings in scripture. It serves as an allegory for the Gentiles' conversion and a warning against the procrastination of repentance.</summary>
<keywords>Raven, Corvus, Solomon, Job, Song of Songs, Gentiles, Allegory, Penitence, Medieval Bestiary</keywords>

...deferring from day to day with vain hope. <note>original: *vana spe de die in diem differens*; this refers to the "Cras! Cras!" (Tomorrow! Tomorrow!) croak of the raven, which medieval writers interpreted as the voice of the procrastinating sinner who refuses to repent today.</note> As it says in **Zephaniah**: "The voice of the singer in the window, the raven on its threshold." <note>original: *Vox cantantis in fenestra, corvus in superliminaribus eius*; a reference to Zephaniah 2:14.</note>

# The Crow

The **long-lived Crow** <note>original: *Cornix annosa*; the crow was famously long-lived in classical and medieval lore.</note> has for a long time been called by its Greek name among the Latins. <note>The Latin *cornix* is related to the Greek *korōnē*.</note> Soothsayers <note>original: *augures*</note> say that this bird acts upon the cares of men with its signs, showing the ways of ambushes and predicting the future. It is a great sin <note>original: *nephas*</note> to believe these things: that God would entrust His counsels to crows. Among many things, they attribute omens to this bird and say it portends rain with its calls. From which comes that saying: *“Then the wicked crow calls for the full rain with its cruel voice.”* <note>original: *Tunc cornix plenam pluviam vocat improba voce*; a quote from Virgil’s *Georgics*, I, 388.</note>

Let men learn to love their children from the habits and devotion of crows, who even follow their flying children with diligent company; and, concerned lest they should perhaps fail while they are tender, they offer them food and do not abandon the duties of nurturing for an extended space of time. 

But on the other hand, women of our own race even commit adultery against those they love, and they feel disgust at nursing those who are more unsightly. <note>original: *foeditiores*; implying children born with perceived defects or those who are simply unwanted.</note> If they are poorer, they cast away and expose their little ones, and deny them when they are discovered. Even the rich themselves, lest more [of their inheritance]... <note>The text breaks off here; it is likely a critique of the wealthy who limit their offspring to preserve their estates.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the Raven's symbolism of procrastination and introduces the Crow, dismissing its role in divination while praising its superior parental devotion compared to human mothers.</summary>
<keywords>Raven, Crow, Zephaniah, Virgil, augury, parenting, morality, bestiary, superstition</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing section on the crow (Cornix) or raven, contrasting the bird's natural care for its young with human cruelty and greed. It then transitions into the entry for the dove (Columba), using its biological traits as an allegory for the virtues of the clergy.</meta>

...divides the inheritance. There are those who kill the fetuses within the womb, and with murderous potions <note>original: *parricidialibus succis*; literally "parricidal juices." In this context, it refers to herbal concoctions used as abortifacients.</note> in the very birth-canal, they extinguish the pledges of their own belly. Thus, life is taken away before it is even granted. Who taught man to disown his children? Who discovered such harsh laws for fathers? Who, in the fraternal fellowship of nature, made brothers unequal? 

The sons of one rich man fall into different fates: one is awarded the father's entire estate by legal decree, while another mourns the exhausted portion of his father's wealthy inheritance. Did nature divide things among sons? No—she granted to all equally, so that they might have the means for being born and living. Let nature herself teach you not to differentiate between inheritances, for she treats all humanity as equals. Indeed, those to whom she gave the common gift of being born should not be deprived of the commonality of this life; they should possess what they were given by a single lot.

# The Dove

The **Dove** <note>original: *Columba*</note> is a simple bird; it lacks gall <note>original: *felle caret*; medieval natural history held that the dove lacked a gallbladder, which was believed to be the organ that produced "bitter" bile or anger. Thus, the dove became a symbol of perfect gentleness.</note> and joins in love with a kiss. Likewise, **preachers** <note>original: *predicatores*</note> ought to lack anger and bitterness, because although they may become angry, their anger does not endure. Since indeed...

She tears [nothing] with her beak. <note>The Latin here says *Hec lacerat rostro* ("She tears with her beak"), but the comparison that follows suggests a missing "not," as it equates this to preachers *not* corrupting the scriptures.</note> And this well suits preachers, who do not corrupt the holy scriptures as heretics do. She sits beside the streams, so that having seen the hawk, she may dive in and thus escape. Similarly, preachers dwell beside the holy scriptures, so that having seen the attack and temptation of the devil, they may dive into those scriptures by acting according to the precepts of those scriptures, and thus escape. They defend themselves with others. They nest in the holes of the rock; so also preachers [nest] in the wounds—that is, in the faith of the wounds of Christ, of whom it is said: "But the rock was Christ." <note>original: *Petra autem erat Christus*; a quotation from 1 Corinthians 10:4.</note> They make a nest—that is, a fortification <note>original: *munimentum*</note>—for themselves and for others. **T**he turtledove <note>original: *Turtur*. In medieval bestiaries, the turtledove is a distinct symbol from the common dove, representing chaste widowhood and solitude.</note> is called a devoted and modest bird. She is always found dwelling upon the ridges of mountains and in desert solitudes. Indeed, she flees from the crowds and conversation of men, and stays in the woods. It is also said that in the winter time, having lost her feathers, she is known to live in the hollowed-out cavities of trees. 

The turtledove also places leaves of squill <term>squill: a bulbous flowering plant (Drimia maritima) used in ancient and medieval medicine, believed to have protective or repellent properties</term> over her nest, so that a wolf might not attack her chicks. For she knows that wolves are accustomed to flee from leaves of this kind. 

It is said of this bird that if she is widowed by the loss of her mate, she rejects the future use of the marriage bed and the name of a spouse. This is because her first love failed her, she having been deceived by the death of her beloved; since he was unfaithful to permanence <note>That is, his life was not eternal, and thus he "betrayed" her by dying.</note> and became bitter to grace, producing more pain through his death than sweetness through his love. Therefore, she refuses a second union, nor does she break the laws of modesty or the covenants made with her pleasing husband. For him alone she reserves her love; for him certainly...

Who, then, gave these laws to the turtledove? If I seek a man [who did so], I find none; for no man has dared, just as not even Paul <note>Saint Paul the Apostle, whose letters in the New Testament provide the primary Christian instructions on marriage and widowhood.</note> dared to prescribe laws for maintaining widowhood. Finally, he himself says: *I desire therefore that the younger women marry, bear children, be mistresses of families, and give no occasion to the adversary.* <note>1 Timothy 5:14. Paul suggests younger widows remarry to avoid the temptation of sin or social scandal.</note> And elsewhere: *It is good for them if they so continue. But if they cannot contain themselves, let them marry. For it is better to marry than to burn.* <note>1 Corinthians 7:8-9.</note> Paul desires in women that which perseveres in turtledoves. In another place, he does not exhort the younger women to marry, because women can scarcely fulfill modesty in the manner of the turtledove. 

Therefore, God infused this grace or affection into the turtledoves; He gave this virtue of continence <term>continence: self-restraint, particularly regarding physical desires or remarriage</term>, He who alone is able to prescribe what all should follow. The turtledove is not united only in the flower of youth; she is not tempted by the lure of opportunity. The turtledove knows not how to make her first faith void, because she knows how to preserve the chastity promised in her first lot of marriage.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the turtledove's widowhood into the entry for the swallow (Hirundo). The text focuses on the bird's natural behaviors, its interaction with humans, and the incredible architectural skill it uses to build nests.</meta>

She is a talkative bird <note>original: "garrula avis"</note> and flies in sweeping circles and winding circuits. She is most skillful in building nests and raising her young, even possessing a certain power of foresight, for she deserts and does not seek out rooftops that are about to collapse. She is not attacked by other birds unless she is prey; she flies across the seas and dwells there in the winter.

The **S**wallow is a bird small in body but exceptionally lofty in its own behavior. Lacking all things, she builds her nests with foresight, for she nests wisely. Indeed, the nest of a wise one <note>original: "nidus sapientis"</note> is more precious than gold. <note>The OCR reads "auto," which is a likely transcription error for "auro" (gold), a common comparison in medieval bestiaries regarding the "nest of wisdom."</note> For what is more becoming to a wise person than to be tested by the wandering freedom of flight, and to entrust her young to human dwellings and roofs, so that she might venture her flight among us? 

For it is a beautiful thing that from their very birth she accustoms her chicks to the benefit of human company and keeps them safer from the attacks of enemies within their nests. Then there is that remarkable thing: that she, as a master artisan herself, <note>The OCR reads "pica" (magpie), but the context and traditional sources for this text suggest "ipsa" (herself/itself), referring to the swallow acting as its own builder.</note> assembles a square home for herself without any helper. For she gathers bits of straw <term>straw: specifically "festucas," small stalks or stems of dried grass</term> in her beak and plasters them with mud <note>original: "luto"</note> so that she can glue them together. But because she cannot carry mud with her feet, she dips the tips of her feathers in water so that the dust may easily adhere to them and become slime. With this, she binds together little by little the straw or tiny twigs and makes them stick. In this manner, she builds the structure of the entire nest, so that her chicks may move about on the floor as if on a pavement within their home without harm, lest...

### The Quail

It is well-established that **Q**uails <note>original: "Coturnices"</note> are named from the sound of their voice. The Greeks call them *ortygia* <note>original: "ortigias"</note> because they were first seen on the island of Ortygia <note>An ancient name for the island of Delos</note>. These birds have specific times for arriving, for when summer is driven away, they cross the seas. The one who leads the flock is called the *leader of quails* <term>leader of quails: "ortigometra," likely the Corncrake, which was traditionally believed to guide quail migrations</term>. When this leader approaches the land, he is seized by the hawk <note>The OCR "vides rapit" is likely a corruption of "vultur" or "accipiter rapit," referring to the vulnerability of the leader bird to predators as it nears the shore</note>. 

And there is a special care among all of them to summon a leader of an external kind <note>original: "ducem sollicitare genus exterm"</note> through which they may avoid the first dangers. Their most pleasing foods are the seeds of poisons <note>Historically identified as hellebore</note>, for which reason the ancients forbade people from eating them. For only this animal, just like man, [falls from the falling sickness]. <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to the medieval belief that quails, like humans, suffered from epilepsy (morbus comitialis) because they ate poisonous plants.</note>

<summary>This page describes the swallow's legendary ability to heal its blinded chicks with herbs and introduces the quail, detailing its migration led by the "ortigometra" and its dangerous diet of poisonous seeds.</summary>
<keywords>Swallow, Hirundo, healing, Quail, Coturnix, Ortygia, migration, Ortygometra, poison, epilepsy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the entry on the Quail (Coturnix), moving from its naming and migration patterns into a detailed Christian allegory. ...suffers from the falling sickness <term>falling sickness: epilepsy, or "morbus caducus." Medieval lore claimed quails were the only creatures besides humans to suffer from this, due to their diet of poisonous seeds</term>. Quails have specific times for their arrival; for when summer is driven away, they fly across the seas. The heat of summer represents the fire of charity <note>original: "ardor caritatis"</note>. The cold of winter is the temptation of a chilled mind. Therefore, through the love of neighbor, the righteous man crosses the sea of this world toward the love of God, so that he may remain forever in that warm region. He always burns within himself with the heat of affection so that he might avoid the cold of winter—namely, the storms and winds of sudden temptation.

The bird that leads the flock is called the **Ortygometra** <term>Ortygometra: Literally the "quail-mother" or leader, often identified in modern terms as the Corncrake</term>. When it approaches the earth, the hawk sees it and seizes it. The "earth" represents earthly desires and the wicked vanities of the world. The hawk is the lurking devil who tempts through suggestion. Therefore, seeing it approaching the earth, the hawk seizes it; because the devil entangles with his snares those who approach the world while holding fast to poverty <note>In this context, likely a "poverty of spirit" or spiritual weakness that makes one vulnerable to earthly lure</note>, seeking out those who desire earthly things. Whether they turn these things to their own uses or seek them only for necessity, the hawk—that is, the devil—seizes the one who, having cast aside spiritual things, does not cease to gape after earthly ones. Therefore, it is a concern for all to urge on the leader of the foreign race <note>original: "genis exteri"; here the "leader" may refer to Christ or a spiritual guide who protects the "foreigners" or pilgrims of this world</note>, through whom they may avoid the first dangers.

There are two kinds of men: namely, the good and the evil. The "foreign races" are the little people. The righteous, therefore, diligently pay attention to these little ones and to all men, and to the divine fortunes and outcomes that befall them. While they consider these things attentively, they [avoid] the first dangers of sinning...

The **Goose** <note>original: "Anser"</note> bears witness to the night watches with the persistence of its loud cackling. Indeed, no other animal detects the scent of a human being as well as the goose. It was for this reason that the approach of the **Gauls** <note>This refers to the legendary Siege of Rome in 390 BC, when the sacred geese of Juno gave warning of a secret night attack on the Capitoline Hill</note> into the **Capitol** was detected by its cry. Regarding this, **Rabanus** <note>Rabanus Maurus (c. 780–856), a famous Benedictine scholar. His work *De universo* provided many of the moral interpretations found in later bestiaries</note> says: "This bird can signify prudent people who are well-vigilant in their own watchfulness."

There are two species of geese: namely, the domestic and the wild. The wild geese fly high in an orderly formation; they represent those who, living far from earthly affairs, maintain an orderly way of life. Domestic geese, on the other hand, live together in villages, [sharing] many...

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of the Goose, transitioning from its physical characteristics (tearing at itself with its beak and its grey versus white coloration) to its symbolic role within the monastic community, specifically comparing the Roman Capitol to the monastic Chapter.</meta>

they cry out and tear themselves with their beaks. They also signify those who, even if they come together and love one another, yet find time for talkativeness and detraction <note>original: "detractioni"; this refers to gossip or speaking ill of others</note>. Wild geese <note>original: "Campestres anseres"</note> are all of an ash-colored hue <note>original: "coloris cinerei"</note>, and I have never seen any of them mottled or snow-white. In domestic geese, however, not only is the ash-colored hue found, but also mottled or white.

In the wild geese, the ash-colored hue signifies the lowly garment of penance worn by those who are removed from the world. Those, however, who dwell in cities or in villas wear clothing of a more beautiful color. 

The goose, beyond all other animals, senses the scent of a man approaching and does not cease to cry out at night, because a discerning man <note>original: "discretus homo"</note> recognizes others, even if they are far removed, by their good or bad reputation. And when the goose senses the scent of one approaching and does not cease to cry at night, it is because a discerning brother <note>original: "discretus frater"</note> ought to cry out when he sees others failing through the negligence of ignorance.

In the **Capitol** <note>The Capitoline Hill in Rome</note> the cry of the goose once benefited the Romans; in the **chapter** <note>original: "in capitulo." A clever pun on the Roman *Capitolium* and the monastic *Capitulum* (the Chapter house), where monks gathered for daily business and correction</note> it benefits daily when the cry of a discerning brother sees negligence. The cry of the goose repelled the **Gaulish** enemy from the Capitol; from the chapter, however, the cry of a discerning brother repels the **ancient enemy** <note>A common medieval epithet for the Devil</note>. The cry of the goose kept the city of Rome immune from the onslaught of enemies; the cry of a discerning brother guards the intention [of the heart] so that it is not corrupted by small things. The enemy, however...

<summary>This page uses the goose's legendary role as a Roman sentry to illustrate the duty of a 'discerning brother' in a monastery to speak out against spiritual negligence. It also allegorizes the bird's plumage, equating the grey of wild geese with the humble dress of monks.</summary>

<keywords>Goose, allegory, penance, Capitol, Chapter house, monastery, discernment, gossip, Gauls, the Devil</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific allegory of the goose (and its role in the Roman Capitol and monastic Chapter) to a general statement on the divine purpose of birds, before beginning new entries for the Peacock and the Screech-owl.</meta>

Divine providence <note>original: "diuina prudentia"</note> would not have set the natures of birds before us unless it perhaps wished them to be of some benefit to us in some way.

The tail feathers are decorated with blue and green "eyes." The miniature is framed by a simple red and blue border.</image-desc>

The **Peacock** <note>Latin: *Pauo*</note> takes its name from the sound of its voice. Its flesh is so hard that it hardly feels decay <note>A common medieval belief, popularized by St. Augustine, was that peacock meat was incorruptible and did not rot</note> nor is it easily cooked; about which a certain person <note>The Roman poet Martial, Epigrams 13.70</note> says:

> You marvel as often as he unfolds his jeweled wings. Can you hand this bird over to a cruel cook for his sauce? <note>original Latin: "Miraris quotiens gemantes explicat alas. Si potes hunc seuo tradere iure coquo."</note>

Indeed, because the peacock was brought from distant regions for **Solomon** <note>Referring to the Bible, 1 Kings 10:22, where Solomon's fleet brought back gold, silver, ivory, apes, and peacocks</note> and has various colors in its feathers, it signifies the **Gentile people** <note>Non-Jewish nations</note> coming from distant parts of the earth to **Christ** <note>original: "xpm," a common abbreviation for the Greek "Christum"</note>; they shine before him through the grace and adornment of many virtues.

The **Screech-owl** <note>Latin: *Ulula*</note> is a bird named from the Greek word *ololýzein* <note>The transcription "proto oloi zin" is a phonetic rendering of the Greek "apò toû ololýzein," meaning "to cry out or wail"</note>, that is, named from lamentation and mourning. For when it cries, it either imitates weeping or gro[aning...]

...[definit]ely known. Whence also among the **Augurs** <note>Ancient Roman religious officials who observed the flight and sounds of birds to interpret the will of the gods</note>, if it is silent while mourning sadness, it is said to show prosperity. This bird can signify the wailing of sinners in hell. Whence the prophet, when mention is made of the devastation of **Babylon** <note>Referring to Isaiah 13:21, describing the desolation of the fallen city</note>, screech-owls are said to dwell in its houses, just as are the **pelican** <note>original: "onocrotalus"</note> and the **hedgehog** <note>original: "ericius"</note>.

## The Hoopoe

The Greeks call it the **Hoopoe** <note>Latin: *Hupupa*</note> because it sits in human excrement and feeds on stinking dung. It is a most filthy bird, and is "helmeted" with protruding crests, always dwelling in tombs and human dung. If anyone anoints themselves with its blood, when they go to sleep, they will see demons suffocating them <note>A common medieval superstition; the bird's association with filth and tombs led to its connection with nightmares and the demonic</note>.

Whence **Rabanus** <note>Rabanus Maurus, a 9th-century scholar whose encyclopedias often linked animal behaviors to moral lessons</note> says: This bird signifies wicked men and sinners who continually delight in lingering in the filth of sins. The hoopoe is also said to love mourning, because "the sadness of the world works spiritual death" <note>A paraphrase of 2 Corinthians 7:10</note>. For this reason, it is necessary for one who loves God to "rejoice always, pray without ceasing, and give thanks in all things" <note>1 Thessalonians 5:16-18</note>, because joy is the fruit of the Spirit.

Concerning the hoopoe, the **Physiologus** <note>An ancient Greek text that interpreted animal characteristics through a Christian lens, serving as the foundation for medieval bestiaries</note> also says that when it grows old and is unable to fly, its children [come] to...

<summary>This page concludes the moralized description of the screech-owl as a resident of ruins and begins the entry on the hoopoe, described as a filthy bird representing sinners and worldly sorrow.</summary>
<keywords>hoopoe, screech-owl, Rabanus Maurus, Physiologus, Babylon, superstition, demons, spiritual death, Augurs</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for the Hoopoe (begun on the previous page) which describes its habit of caring for its aging parents, followed by the beginning of the entry for the Cock (Gallus).</meta>

...they come to her and pluck the oldest feathers from her body, and they do not cease to warm her until her feathers grow back. They sustain her with food, as the writing says <note>The author likely refers to the "Physiologus" or earlier natural history texts often treated with authority similar to scripture</note>, until they become as they were before and she can fly away with her strength regained. This example is given to human children who expel their parents from their homes when they have grown old; who refuse to sustain them when they fail, even though these parents were the ones who raised them when they were still little. Let a rational man see, therefore, what he owes to his father or mother, when an irrational creature, as we have said, returns the favor to its parents in their necessity when they grow old.

### The Cock

The **Cock** <note>Latin: *Gallus*</note> is so called from "castration" <note>The text follows a false medieval etymology linking the bird's name to the *Galli*, the castrated priests of the goddess Cybele</note>. For among all other birds, these alone have their testicles removed <note>A reference to the practice of creating capons, or perhaps a misunderstanding of bird anatomy</note>; for the ancients called those who were cut "galli." Just as a lioness <note>Latin: *leena*</note> comes from a lion, and a dragoness <note>Latin: *dracena*</note> from a dragon, so a **hen** <note>Latin: *gallina*</note> comes from a cock. Its limbs, as some say, if they are mixed with liquid gold, are seen to consume it <note>A medieval belief that the flesh of a rooster was so potent it could dissolve or "eat" gold during a boiling or alchemical process</note>. The **cock-crow** <note>Latin: *gallicantus*</note> is also most sweet in the nights; and not only sweet, but also useful, for like a good housemate he both wakes the sleeper and warns the anxious, and [guides] the traveler...

<summary>This page concludes the description of the hoopoe's filial piety before beginning the entry on the cock, focusing on its etymology and the utility of its morning song.</summary>
<keywords>hoopoe, filial piety, parents, rooster, cock-crow, etymology, gold consumption, medieval nature lore</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for the Cock (Gallus), shifting from its etymology to its symbolic and spiritual significance as the herald of dawn and a catalyst for repentance.</meta>

...comforts [the weary]. Standing out with the musical significance of the deep night—when this bird sings, the thief abandons his ambushes. At this time, the **morning star** <note>Latin: *lucifer*, meaning the "light-bringer" or the planet Venus as it appears before sunrise; in this context, it is a natural phenomenon rather than a reference to a fallen angel</note> having risen, illuminates the entire sky. While it sings, the anxious sailor sets aside his sadness, and every storm and tempest, often stirred up by the first gusts of wind, subsides <note>original: *immefat*, likely a scribal error for a word meaning to grow calm or diminish</note>. At this sound, a surge of devotion leaps up in man to pray to the **Lord** <note>Latin abbreviation: *dñm* for *dominum*</note>, and it renews the duty of reading. Finally, when this bird sings, the very **Rock of the Church** <note>A reference to Saint Peter, whom Christ called the "rock" upon which the church would be built</note> washes away his own guilt, which he had contracted by his denial before the cock crowed. Through this song, hope returns to all people; health is given to the sick; the discomfort of pains and wounds is diminished; the burning heat of fevers is soothed. Faith returns to those who have fallen. **Jesus** <note>Latin abbreviation: *ihc*</note> looks back at those who falter; he corrects those who wander; at last, he looked upon Peter and immediately the error departed. Denial was cast out; confession followed. The reading teaches that this did not happen by chance, but by the decree of the Lord.

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual symbolism of the Cock to that of the Hen, followed by a new entry on the Duck, continuing the bestiary's survey of birds and their moral or etymological significance.</meta>

By the name of the **Hen**, the **Wisdom of God** is designated <note>Latin: *sapientia dei*. In medieval theology, the "Wisdom of God" is often identified with Christ, the second person of the Trinity</note>, just as is said through Wisdom itself in the Gospel: 

> **Jerusalem** <note>original: *Ierlm*, a common medieval abbreviation for *Ierusalem*</note>, 
> **Jerusalem**, you who kill the prophets, how often I wanted 
> to gather your children together just as a 
> **hen** gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were 
> unwilling. 

The name of the hen is not ignoble. Consider other birds. Many birds produce offspring before our eyes and keep their chicks warm, but no other bird becomes so **weakened** <note>Latin: *infirmatur*. This refers to the visible change in a hen's appearance—drooping wings and a hoarse cry—when she is caring for her brood, a state medieval writers compared to Christ’s voluntary "weakness" in the Incarnation</note> when with her chicks as the hen. We have seen **swallows**, **geese**, and **storks** outside their nests, and we did not even know whether they had offspring. But indeed, we recognize the hen by the weakness of her voice and the loosening of her feathers. She is entirely changed by her affection for her chicks. Because they are weak, she makes herself weak. Why? Because we too were weak, and the **Wisdom of God** made himself weak, because "the Word was made flesh and dwelt among us."

In the background are three stylized green trees on the left and a red field decorated with small white stars on the right. The duck in the foreground has dark grey-blue plumage, while the duck behind it is brownish-tan. The illumination is placed to the right of the text regarding ducks.</image-desc>

<margin>
An aquatic bird <note>Latin: *auis aquatica*</note>
in English, a **duck** <note>original: *a ducke*. This is an interesting instance of a Middle English gloss appearing in a Latin manuscript to clarify the animal's identity</note>

The **Duck** <note>Latin: *Anas*</note> received its name among us from its **assiduity**—its constant persistence—in swimming <note>The author is using a false etymology, linking *anas* (duck) to *assiduitate* (persistence/assiduity)</note>. Therefore, some of the people of **Germany** say: that it is nourished more than others. The name of the **Goose** <note>Latin: *Anser*</note> gave [the name] to the duck by way of a diminutive, either from a likeness or because the duck itself has a frequency of swimming. This...

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the symbolism of the Hen as the Wisdom of God, comparing her protective nature and physical "weakness" when raising chicks to Christ’s Incarnation. This page transitions to the Goose and then to a general natural history of the Egg.</meta>

...it bears witness to the watches of the night by the persistence of its cry. No animal, however, senses the scent of a human as the **goose** <note>Latin: *anser*</note> does. Hence, by its cry, the ascent of the **Gauls** <note>original: *gallorum*. This refers to the famous legend of 390 BC, where the Senones Gauls attempted a midnight surprise attack on the Roman Citadel, only to be thwarted when the sacred geese of Juno began honking, alerting the defenders</note> into the **Capitol** was detected. 

All kinds of birds, however, are born twice: first they produce **eggs**; then, they are formed and animated by the heat of the mother’s body. **Eggs** <note>Latin: *oua*</note> are so named from the fact that they are full of moisture <note>Latin: *humore*</note> on the inside. For that which has moisture on the outside is called "humid," but that which is moist on the inside is called "uvidus" <note>This is a Latin etymological wordplay: *ovum* (egg) is being linked to *uvidus* (moist/damp)</note>. Some, however, think the name of the **egg** has a Greek origin. For they [the Greeks] call them *oa* <note>original: *oa* (ὠά). The author suggests the Latin *oua* is simply the Greek word with a 'u' added</note>, with the letter 'u' removed. 

Certain eggs are conceived by the empty wind; however, they are not capable of producing life unless they have been conceived through male intercourse and penetrated by the seminal spirit <note>This refers to the ancient and medieval belief in "wind-eggs" (*subventanea*), which were thought to be produced by hens without a mate, resulting in infertility</note>. 

The power of eggs is said to be so great that wood soaked in them will not burn, and not even a garment [so treated] will catch fire. When mixed with lime, it is said to glue together fragments of glass. This [the goose] can signify prudent men who are well-vigilant in their own guardianship.

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the natural history of the egg, noting its etymology and the ancient belief in "wind-eggs." This page shifts focus to the Sparrow, moving from the physical characteristics of the bird to its deep symbolic meaning in the Psalms and the Book of Leviticus.</meta>

**Sparrows** are tiny flying creatures, named for their smallness <note>The author is using a common medieval etymology, linking the Latin *passer* (sparrow) to *parvitas* (smallness)</note>, from which they are also called "the tiny" and "the small" <note>original: *pusilli & parvi*</note>. Concerning which it is written in the Psalm: *The sparrow has found a house for itself* <note>Psalm 84:3</note>. And elsewhere: *Like a solitary sparrow on a building* <note>Psalm 102:7</note>. And in another part of the Psalm: *Move to the mountain like a sparrow* <note>Psalm 11:1</note>. Likewise, these sparrows in the Psalm: *There the sparrows shall build their nests* <note>Psalm 104:17</note>.

The **sparrow** is very swift because of its great speed; it is a bird that does not tolerate living in the forests, but longingly seeks a home for itself in the holes of walls. Since, when it finds one, it exults, happy with excessive joy, because it believes it is no longer exposed to the snares of various adversities <note>The OCR reads *denisarum aduisitatum*, likely a transcription of *diversarum adversitatum* (various adversities)</note>. Thus the **soul** rejoices when it perceives its dwelling place to be prepared in the kingdom of heaven.

Likewise, those sparrows which were ordered to be offered in the cleansing of the **leper** <note>original: *lepsi*, a contraction for *leprosi*</note> in the book of **Leviticus** <note>Leviticus 14:4 describes a ritual involving two birds, cedar wood, scarlet yarn, and hyssop to cleanse a person of leprosy</note> signify the sacrament of Christ’s Passion, in which we are cleansed from the filth of our sins. This is what **Paul** shows, saying: *All of us who were baptized in Christ Jesus were baptized into His death* <note>Romans 6:3</note>. For in this [the blood of the slain sparrow], the living sparrow and the cedar wood and the twisted scarlet thread and the hyssop are dipped. For all these things are contained within the power of **baptism**, which we have said is a figure and a sign of the Divine Passion. But the **living sparrow** we must understand as the divinity of the **Only-Begotten** <note>This refers to Christ. In medieval exegesis, the bird that was killed represented Christ's mortal humanity, while the bird that lived and was released represented His eternal divinity</note>.

<summary>This page describes the sparrow as a symbol of the soul seeking heaven and as a Christological figure in the cleansing rituals of Leviticus. It explains how the sparrow's preference for domestic walls over forests mirrors the soul's desire for a home in the kingdom of God.</summary>
<keywords>sparrow, Psalms, Leviticus, baptism, Passion of Christ, leprosy ritual, symbolism, soul, Hugh of Fouilloy</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the sparrow, specifically focusing on the ritual for cleansing lepers described in Leviticus 14 and the symbolic meanings of the sparrow’s behavior in the Psalms.</meta>

In the blood, even here, the slain bird and water are dipped; that is, in the grace of **baptism**. For we are not baptized into the suffering of a mere man, but into the suffering of one who is both God and man. Then, after this, the living **sparrow** is released into the field to fly away, because the **impassible divinity** <note>In medieval theology, "impassibility" is the quality of being incapable of suffering or pain; here it refers to Christ's divine nature remaining untouched by death even as His human nature suffered.</note> was inseparable from the flesh that suffered. Thus, while the mortal part is baptized in blood, the living, impassible part flies free into the open field; it is released, specifically, into the wide expanse of **contemplation**. When our intellect releases it, it is held within contemplations of this kind.

There is also another meaning for the **sparrow**, as we mentioned above; hence we read: *flee to the mountain like a sparrow* <note>Psalm 11:1</note>. There are many kinds of sparrows. Some delight in the holes of walls; others seek out dewy valleys; some desire rugged mountains. This discourse speaks of those who are swept up to the highest places of the earth with the lightest flight; these are rightly compared to those who are carried away toward wicked teachings <note>original: *nequissimas predicationes*</note> by the lightness of an inconstant mind. 

For in the **Psalter**, the prophet says: *I have become like a night-owl in the house; I have watched and become like a solitary sparrow on a building* <note>Psalm 102:6–7. The "night-owl" (nicticorax) and the "solitary sparrow" were common symbols of the lonely, penitent soul or the monk in seclusion.</note>. Through these names of birds, I believe the different types of **penitents** are revealed to us.

<summary>This page uses the ritual of the two sparrows from Leviticus to explain Christ's dual nature and interprets the sparrow's flight as a metaphor for spiritual contemplation and the inconstancy of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>sparrow, baptism, divinity, contemplation, Psalms, night-owl, penitence, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the sparrow mentioned at the end of the previous page, specifically focusing on its vigilance and its symbolic habitation on the "rooftops" of the Church, completing a triad of birds (pelican, night-owl, and sparrow) that represent the stages of a penitent life.</meta>

It is a small bird, as we have said before, but it is exceedingly shrewd and watchful. It is not easily entangled in snares, nor is it deceived by the gluttony of its belly or the desire for food. Because of its own smallness, it is careful not to be caught by a predator or to have its young devoured by the treachery of serpents. Among birds, it is protected by a unique caution. It hastens to the high gables <note>original: *fastigia*</note> of houses so that it may be removed from those suspicious accidents to which it is proven to be vulnerable through many dangers.

**H**uic <note>The bolded initials on this page spell out the word *HUIC*, meaning "To this..."</note> To this bird is rightly compared the person who, fearing the snares of the devil, takes refuge spiritually within the walls of the Church. Watching from its high gable, they persevere in the greatest safety. 

**U**nicam <note>original: *unicam*—the text uses "single" or "unique" to refer to the "solitary" sparrow of the Psalms.</note> It is called "solitary" because of charity, which makes one out of many. 

**I**n the building, it stays because of the height of faith and the strength of the mind. 

**C**onsideration must also be given to how these qualities are properly drawn from the larger birds—the **pelican** and the **night-owl**—and reach down to the tiny smallness of the **sparrow**. For by descending step by step through penance, in the first place one is great <note>Like the Pelican, symbolizing the beginning of conversion or the solitary life.</note>; in the second, moderate <note>Like the Night-owl, an intermediate stage.</note>; and in the third, through habit itself, entirely endurable <note>Like the tiny Sparrow, representing the humble, perfected soul.</note>. 

These birds, however, are parables by way of figure. They are different in kind from men, but they are similar in the quality of their habits.

<meta>This page transitions from the final bird in the bestiary's ornithology section, the kite, to the start of the section on insects, beginning with the bee. It maintains the pattern of providing an etymological origin for the animal's name followed by its moral or symbolic significance.</meta>

The **Kite** <note>original: *Miluus*</note> is soft in both its strength and its flight; it is, as it were, a "soft bird" <note>This reflects a common medieval etymology connecting the word *miluus* (kite) with *mollis* (soft), suggesting the bird's graceful, effortless-looking flight.</note>, from which it also receives its name. It is, however, exceedingly rapacious <note>greedy or predatory</note>, and it is always lying in wait for domestic birds. The kite signifies, I believe, a rapacious or arrogant person, as it says in the Psalter according to the Hebrews: "The fir tree is the house of the kite." <note>This refers to Psalm 104:17 (or 103:17 in the Vulgate). In many medieval translations, the bird inhabiting the fir tree was identified as the kite rather than the stork or heron found in modern translations.</note>

---

<image-desc>A framed rectangular miniature with a red border. On the right, a tall, conical beehive (skep) is depicted with horizontal bands of red and tan. On the left is a stylized plant with green stalks and three large blue flowers. Several small, dark bees fly in the space between the flowers and the hive. The background is purple, decorated with small white circles, except for a central rectangular area that is gilded with gold leaf.</image-desc>

---

**Bees** <note>original: *Apes*</note> are so called either because they bind <note>original: *alligent*</note> themselves to one another by their feet, or because they are born without feet <note>A classic Isidorean etymology: *apes* was thought to come from *a-* (without) and *pes* (feet), based on the appearance of bee larvae.</note>; for only later do they receive both feet and wings. These skillful creatures, assigned to the duty of producing honey, inhabit their appointed places. They construct their dwellings with inexpressible art, and from various flowers, they build the honeycomb. With woven waxes, they fill their camps <note>The text uses the military term *castra* (camps or fortresses) to describe the hive, reflecting the organized, disciplined nature of bee society.</note> with countless offspring. They have the kings <note>Medieval observers incorrectly identified the Queen bee as a "King" (*rex*), viewing the hive as a masculine monarchy.</note> of their armies and wage battles. They flee from smoke and are provoked by noise. Most people...

<summary>This page concludes the section on the kite, linking its 'soft' flight to a predatory nature and moral arrogance, before introducing the bee as a model of industrious, organized society with complex etymological origins.</summary>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on bees, moving from the etymology of their name to the ancient belief in their spontaneous generation from animal carcasses, and then into a description of their communal social structure, which served as a moral exemplar in medieval thought.</meta>

...are known to be born from the carcasses of oxen. For to create them, the flesh of slaughtered calves is beaten, so that worms are created from the putrefied blood, which afterwards become bees. Strictly speaking, however, bees are called "born from oxen," <note>original: *apes uocantur ore de bob’*. The author is referencing the "bugonia" myth, popularized by Virgil and Isidore of Seville, which claimed bees were spontaneously generated from dead cattle.</note> just as hornets are born from horses, drones from mules, and wasps from donkeys.

The Greeks call them *Castros*, <note>original: *Castros*. This likely refers to the "drones" or the larger cells in a hive. In medieval Latin bestiaries, these were often confused with the "kings" or leaders of the hive.</note> which are created larger in the furthest parts of the honeycombs; some think they are called "kings" because they lead the camps <note>original: *castra*. A wordplay on *castros* (drones/kings) and *castra* (military camps).</note>.

Bees alone among every kind of living creature have offspring in common among all; all inhabit one dwelling; they are enclosed by the threshold of one homeland. For all, labor is in common, food is in common, work is in common, use and profit are in common, and flight is in common. What more? Generating is common to all, and the integrity of a virginal body and giving birth is common to all. For they neither mix with one another in any sexual intercourse, nor are they undone by lust, nor do births come through pains. <note>This description of the bee's "virgin birth" was frequently used by medieval theologians as a natural parallel to the Virgin Mary and as a model for the celibate monastic life.</note>

<margin>A</margin> And suddenly they send out a very great swarm of children, gathering their offspring with their mouths from leaves and herbs. <note>Before the observation of the queen laying eggs was understood, it was believed bees "collected" their young from the nectar of flowers or surfaces of leaves.</note> They ordain a king <note>Modern science identifies the leader as a queen bee, but medieval authors consistently identified the leader as a king (*rex*).</note> for themselves; they create peoples; and though placed under a king, they are nonetheless free. For they hold the prerogative of judgment and the feeling of faithful devotion, for they love him as if he were appointed by themselves, and they honor him with such a great swarm. Moreover, the king is not chosen by lot...

<summary>This page describes the legendary origins of bees from ox carcasses and praises their communal, virginal lifestyle as a model of social harmony and purity.</summary>
<keywords>bees, bugonia, spontaneous generation, communal life, virginity, drone, king bee, medieval biology</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the social and political structure of the bee colony, which Saint Ambrose uses as a moral allegory for human society. It follows the previous page’s description of the communal nature of bees and their unique "virgin" birth.</meta>

<margin>
<image-desc>A manicula (pointing hand) in the right margin, pointing towards the first line of the text.</image-desc>
</margin>

...because in drawing lots there is only chance, not true judgment. <note>Ambrose is arguing that the natural order of bees is superior to human systems of selecting leaders by chance or ballot, as the bee "king" is chosen by nature itself rather than by luck.</note> And often, through the irrational accident of the lot, an inferior person is preferred over better men. 

For bees, however, a king <note>original: *rex*. Ancient and medieval writers, following authorities like Aristotle and Virgil, mistakenly believed the leader of a beehive was a king. It was not until the 17th century that the "king" was correctly identified as the Queen bee.</note> is formed by the clear marks of nature, so that he stands out in body size and beauty. Furthermore—and this is most important in a king—he is distinguished by a gentleness of character. For even if he possesses a sting, he does not use it for vengeance. For these are the laws of nature, not written in letters but impressed upon character: that those who possess the greatest power should be the most lenient in punishing. 

But even those bees who do not obey the laws of the king punish themselves with the condemnation of repentance; they die from the wound of their own sting. <note>Ambrose refers to the biological fact that a honeybee dies after using its barbed sting, which he interprets as a moral act of self-execution for "sinning" against the natural law of gentleness.</note> It is said that this nation of bees observes this rule even today: that through the sin they have committed, they carry out the sentence of death upon themselves.

Therefore, no one follows their king so faithfully—not the Persians, who have the harshest laws for their subjects, nor the Indians, nor the peoples of the Sarmatians <note>original: *Sarmatarum*. The Sarmatians were an ancient nomadic people of the Eurasian Steppe, often cited by Roman authors as examples of disciplined or "barbarian" social structures.</note>—none observe their ruler with such reverence and devotion as the bees. None dare to exit their homes or go out to any pastures unless the king has first departed and claimed the leadership of the flight. 

Their journey is through fragrant countrysides, where sleep is breathed in from the flowers, where a fleeing stream runs through the grasses, and where the riverbanks are pleasant. There, the eager youth play; there, they perform their field exercises; there, they find a release from their cares.

<summary>Ambrose describes the bee colony as a model monarchy where the leader is chosen by nature for his beauty and gentleness rather than by chance. He argues that bees exhibit a natural devotion and moral discipline that surpasses even the most famous human civilizations.</summary>
<keywords>bees, natural law, monarchy, St. Ambrose, leadership, Sarmatians, allegory, social order</keywords>

The work itself is sweet; from flowers and from sweet
herbs the first foundations of the **camp** <term>original: *castrorum*. Ambrose uses military terminology to describe the hive as a fortified, organized settlement.</term> are laid.
For what else is a **honeycomb** <term>original: *fauus*</term> but a kind of
camp? Finally, the honeycomb is protected by these
masses of **bees**. <term>original: *apum*</term> What square camps
can possess as much structure and grace as these hollowed
honeycombs, in which tiny and round cells
are supported by their connection to one another?
What **architect** <term>original: *architectus*</term> taught them to compose those
**hexagonal** <term>original: *hexagonas*</term> <note>Ambrose marvels at the mathematical precision of the six-sided cells, a common theme in natural theology where the complexity of nature proves a Divine Creator.</note> cells with such indistinguishable equality of sides?
And to suspend thin waxes between the walls of their houses,
to pack away **honey**, <term>original: *mella*</term> and to expand their storehouses
(mixed with flowers) with a certain **nectar**? <term>original: *nectare*</term>
You may see all of them competing in their duty: some are
vigilant in seeking food. Others provide a skillful
guard for the camp. Others scout for coming
rains and watch the movements of the
clouds. Others fashion wax from flowers. Others
collect with their mouths the **dew** <term>original: *roris*</term> <note>Ancient naturalists believed that honey fell from the sky as a "divine dew" and that bees simply gathered it from the petals of flowers rather than producing it themselves from nectar.</note> poured into the flowers. Yet
none of them lie in wait for the labors of others, or
seek food by robbery—and would that they did not fear
the ambushes of robbers! <note>A reference to humans or animals who raid the hive for its honey.</note> Yet from here they release their
stings and pour out venom even among the honey
if they have been provoked, and they lay down
their lives in the wound with the heat of vengeance. Therefore,
into the middle of the camp's valleys, that moisture of dew
is poured, and gradually over time it is thickened into honey,
though it was liquid from the start, from the gathering of wax and flower.

<summary>Ambrose describes the hive as a sophisticated military camp and marvels at the architectural precision of hexagonal cells and the selfless division of labor among the bees.</summary>

Justly, because **Scripture** <term>original: *scriptura*</term> praises the **Bee** <term>original: *Apem*</term>

they think it beautiful to die for him.

has had his office interrupted. Thus, while other birds
hardly produce a single brood in a year,
bees create twins, and they outweigh the rest with a double
