# The Peridexion Tree

The **Perindens** <note>original: *Perindens*. A common medieval misspelling of the Greek *peridexion*, likely meaning "turning to the right" or "all-right." This legendary tree of India was a staple of the medieval bestiary, symbolizing the protection of the Church or God.</note> is a **tree** in **India**. <term>original: *india*</term> Now, the **fruit** <term>original: *fructus*</term> of this tree is entirely **sweet** and very **pleasant**. <term>original: *suauis*</term> Moreover, **doves** <term>original: *Columbe*</term> <note>In bestiary tradition, these doves represent the faithful or the Holy Spirit finding refuge in the teachings of the Church.</note> take delight in the fruits of this tree and **live** within it, feeding upon its fruits. However, the **dragon** <term>original: *Draco*</term> is an enemy to the... <note>The Latin text breaks off at *co*, which would have continued as *columbis* (the doves). The legend concludes that the dragon fears the shadow of the tree and cannot approach it; he waits at a distance for a dove to leave the safety of the branches.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Peridexion tree of India, a legendary tree whose sweet fruit attracts doves while its shadow provides them safety from a predatory dragon.</summary>
<keywords>Perindens, Peridexion tree, India, Doves, Dragon, Allegory, Bestiary, Spiritual protection</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from page 91. The Latin text begins with the completion of the word "columbis" (doves), following the break from the previous page's final word "draco" (dragon), which was an enemy to the "doves."</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>

to the **doves**. It <note>The dragon.</note> fears the **tree** and its **shadow**, where the **doves** dwell; it cannot approach the tree nor its shadow. For if the shadow of the tree moves toward the **west**, the **dragon** flees toward the **east**; and if its shadow moves toward the **east**, it flees toward the **west**. But if it should happen that a **dove** is found outside the tree or its shadow, the **dragon** kills it. 

### The Spiritual Meaning of the Tree

Understand the **tree** to be **God the Father**. The **shadow** is the **Son**, just as the angel **Gabriel** said to **Mary**: *“The Holy Spirit shall come upon you, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow you”* <note>original: *Spiritus sanctus superueniet in te ⁊ uirtus altissimi obumbrabit tibi*. A quotation of Luke 1:35.</note>. The **fruit** represents heavenly wisdom—that is, **Christ**. The **dove** represents the **Holy Spirit**. 

Therefore, take heed, O man, lest after you have received the **Holy Spirit**—that is, spiritual understanding descending from heaven and remaining upon you—you be cast outside. You would then be alienated from the **Father**, the **Son**, and the **Holy Spirit**, and the **dragon** would attack you—that is, the **devil**. For if you possess the **Holy Spirit**, the **dragon** cannot approach you. 

Pay attention, therefore, O man, and remain in the **universal faith** <note>original: *fide catholica*. In this context, "catholic" refers to the "universal" or "whole" orthodox Christian faith.</note>; dwell there, and there persevere in the one **universal Church**. Be as careful as you can, lest you be found outside the house, and that **dragon**, the **ancient serpent**, seize you and devour you just like **Judas**, who, as soon as he went out from the **Lord** and from his brothers the **apostles**, was immediately devoured by a **demon** and perished.

# On Snakes

**The snake** <term>original: *anguis*</term> is a general name for all **serpents** that can be folded and twisted. It is called *anguis* because it is **angular** <note>original: *angulosus*. Medieval writers often used "folk etymologies," linking words by their sound to explain their nature. Here, the snake is linked to "angles" because it does not move in a straight line.</note> and never straight. The **adder** <term>original: *colubrum*</term> is so called because it **inhabits the shadows** <note>original: *colat umbras*. Another folk etymology, linking the word *coluber* to the Latin words for "inhabit" (*colere*) and "shadow" (*umbra*).</note>.

<meta>This page continues the description of the dragon from page 91 and transitions into a general discussion of serpents and reptiles, drawing heavily from the "Etymologies" of Isidore of Seville.</meta>

...ou, or because it slides along slippery paths with winding bends. For the term **slippery** <note>original: *lubricum*</note> is applied to anything that slides away when it is held, like a fish or a snake. 

The **serpent** <note>original: *serpens*</note>, moreover, received its name because it "creeps" <note>original: *serpit*; the author is connecting the noun "serpent" to the Latin verb for creeping or slithering</note> by hidden approaches; it does not move with distinct steps, but crawls by the tiny pressures of its scales. Those animals, however, that move on four feet, such as **lizards** and **newts** <note>original: *stelliones*; a "stellio" was a spotted lizard, often used to symbolize craftiness</note>, are not called "serpents" but **reptiles**. 

But serpents are those reptiles that crawl on their bellies and chests. Of these, there are as many types of **venom** as there are species; as many ways to die and as many types of pain as there are colors among them.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of serpents from page 91, focusing specifically on the dragon (draco), the largest of all serpents. The text transitions from the physical description of the beast to its allegorical significance in Christian teaching, maintaining the structure of medieval bestiaries which blend natural history with moral lessons.</meta>

The **dragon** <note>original: *draco*</note> is the largest of all serpents, or indeed of all living creatures upon the earth. The Greeks call it *draconta* <note>original: *dracontā*</note>, from which the name was derived into Latin as *draco*. It is said that the dragon is often drawn out from its caves and borne into the air, and the air becomes turbulent because of it. It has a crest, a small mouth, and narrow pipes <note>original: *fistulis*; referring to the nostrils or windpipe</note> through which it draws breath and thrusts out its tongue. Its power, however, lies not in its teeth but in its tail, and it causes harm by lashing rather than by its bite <note>original: *rictu*; literally the opening of the jaws</note>. 

Yet, the dragon is harmless in terms of poison. They say that poisons are not necessary for it to cause death, because if it binds anyone in its coils, it kills them. Even the **elephant** is not safe from it despite its great bodily size. For the dragon hides near the paths along which elephants are accustomed to travel; it binds their legs with knots and destroys them by suffocation. It is born in Ethiopia and in India, where there is perpetual heat in the burning sun.

To this dragon the **Devil** is likened, who is the most monstrous serpent. He is often stirred up from his cave into the air, and the air shines because of him, because the Devil, raising himself from the depths, transforms himself into an angel of light <note>A reference to the biblical passage 2 Corinthians 11:14</note> and deceives fools with the hope of false glory and earthly, human honor. He is said to be crested because he is the king of pride. He has power not in his teeth but in his tail because, having been stripped of his true strength, he deceives with lies those whom he draws to himself. Just as the dragon lies in wait around the paths along which the elephants...

### **The Basilisk**
The **basilisk** <note>original: *basiliscus*</note> is called *regulus* <note>original: *regulus*; literally 'little king'</note> in Latin, because it is the king of serpents, so much so that when other serpents see it, they flee. This is because it kills them with its very smell.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature with a gold leaf background, framed in orange. It depicts a basilisk, a creature with the body and head of a crested bird and a long, scaly, serpentine tail. The basilisk faces right toward three rearing snakes. To the left is a stylized tree with blue and green foliage. The miniature is positioned within the text block.</image-desc>

For even if it merely looks at a man, it destroys him. Indeed, no flying bird can pass by its gaze unharmed; even if the bird is far away, it is consumed and devoured by the creature’s breath <note>original: *ore*; literally 'mouth' or 'breath'</note>. However, it is conquered by **weasels** <note>original: *mustelis*</note>, which men place into the caves where the basilisks hide. Thus, when the basilisk sees the weasel, it flees, but the weasel pursues and kills it. For the Parent of all things <note>A common medieval reference to God as the Creator of nature</note> created nothing without a remedy. It is, moreover, half a foot in length and marked with white spots. These "little kings" <note>original: *reguli*</note>, just like scorpions, frequent sandy places; and once they have come to the water, they cause those who drink it to become hydrophobic and maddened <note>original: *lymphaticos*; a term for madness or being "possessed by water nymphs," here linked to the effects of the basilisk's venom in water</note>. The *sibillus* <note>A variant name for the basilisk, possibly derived from its hissing</note> is the same creature as the *regulus*.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegory of the basilisk, identifying it with the Devil and interpreting Psalm 91:13. It concludes the entry on the basilisk by affirming orthodox Christian doctrine against heresy.</meta>

For it kills with its hiss; even before it bites, it causes sores. The **basilisk** <note>original: *basiliscus*</note> signifies the **Devil**, who openly kills the unwary with the poison of his wickedness. But this same beast is overcome—just as it is by all harmful animals—by the **soldier of Christ** <note>A common metaphor for a faithful Christian</note> who places his entire hope in Him whose power overcomes and tramples every adverse power. Concerning this, the prophet also says in the Psalms: *You shall walk upon the asp and the basilisk, and you shall trample the lion and the dragon* <note>Psalm 91:13 (Vulgate 90:13). This verse was frequently used in the Middle Ages to illustrate Christ's victory over evil.</note>. Here, divine power is expressed, which remains unafraid of such raging things.

For all these names are fittingly applied to the **Devil**. He is the **asp** <note>original: *aspis*</note> when he acts in secret; he is the **basilisk** when he openly spreads his poisons. He is a **lion** <note>original: *leo*</note> when he persecutes the innocent, and a **dragon** <note>original: *draco*</note> when he swallows the negligent with unholy greed. Truly, all these things lay prostrate at the feet of the Lord in His glorious arrival. For He alone was able to subdue such ferocity—He who is proven to exist as co-eternal and consubstantial <term>consubstantialis: sharing the same divine essence or substance</term> with the Father according to His divinity. If we handle these matters diligently <note>Latin abbreviation: *diligenter*</note> through the preaching of the holy fathers, so that we are not disturbed by the heretical wickedness of madmen <note>A reference to theological disputes regarding the nature of Christ</note>, all things will stand for us in absolute truth just as they have been told <note>Latin abbreviation: *tractemus*</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the basilisk as a symbol of the Devil to the entry on the viper, another serpent frequently featured in medieval bestiaries. It uses the supposed "unnatural" biology of the viper to transition into moral lessons about parenthood and marriage.</meta>

The **viper** <note>original: *vipera*</note> is so called because it gives birth by force <note>The author uses a popular medieval folk etymology, linking the Latin *vipera* to *vi* (by force) and *parere* (to give birth)</note>. For when its womb has matured for birth, the young do not wait for the natural release provided by nature; instead, they burst through the mother's sides, gnawing them open, which in turn results in the death of the mother.

It is also said that the male, having inserted his head into the female's mouth, spits his seed into her. She, however, driven into a frenzy by pleasure, bites off the male's head which she has received in her mouth. Thus it happens that both parents perish: the male while he mates, and the female when she gives birth.

Furthermore, **Saint Ambrose** <note>Ambrose (c. 340–397 AD) was a prominent theologian. This specific legend is found in his *Hexameron*, where he uses animal behavior to teach Christian ethics</note> says that it is the most wicked kind of beast and is more cunning than all other types of serpents. When it is seized by the desire to mate, it seeks out its known partner, the **sea moray** <note>original: *murena maritima*; a type of eel</note>, or prepares for her arrival. Proceeding to the shore, it announces its presence with a hiss and calls her into a conjugal embrace. The moray, having been invited, does not fail to appear, and she grants the venomous serpent the desired union. 

What, then, is the meaning of such a story, if not that the temperaments of spouses must be endured, and if one is absent, their presence should be sought? <note>This moral suggests that even if a spouse has a "poisonous" or difficult nature, the bond of marriage requires endurance and seeking out the other.</note>

<summary>This page describes the legendary reproductive cycle of the viper, where the offspring kill the mother and the female kills the father, followed by the viper's strange mating ritual with the sea moray. These tales serve as a medieval allegory for the complexities and duties of human marriage.</summary>

<keywords>viper, moray eel, Saint Ambrose, bestiary, animal allegory, medieval marriage, reproduction, natural history</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse based on Saint Ambrose’s *Hexameron*. Following the description of the strange mating between the land-dwelling viper and the sea-dwelling moray eel, the author uses this "natural" example to lecture wives on the necessity of enduring their husbands' flaws and remaining faithful, contrasting the "virtuous" behavior of the female viper with the perceived failings of human women.</meta>

...harsh temperament, or is deceitful, unrefined, slippery, or even a drunkard—what is worse than the poison that the **sea moray** <note>original: *murena*; a type of eel believed in the Middle Ages to mate with vipers</note> does not flee in her spouse? When called, she is there; she embraces the slippery serpent with diligent affection.

The husband bears your faults and the fickle nature of women; can you, woman, not endure your husband? **Adam** was deceived by **Eve**; it was not Eve who was deceived by Adam. Since the woman led the man into sin, it is only just that she take him as her guide <note>original: *gubernatorem*; literally "governor" or "navigator"</note>, so that she might not slip again through feminine fickleness <note>original: *facilitate*; here implying a lack of moral steadiness or "easy" susceptibility to temptation</note>.

Even if he is rough and unrefined, he once pleased you. Should a husband be chosen frequently? The lion seeks out its own mate, and the horse loves its own. If a horse takes another mate, the partner of the other does not know how to pull the yoke together and does not feel safe. Yet you repudiate your partner and think he should be changed often; if he is away for even one day, you bring in a rival. Without even knowing the cause, you quickly commit an offense against your own modesty.

The **viper** seeks her absent mate, calls to him, and cries out with an alluring hiss. When she senses her partner approaching, she vomits up her venom—showing reverence to her husband and offering nuptial modesty and grace <note>Medieval bestiaries claimed the female viper would vomit her poison before mating out of respect for her husband, a behavior the author here uses to shame human women for their "venomous" words</note>. You, woman, drive away your husband with insults when he arrives from a long journey. The viper gazes out over the sea and watches for her spouse's path. You, however, block your husband's path with grievances. You stir up the poisons of strife rather than controlling them. You, for whom the conjugal embrace...

<summary>This page uses the legendary mating of the viper and the moray eel to provide a moralizing lesson on marriage, arguing that women must endure their husbands' flaws just as the eel endures the viper's poison.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, viper, moray eel, Adam and Eve, Saint Ambrose, fidelity, feminine fickleness, medieval allegory</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing commentary derived from Saint Ambrose’s *Hexameron*. While the previous page focused on the duties of wives to endure their husbands, this section shifts the focus to husbands. Using the same allegory of the viper and the moray eel, the author argues that if a venomous serpent can set aside its poison to mate, a human husband must set aside his pride and anger to treat his wife with dignity and remain faithful.</meta>

...at that time, you seethe with a dire poison. You do not blush at the marriage rights, nor do you fail to revere the husband. I could hardly follow the second meaning; nonetheless, receive it in this way: lay aside the swelling of your heart <note>original: *tumore cordis*; a metaphor for pride or arrogance</note> and the harshness of your manners when your diligent wife comes to meet you. Drive away your indignation when your charming spouse calls you to affection.

You are not a master <note>original: *dominus*; implying a slave-owner</note> but a husband; you have not obtained a slave-girl but a wife. God willed you to be the guide <term>governor (gubernatorem)</term> <note>This term suggests a navigator or pilot of a ship, implying leadership through care rather than force.</note> of the weaker sex, not its tyrant. Return her devotion in turn; return grace for her love. 

The **viper** pours out her venom; can you not lay aside the hardness of your mind? If you have a natural harshness, you ought to temper it by contemplating your marriage; out of reverence for your union, lay aside the wildness of your soul.

It can also be understood in this way: Men, do not seek another’s bed; do not plot against another’s union. **Adultery** is a grave matter; it is an injury against nature itself. In the beginning, God made two—**Adam** and **Eve**—that is, a man and a wife. He made the wife from the man, from the rib of **Adam** <note>A reference to the Book of Genesis, emphasizing that husband and wife share the same substance.</note>, and He commanded both to be in one body and to live in one spirit. 

Why do you separate a single body? Why do you divide a single spirit? Adultery is an offense against nature. This is what the embrace of the **moray eel** and the **viper** teaches us—an embrace sought not by the laws of their own kind <note>original: *iure generis*; referring to the fact that they are different species (land vs. sea)</note>, but by the heat of desire. Learn, O men who seek to seduce another's wife, what kind of serpent’s companionship you truly desire to join.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the viper and the moray eel, exhorting husbands to abandon pride and anger in favor of mutual respect and marital fidelity. It argues that adultery is a violation of the natural order established at Creation.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, husband’s duties, adultery, Adam and Eve, Saint Ambrose, viper, moray eel, natural law</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the allegory of the viper and the moray eel, which served as a moral lesson for husbands on the previous page. It then transitions into a formal description of the Asp, focusing on its etymology and its legendary interaction with the snake charmer.</meta>

Since even the serpent is full of desire at that time, it hastens 
to the **moray eel**, which pours itself into the lap of the sea 
not by a direct path of travel, but by the slippery flow of a river. 
The **viper** hisses toward her and spits out its venom. 
It is said that by the pact of their union, the viper 
drinks back the venom it had vomited out.

<image-desc>A framed miniature depicts a medieval bestiary scene. On the left, a large, tan-colored serpent with horizontal ribbing (the asp) is coiled on a gray, rocky mound. On the right, a man representing an enchanter (incantator) stands in a long blue robe with a cowl and a small hat. He holds an open book and gestures with one hand toward the serpent. Behind him is a stylized green tree. The background is red with a white grid pattern, all enclosed in a red and black border.</image-desc>

The **Asp** <note>original: *Aspis*</note> is so named because it injects and 
scatters venom with its bite. In Greek, "poison" is called *ios* 
<note>original: *ios* (ἰός)</note> and from this comes "aspis," because it 
injects through a venomous bite. They say indeed that its mouth 
is always open and steaming. There are various types and 
species of this creature, with different ways of causing harm. 
However, it is said that the **asp** can be influenced by an 
**enchanter** <term>incantator (charmer)</term> <note>A figure common in medieval bestiaries, representing someone who attempts to control nature through spells or music.</note> who calls it forth 
with specific songs <note>original: *carminibus*; referring to incantations or charms</note> to draw it out 
from its cavern. When it does not wish to emerge, it [presses] one ear... <note>The text likely continues on the next page to describe how the asp plugs its ears to avoid the charmer's song.</note>

<summary>This page completes an allegory of the viper and moray eel as a model for marriage and begins a natural history of the asp, emphasizing its venomous nature and its resistance to the charms of the enchanter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the asp's behavior, specifically its defense against enchanters, and transitions into a catalog of different species of asps and serpents, likely derived from Isidore of Seville's *Etymologies* or Lucan's *Pharsalia*.</meta>

...presses [one ear] into the earth. The other it blocks and covers with its tail; and thus, not hearing those magical voices, it does not come out to the **enchanter** <term>incantator (charmer)</term> <note>A figure who attempts to control or summon animals through spells or music.</note>. Such indeed are the men of this world, who in their earthly desires press down one ear. And the other [they block] with the sins they have committed, so that they may not hear the voice of the Lord saying: "He who does not renounce all that he possesses cannot be my disciple nor my servant" <note>A reference to Luke 14:33.</note>. Asps do this only to stop up their ears. But these men also blind their own eyes so that they may not see heaven, nor remember the works of the Lord.

The **Dipsas** <note>original: *Dipsa*</note> moreover is a kind of asp, which in Latin is called a "bucket" <note>original: *situla*; likely a corruption or folk etymology relating to the thirst the venom causes</note>, because whoever it bites perishes of thirst. The **Hypnalis** <note>original: *Ypnalis*</note> is called a kind of asp that kills by sleep. This is the one **Cleopatra** applied to herself, and by that death she was released as if by sleep. The **Haemorrhois** <note>original: *Emorrois*</note> is so named because it sweats blood. Whoever is bitten by it becomes so weak that, with the veins dissolved, whatever life remains is drawn out through the blood. For in Greek, "blood" is called *haima* <note>original: *emach*, a corruption of the Greek αἷμα</note>. The **Prester** asp always runs with its mouth open and steaming, of which the poet remembers thus:

->The greedy prester distending its smoking jaws.<-
<note>A quote from the Roman poet Lucan’s *Pharsalia* (Book 9, 722), describing the horrors encountered by Cato’s army in the Libyan desert.</note>

This one distends the person it has struck; they are killed by enormous corpulence, for an overflowing rot follows. The **Seps** is a wasting asp, which, when it bites a man, consumes him immediately, so that he liquefies entirely <note>The text suggests the body dissolves as if being digested externally by the venom.</note> in the mouth of the serpent. The **Cerastes** is a serpent so named because on its head it has horns...

<summary>This page describes various types of asps—the Dipsas, Hypnalis, Haemorrhois, Prester, and Seps—and provides a moral allegory comparing the asp's refusal to hear the enchanter to a sinner's refusal to hear God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of serpents from the previous page, moving from various types of asps to the Cerastes and the Scitalis. The text continues to draw from classical authorities like Isidore of Seville and the poet Lucan to describe the miraculous and deadly traits of these creatures.</meta>

[The Cerastes] has [horns] similar to those of **rams** <term>original: arietum</term>. For in Greek, they are called **Cerastes** <note>original: Cerasta; derived from the Greek word *keras* (κέρας), meaning "horn".</note> because of these horns. It has four small horns, by the display of which it lures animals as if to bait; when they are enticed by the sight, it kills them. For it covers its entire body in the sands, leaving no sign of its presence except for the part with which it catches the birds or animals it has invited. Moreover, it is more flexible than other snakes, so much so that it seems to have no spine.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature within a square red-bordered frame. It depicts a blue bipedal creature with wings and a long, coiled tail. The creature's body is decorated with red and green spots. The background is dark blue with white decorative dots. This image is placed to the right of the following text block.</image-desc>

The **Scitalis** <note>original: Scitalis; likely the *Scythale*, a serpent described in ancient bestiaries as having a beautifully patterned back.</note> serpent is so named because it shines with such a great variety of markings on its back that the beauty of these spots slows down those who look upon it. And because it is slower at crawling, it captures those it cannot overtake by stunning them with the wonder of its appearance. 

It is of such great heat that even in the winter time, it sheds its burning skin. 

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature within a square red-bordered frame. It shows a striped, serpent-like creature coiled into a circle, its head facing inward as if biting its own tail or another smaller head. The background is blue with white dots and green stylized foliage on the left. This image is placed to the right of the following text block.</image-desc>

About which the poet **Lucan** <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet whose work *Pharsalia* (specifically Book 9) provided many medieval authors with descriptions of exotic Libyan snakes.</note> says:

->“And the **Scitalis**, who even now while the frosts are pressed down, is alone about to lay aside its skin.”<-
<note>original: *Et scitalis pressis etiam nunc sola pruinis / Exuvias positura suas*. This refers to the snake's internal heat being so high it molts even in the cold.</note>

<meta>This page continues the medieval bestiary's catalog of serpents and monstrous creatures, following the descriptions of the Cerastes and Scitalis. It introduces the Amphisbaena and the Hydrus/Hydra, maintaining the tradition of blending natural history with classical mythology and etymology.</meta>

The **Amphisbaena** <note>original: Amphisbaena; from the Greek *amphis*, meaning "both ways," and *bainein*, "to go."</note> is so named because it has two heads: one in its proper place, and the other at its tail. It can run from either head, moving its body in a circular motion. This is the only serpent that braves the cold, emerging earlier than all other snakes. Regarding this creature, the same Lucan <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet often cited in bestiaries for his descriptions of Libyan snakes in the poem *Pharsalia*.</note> wrote: "And the heavy Amphisbaena, inclining toward its twin head." Its eyes shine like lamps.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature set within an orange and gold frame. It depicts the Amphisbaena, a mythical serpent with a head at each end of its body. The creature has a tan-colored, four-legged body with a row of circular scales and a red-spotted underbelly. Its primary head on the right has an open mouth with a small snake or forked tongue protruding; the tail curls upward to reveal a second, similar head on the left. The background is divided into a section of burnished gold leaf and a red square patterned with white dots.</image-desc>

There is an animal in the river Nile called the **Hydrus** <note>original: idrus</note> which lives in the water. For the Greeks call water **hydros** <note>original: idros; referring to the Greek ὕδωρ (*hydor*).</note>. From this comes the **dracontis** <note>Likely a reference to the *prester* or a similar water-serpent described in ancient texts.</note>, a serpent whose bite causes the victim to swell up. Some call this disease **the cow-sickness** <term>original: boam</term> because it is cured by applying the dung of a cow. 

The **Hydra** <note>original: Idra</note> is a dragon of many heads, such as that which lived on the island of Lerna, or the swamp in the province of Arcadia. In Latin, this creature is called an **excetra** <note>A term used in Roman mythology for a multi-headed water snake, or more generally for something multi-layered and dangerous.</note> because when one head was cut off, three more would grow in its place.

<summary>This page describes two legendary serpents: the two-headed Amphisbaena, known for its ability to move in both directions and endure the cold, and the multi-headed Hydra (or Hydrus), associated with the labors of Hercules and the marshes of Lerna.</summary>

<keywords>Amphisbaena, Hydrus, Hydra, Lucan, Nile River, Lerna, Greek etymology, medieval bestiary, mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the entry on the Hydrus from page 97. It transitions from a rationalized explanation of the mythological Lernaean Hydra to a description of the Hydrus as a real animal that hunts crocodiles, ultimately serving as an allegory for Christ’s descent into Hell.</meta>

But this is a fable, for it is well known that the **Hydra** <note>original: ydram</note> was a place spewing forth waters, devastating the city of Lerna <note>The OCR reads "uianam," but in the context of the Hercules myth, this refers to the marshes of Lerna.</note>. When one water-channel there was closed, many more would burst out. Seeing this, **Hercules** drained the area and thus closed the water channels. For the Hydra is named after "water" <note>original: ydra ab aqua; referring to the Greek *hydros*.</note>.

This **Hydrus** <note>original: idrus</note> is a great enemy to the **crocodile**, and it has this nature and habit: when it sees a crocodile sleeping on the shore, it goes with its mouth open and rolls itself in mud, so that it might more easily slip into the crocodile's jaws. The crocodile then suddenly swallows it alive. But the Hydrus, tearing apart all the crocodile's internal organs, comes out not only alive but also unharmed.

Thus, **Death** and **Hell** <note>original: infernus</note> have the form of the crocodile, whose enemy is our **Lord Jesus Christ**. For taking on human flesh, He descended into Hell, and breaking all its innards, He led out those who were being held unjustly by it <term>The Harrowing of Hell: the Christian doctrine that Christ descended into the underworld after His death to liberate the souls of the righteous.</term>. For He put Death itself to death, rising from the dead. And the prophet mocks it, saying: "O Death, I will be your death; I will be your bite, O Hell" <note>original: O mors ero mors tua. morsus tuus ero inferne; a reference to Hosea 13:14, frequently quoted in medieval liturgy.</note>.

<image-desc>
A rectangular miniature illustration framed in blue and gold. The scene depicts two large cattle in the upper register: a grey ox on the left and a reddish-brown cow or ox on the right, standing on a green landscape against a solid gold background. Below them, in a separate but adjacent part of the composition, a reptilian "idrus" (hydrus) is shown crawling into the open mouth of a larger, scaly "cocodrillus" (crocodile) which is lying on its back. A stylized green tree is visible on the far left.
</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the Hydra as a literal water system drained by Hercules before describing the Hydrus's legendary battle with the crocodile, which serves as a metaphor for Christ's victory over death and Hell.</summary>
<keywords>Hydrus, Hydra, Hercules, Crocodile, Christ, Harrowing of Hell, Resurrection, Allegory, Lerna</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of serpents following the account of the Hydrus. It moves from the allegorical and semi-aquatic Hydrus to several terrestrial and flying serpents—the Boas, the Iaculus, the Siren, and the Seps—drawing heavily on the works of Solinus and the Roman poet Lucan.</meta>

The **Boas** <note>original: Boas</note> is a snake of Italy of immense size; it pursues herds of cattle and water buffalo. When it is swollen with a great amount of milk, it entwines itself around the udders and kills the animal by sucking. From this destruction of cattle <note>original: a boum depopulatione</note>, it received the name **Boas**.

<image-desc>A framed rectangular illumination. On the left, a stylized tree with blue and green foliage stands against a gold background. A winged serpent is perched within its branches. To the right, another winged serpent with a red crest and tail leaps from the air onto the back of a white bull or cow. The background is a deep blue sky dotted with white stars, bordered by a frame of orange and red.</image-desc>

The **Iaculus** <term>literally "javelin"</term> is a flying serpent. Concerning it, the poet **Lucan** <note>original: lucanus; referring to the Roman author of the epic *Pharsalia*, which contains a famous catalog of venomous snakes.</note> wrote: "And the flying javelins" <note>original: Iaculiq[ue] uolantes</note>. For they spring forth from trees, and whenever any animal comes into their path, they hurl themselves upon it and kill it. For this reason, they are called **Iaculi** <note>from the Latin *iacere*, meaning "to throw" or "to hurl".</note>.

---

<image-desc>A horizontal rectangular miniature depicting two winged, white-bodied serpents (Sirens) with long, winding tails. They face each other in profile against a background of gold and blue within a stepped, multi-colored border.</image-desc>

In **Arabia**, however, there are white serpents with wings called **Sirens** <note>original: syrene; not to be confused with the half-woman maritime creatures of the Odyssey, these are described in medieval bestiaries as deadly winged snakes.</note>, which run faster than horses, but are also said to fly. Their venom is so potent that death follows the bite before the pain is even felt.

---

<image-desc>A narrow horizontal frame containing a single brown-and-tan striped serpent crawling across the ground.</image-desc>

The **Seps** is a tiny serpent which consumes not only the body but even the bones with its venom. The poet mentions it thus:

<image-desc>A small framed illumination to the right of the concluding text, showing a human leg and foot being bitten at the ankle by a small greyish-brown serpent.</image-desc>

> The decaying seps, dissolving the bones along with the body. <note>original: Ossaq[ue] dissoluens cum corpore tabificus seps; another reference to Lucan's description of the horrific deaths of soldiers in the Libyan desert.</note>

<summary>This page describes four types of serpents—the milk-drinking Boas, the leaping Iaculus, the winged Siren of Arabia, and the bone-dissolving Seps—blending natural history with classical poetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the bestiary's catalog of snakes and lizards. It concludes the description of the Dipsas—following the account of the Seps and Siren mentioned previously—and transitions to the genus of lizards, drawing on the etymological traditions of Isidore of Seville and the poetic descriptions of Lucan.</meta>

The **Dipsas** <note>original: Dipsa; the name comes from the Greek word for 'thirst,' as its bite was traditionally said to cause unquenchable thirst, though this text focuses on its small size.</note> serpent is said to be of such small size that when it is stepped on, it is not seen. Its venom kills before it is even felt, so that the face, overtaken by sudden death, does not show the sadness usually seen in the deceased. Concerning this, the poet <note>The Roman poet Lucan in his epic *Pharsalia*, which describes a march through a desert infested with snakes.</note> wrote:

> The standard-bearer youth of Tyrrhenian blood,
> Aulus. Turning its head back, the trodden **Dipsas**
> bit him. There was scarcely any pain or sensation of the tooth. <note>original: "Signiferum iuvenem tyrreni sanguinis Aulum. Torta caput retro dipsas calcata momordit. Hic dolor aut sensus dentis fuit." In Lucan's original text, the word is 'Vix' (scarcely) rather than 'Hic' (this/here), emphasizing that the bite was unfelt.</note>

The **Lizard** <note>original: Lacertus</note> is a type of reptile, so named because it has "arms" <note>original: brachia; a medieval etymological pun linking the name of the animal, *lacertus*, with the Latin word *lacertus*, which refers to the muscles of the upper arm.</note>.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature depicting a lizard-like creature with four legs and a long tail, facing right, within a frame with a red and black border. The creature is rendered in grey tones against a gold background.</image-desc>

There are many kinds of lizards, such as the **botrax**, the **salamandra**, the **saura**, and the **stellio**. The **Botraca** <note>original: Botraca</note> is so called because it has the face of a frog; for the Greeks call a frog *botrachos* <note>original: botracam</note>.

<image-desc>A large square miniature with a blue border and a background of gold leaf surrounding a central red rectangle decorated with a pattern of white saltire crosses and dots. --- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the bestiary’s discussion of reptiles and lizards, following the description of the Botrax which was cut off on the previous page. It details the legendary properties of the Salamander, the rejuvenation of the Saura, and the star-like appearance of the Stellio, largely drawing on the Etymologies of Isidore of Seville and classical poetry.</meta>

The **Salamander** <note>original: Salamandra; in medieval folklore, the salamander was famously associated with fire, a belief dating back to Aristotle and Pliny the Elder.</note> is so named because it is effective against fires. Among all poisonous creatures, its power is the greatest. For while other venomous animals strike individuals, this one kills many at once <unclear>mānnr</unclear> <note>The unclear Latin word likely intended to be *perimit*, meaning 'destroys' or 'kills'.</note>. For if it crawls into a tree, it infects all the fruit with poison, and it kills those who eat from it. Even if it falls into a well, the power of its venom kills those who drink from the water. This creature alone among animals extinguishes fires, resisting the flames. For it lives in the midst of flames without pain or being consumed; and not only is it not burned, but it actually puts out the fire.

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature of a lizard-like creature (saura) with four legs and a long tail, colored in shades of tan and brown with dark spots, facing right.</image-desc>

The **Saura** <note>original: Saura; from the Greek 'saura' meaning lizard. This specific entry describes the 'Sun Lizard' legend.</note> is a lizard that, when it grows old, finds its eyes are blinded. It then enters a hole in a wall, facing toward the East; when the sun rises, its sight is rejuvenated and restored by the light <note>original: illuminatur; literally 'illuminated,' referring to the medieval belief that the sun's rays could heal the lizard's blindness.</note>.

<image-desc>A long horizontal rectangular miniature of a blue serpent-like creature (stellio) with white spots across its body, having a head with visible teeth and an eye, facing right.</image-desc>

The **Stellio** <note>original: Stellio; often identified as a gecko. The name is derived from 'stella' (star) due to the spots on its back.</note> takes its name from its color. For its back is decorated with shining spots in the manner of stars. Concerning which the poet Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso, a Roman poet. This line is from his work *Metamorphoses*, describing the transformation of a boy into a lizard.</note> wrote:

> It has a name suited to its color, its body starred with various spots. <note>original: "Aptumque colori nomen habet, variis stellatus corpore guttis."</note>

This creature is said to be so opposed to scorpions that the mere sight of it brings them fear and paralysis. There are also other kinds of serpents, such as the **adinolita** <note>Possibly a corruption of 'haemorrhois' or another specific serpent mentioned in classical texts.</note> and those with **elephant-colored flesh**...

<summary>This page describes the legendary qualities of the Salamander, which can extinguish fire and poison entire trees, alongside the Saura lizard that heals its blindness with sunlight and the star-spotted Stellio.</summary>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of reptiles from Isidore of Seville's Etymologies, specifically moving from the description of the "Stellio" (star-lizard) on the previous page to general observations about the nature of serpents, their venom, and their physiological makeup. It explains why venom is dangerous and concludes with the serpent's biblical reputation for wisdom.</meta>

...**dragons** <note>original: *dracontes*; here used as a concluding term for the various types of large serpents discussed previously.</note>. Finally, regarding their names: only the number has changed.

All serpents are cold by nature; they do not give birth except when they grow warm. For when they are cold, they touch nothing. This is why their venom harms more by day than by night. Indeed, they are sluggish because of the chill of the night, and even more so because they are made cold by the nighttime dew. For these cold pests, being icy by nature, bring a numbness upon their own bodies. Whence it is that in the winter they are exceedingly sluggish, but in the summer they are released <note>original: *soluuntur*; in this context, meaning they are "thawed out" or become active again.</note>.

It is for this reason that whoever suffers from the venom of any serpent first becomes numb; and afterwards, if that same <term>virus<note>original: *uirus*; meaning a potent liquid or poison. In medieval Latin, it refers to the active substance of the venom.</note></term>, having been warmed within the victim, flares up, it immediately extinguishes the man's life.

**Venom** <note>original: *venenum*</note> is so called because it travels through the **veins** <note>original: *venas*; Isidore of Seville uses this wordplay to suggest a linguistic link between the substance and its path through the body.</note>. For once the pestilence has been infused, it runs through the veins, intensified by the body’s own heat and movement, and it drives out the soul. Therefore, venom cannot cause harm unless it touches a person's blood. As the poet **Lucan** says: "The pestilence of serpents is harmful only when mixed with blood" <note>original: *Noxia serpentium est admixto sanguine pestis*; a reference to Lucan's epic poem, *Pharsalia*. This reflects the ancient belief that venom must enter the bloodstream to be fatal.</note>.

Furthermore, all venom is cold, and therefore the soul, which is fiery <note>In medieval science, the "soul" or life-force was often associated with "vital heat" or fire, which is the opposite of "cold" venom.</note>, flees the cold venom. Among the natural gifts which seem to be shared by us and irrational animals, the serpent excels in a certain sharpness of the senses. This is why it is written in **Genesis**: "Now the serpent was wiser than all the beasts of the earth" <note>original: *Serpens autem erat sapientior omnibus pecoribus terre*; a reference to Genesis 3:1.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the medieval understanding of serpent physiology, focusing on how their cold nature dictates their activity and how venom interacts with the "fiery" soul and the blood.</summary>
<keywords>serpents, venom, Isidore of Seville, Lucan, physiology, soul, blood, Genesis, vital heat, dragons</keywords>

<meta>This page shifts from the physiological focus of the previous section into the moralized allegories of the Bestiary tradition. The text continues the theme of how the serpent reacts to heat, cold, and spiritual environments.</meta>

**The Serpent** <note>original: *Serpens*; here the text transitions into a series of allegories where the physical habits of the animal represent spiritual truths.</note>
has three natures. Its first
nature is this: when
it grows old, its eyes grow clouded.
And if it wishes
to be made new: it
abstains and fasts
for many days until
its skin is loosened; and it seeks a narrow crack
in a rock, and enters it, and squeezes itself through, and
sheds its old skin. So also should we, through much

seek the spiritual rock, Christ, and the narrow
fissure—that is, the narrow gate <note>A reference to Matthew 7:14: "strait is the gate, and narrow is the way, which leadeth unto life."</note>. Its second
nature is when it comes to the river to drink water:
it does not carry its venom with it, but leaves it
in a hole. We also, when we come
to the gathering <note>original: *collectā*; referring to the liturgical assembly or the meeting of the faithful.</note> to draw
living and eternal water and to
hear the divine word in the heavenly
church, must cast away from us the venom—that is, earthly and
evil desires <term>concupiscentia<note>original: *concupiscentias*; in medieval theology, this refers to strong, often disordered desires or lusts that lead one away from the divine.</note></term>. Its third nature
is: if it sees a naked man, it fears him; and
if it sees him clothed, it leaps upon him. So also
let us understand spiritually that the first man <note>Referring to Adam; the medieval interpretation holds that while Adam was naked in his innocence, the serpent (the devil) could not harm him, but only attacked once he was "clothed" in the garments of sin.</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Third Nature" of the serpent from the previous page, explaining why the creature fears a naked man and attacks a clothed one. It transitions from pure theological allegory into a mix of moralizing biology and classical natural history, citing Pliny the Elder.</meta>

...leaps upon him. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "if it sees him clothed, it leaps upon him."</note>

As long as Adam was naked in paradise, the serpent was not able to prevail by leaping upon him. But after he was clothed—that is, with the mortality of the body—then the serpent leaped upon him. If you, therefore, have in you a mortal garment—that is, the **old man** <note>A biblical concept (Romans 6:6) referring to the unregenerate, sinful nature of humanity.</note>—and have grown old in evil days, the serpent leaps upon you. But if you strip yourself of the clothing of the princes and powers of this world of darkness, then the serpent—that is, the devil—will not be able to leap upon you.

The serpent, by eating **fennel** <note>original: *feniculi*; ancient and medieval authors like Pliny and Isidore of Seville claimed snakes ate fennel to clear their vision, especially after shedding their skins.</note>, repels the blindness it has contracted. And so, when it feels its eyes clouded by blindness, it seeks known remedies and is not cheated of the result. 

The tortoise, having eaten a serpent <note>original: *testudo in visceribus pasta serpentis*; likely a reference to the belief that tortoises eat snakes, or perhaps a corruption of the tradition that tortoises eat marjoram to protect their innards after fighting snakes.</note>, removes the poison from its bowels; it practices the medicine of its own safety through the serpent’s own body.

If a serpent tastes the **spit of a fasting man** <note>original: *ieiuni hominis sputum*; a widespread ancient folklore belief that human saliva, particularly before one has eaten in the morning, is a deadly poison to snakes.</note>, it dies. 

Moreover, **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of *Natural History*, the most influential encyclopedia of the natural world in the Middle Ages.</note> says it is believed that if someone crushes the head of a serpent even with two fingers, it nonetheless lives. Whence the whole body dies only when strikers hit the head <note>The text suggests that while the head is resilient to small pressure, it remains the creature's "kill point."</note>. To all snakes, vision is dull; they rarely look straight ahead. Nor is it in vain, since they have eyes not in the forehead but in the temples, so much so that they hear more quickly than they see. 

No animal moves its tongue with such speed as the serpent, so much so that it seems to have a **triple tongue** <note>A description of the forked tongue moving so rapidly it creates an optical illusion of being three-pronged.</note>, though it is but one. The bodies of serpents are moist, so much so...

<summary>This page concludes an allegory on Adam's fall and the 'old man' before detailing the serpent's biological quirks, such as its use of fennel for sight and its vulnerability to human saliva.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of serpent lore, following the description of the "Third Nature" of the serpent and the medicinal properties of human saliva. It transitions from the physical movement of the snake to its legendary ability to rejuvenate and its mythical origins from human remains, citing classical authorities like Pythagoras and Ovid.</meta>

...is seen <note>The text begins with the suffix "-ur," likely completing the word *videtur* (it is seen) from the previous page.</note> that wherever they go, they mark their path with moisture.

The tracks of serpents are such that, although they appear to lack feet, they nevertheless crawl by the exertion of their ribs and the movement of their scales. These they have arranged like a **pavement** <note>The Latin *pavi modo* refers to a "paved" or "tiled" manner, describing how the belly scales overlap like shingles or paving stones.</note> from the top of the throat down to the lowest part of the belly. For they lean upon their scales as if they were claws, and upon their ribs as if they were legs. Hence, if it should be struck by a blow on any part of the body from the belly to the head, it is rendered weak and cannot continue its course; for wherever that blow falls, it breaks the spine, which acts as the driver for the "feet" of the ribs and the movements of the body.

Furthermore, serpents are said to live for a long time—so much so that by shedding their old skin, they are believed to cast off old age and return to youth. The skins of serpents are called **sloughs** <note>original: *exuvie*; from the Latin *exuere*, meaning to "strip off" or "undress."</note>, because they strip themselves of these when they grow old. They are called both *exuviae* and *induviae* <note>original: *exuuie et induuie*; a wordplay on "that which is taken off" and "that which is put on."</note> because they are cast off and put on.

**Pythagoras** <note>A famous Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) to whom many legends regarding the transmigration of souls and spontaneous generation were attributed in the Middle Ages.</note> says that a serpent is created from the marrow of a dead man which is found in the spine. **Ovid** also commemorates this in his books of the *Metamorphoses*, saying: 

"There are those who say that when the spine has rotted in the tomb, human marrow is changed into a snake." <note>original: *Sunt qui aiunt putrefacta spina in sepulchro mutari credant humanas angue medullas*; quoting Ovid, *Metamorphoses* 15.389-390.</note>

If this is believed, it happens not without justice: that just as the death of man came through a serpent, so a serpent comes from the death of a man. <note>A moralizing conclusion linking the biology of the snake back to the Fall of Man in Genesis.</note>

<summary>This page describes the unique locomotion of snakes using ribs and scales, their ability to rejuvenate by shedding skin, and the folkloric belief that snakes are spontaneously generated from the spinal marrow of deceased humans.</summary>
<keywords>serpents, locomotion, shedding skin, exuviae, Pythagoras, Ovid, Metamorphoses, spontaneous generation, spinal marrow, allegory of the Fall</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical description and mythological origins of serpents to the nature of the scorpion, moving from zoological lore into biblical allegory and moral teaching.</meta>

The **Scorpion** <note>original: *Scorpio*</note> is an earthly **worm** <note>In the medieval period, "worm" (*uermis*) was a broad category for many creeping or crawling creatures, including insects, arachnids, and small reptiles.</note> which is classified among worms rather than serpents; it is an animal armed with a sting, and it is named thus in Greek <note>original: *scorpio*, from the Greek *skorpios*. Medieval etymologists often associated the name with the animal's ability to "scatter" venom or "pierce."</note> because it pierces with its tail and spreads its venom through a curved wound. It is a unique characteristic of the scorpion that it does not strike the palm of the hand.

The scorpion signifies the **Devil** or his ministers; hence it is said by our Lord in the Gospel: "Behold, I have given you the power to tread upon serpents and scorpions and over all the power of the enemy" <note>A reference to Luke 10:19.</note>. For elsewhere the Lord says in the Gospel: "For who among you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone? Or if he asks for a fish, will he give him a serpent? Or if he asks for an egg, will he reach out to him a scorpion?" <note>A reference to Luke 11:11–12. In medieval biblical commentary, the egg often represented the virtue of Hope, while the scorpion—which hides its sting—represented the destruction of that hope.</note>

Three things are most necessary for us: that is, **faith**, **hope**, and **charity** <note>These are known as the three Theological Virtues, derived from 1 Corinthians 13:13.</note>. **Faith** <term>fides: the virtue of believing in God's truth</term> so that we may believe rightly; **hope** <term>spes: the expectation of divine blessing</term> so that we may always await the promises of God with good patience; **charity** <term>karitas: the highest form of love, or "divine love"</term> so that we may diligently love Him with all our heart, with all our mind, and with all our strength, and our neighbor...

On the contrary, in the **stone**, **hardness** is expressed; in the **serpent**, **infidelity**; and in the **scorpion**, **despair**.

In **baptism**, faith is received, by which one lives in this world <note>original: *sclō*, an abbreviation for *saeculo*, meaning the current age or the secular world.</note>. Hope is represented in the egg, because in the egg the chick is not yet seen, but from it, the chick is hoped for. Charity, however, is compared to bread because it overcomes all things <note>This refers to the idea that Charity (Love) is the greatest of the virtues, sustaining the soul as bread sustains the body.</note>.

For anything that slips away while being held is called **slippery** <note>original: *lubricum*</note>, such as a fish or a serpent.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Cerastes (horned viper), which began at the end of the previous page. It moves from physical characteristics to a detailed moral and allegorical interpretation of the serpent as a symbol for the Antichrist, centered on a prophecy from the Book of Genesis.</meta>

...other serpents, so much so that it seems not to have a backbone. For by the names of the **Coluber** <note>A general Latin term for a snake or adder.</note> and the **Cerastes**, the **Devil** or the **Antichrist** is signified; as is that which the patriarch **Jacob** prophesied concerning his son **Dan**, saying:

> Let Dan be a snake <note>original: *coluber*</note> in the path, and a horned viper <note>original: *cerastes*</note> in the narrow way, biting the horse's heels, so that its rider falls backward. <note>Genesis 49:17. In medieval exegesis, the tribe of Dan was often associated with the lineage of the Antichrist.</note>

For not only is the Antichrist called a **Coluber**, but also a **Cerastes**. For in Greek, *cerastes* means horns <note>The author correctly identifies the Greek root *keras* (horn), though a *cerastes* is specifically a "horned one."</note>. Therefore, this serpent is held to be horned, as was mentioned above. Through this, the coming of the **Antichrist** is aptly asserted: he who will be armed against the life of the faithful with the bite of pestilential preaching and also with the **horns of power**. 

For who does not know that a "narrow way" <note>original: *semita*</note> is more restricted than a "path" <note>original: *uia*</note>? Therefore, "let him be a snake in the path," because in the fullness of life he provokes them to walk in its breadth <note>This refers to the "broad road" of worldly pleasure versus the "narrow gate" of salvation.</note>, flattering them as if by sparing them. But he bites in the path because those to whom he grants freedom, he consumes with the poison of his error. 

Let him be again a "horned viper in the narrow way," because those faithful whom he creeps upon—those who are seen to constrict themselves to the narrow journeys of heavenly commandment—he not only hinders by the wickedness of crafty persuasion but also crushes by the terror of his power. And in the anguish of persecution, after the benefits of feigned sweetness, he puts forth the **horns of power**. 

In this passage, the **horse** signifies this world, which foams with its own pride in the course of gliding time. And because the Antichrist...

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the Cerastes (horned viper), interpreted here as the Antichrist. The previous page established the viper’s connection to the tribe of Dan and the prophecy of Jacob from Genesis 49:17. This page focuses on the specific imagery of the "horse's heels" and the "rider falling backward."</meta>

...he strives to seize the ends of the world. That **horned viper** <note>original: *cerastes*</note> is said to bite the horse’s hooves. Indeed, to bite the horse’s hoof is to touch the very end of worldly happiness. 

"So that its rider falls backward." The rider of the horse is anyone who is lifted up by the worldly dignities <note>original: *indignitatibus*; here the author uses "unworthiness" to describe the hollow nature of worldly honors and status.</note> of this world. He is said to fall backward, and not on his face, as **Saul** <note>Referring to Saul of Tarsus, the future Apostle Paul. His fall on the road to Damascus (Acts 9:4) was traditionally interpreted as a "forward" fall because it led to self-reflection and conversion.</note> is remembered to have fallen. 

For to fall on one’s face is for each person to recognize their own sins in this life and to weep for them in **repentance** <note>original: *poenitendo*</note>. To fall backward, however—which is to fall where one cannot see—is to depart from this life suddenly and to be ignorant of the punishments to which one is being led.

<meta>This page transitions from the allegorical and theological discussion of serpents and the Antichrist into a natural history of "worms" (vermes). In medieval taxonomy, the category of "worm" was much broader than today, encompassing almost any small, creeping creature that was believed to generate spontaneously from decay or moisture, including spiders, leeches, and silkworms.</meta>

A **worm** <note>original: *vermis*</note> is an animal that is mostly born from flesh, or from wood, or from any earthly thing without any mating <note>original: *concubitu*; referring to the medieval belief in spontaneous generation, where life was thought to arise from non-living matter.</note>. Although sometimes they are also born from eggs, such as the **scorpion**. There are, however, worms of the earth, or of the water, or of the air, or of flesh, or of leaves, or of wood, or of clothing. 

The **spider** <note>original: *aranea*</note> is a worm of the air, named from its nourishment in the air <note>An ancient belief held that spiders lived on air or "wind-born" moisture.</note>, or because from its small body it draws out long threads and never ceases to work on its everlasting web, hanging skillfully in its own art. 

The **leech** <note>original: *sanguisuga*</note> is an aquatic worm, so called because it sucks blood <note>original: *sanguinem sugat*</note>; for it lies in wait for those who drink. And when it slips into the throat or where it has attached itself anywhere, it draws out blood; and when it has become soaked with too much gore, it vomits what it drew out, so that it may suck fresh blood once more. 

The **cantharid beetle** <term>Spanish Fly</term> <note>original: *cantarida*</note> is an earthly worm which, if applied to the human body, immediately creates blisters full of fluid by burning the skin. 

The **multipede** <note>referring likely to a woodlouse or "pill bug" based on the description of curling into a ball, though the name literally means "many feet."</note> is an earthly worm named for its multitude of feet; when contracted, it folds itself into a ball. It is born under rocks from moisture and earth. 

The **slug** <note>original: *limax*</note> is a worm named from "slime" <note>original: *limus*</note> because it is born in or from slime; for this reason it is always considered filthy and unclean. 

**Silkworms** <term>bombices</term> are worms of the leaves, from whose weaving silk garments <note>original: *bombicinum*</note> are made. It is called by this name because it empties itself while it produces threads, and only air remains in it <note>Isidore suggests a folk etymology here, linking *bombix* to the idea of a hollow shell or "emptying out."</note>. Cru- <note>The text breaks off here at the beginning of the word *eruca*, meaning caterpillar.</note>

<summary>This page defines "worms" and describes various small creatures—including spiders, leeches, and silkworms—detailing their supposed origins through spontaneous generation and the etymology of their names.</summary>
<keywords>natural history, worms, spider, leech, Spanish fly, centipede, slug, silkworm, spontaneous generation, Isidore of Seville, etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medieval encyclopedia of "worms" (vermes). Having previously discussed spiders, leeches, and beetles, the text now moves into pests that affect agriculture, clothing, and the human body. The author continues to rely heavily on etymology—the history and meaning of words—to explain the nature of these creatures, often drawing from Isidore of Seville's 7th-century work.</meta>

The **caterpillar** <note>original: *eruga*, likely a variant of *eruca*</note> is a worm of the foliage. Found on potherbs or on its vine-shoot, it is named from "gnawing away" <note>original: *erodendo*</note>. Plautus mentions it: "Neither does it provoke if, folded into a beastly and harmful thing in the vine-shoot, it entangles itself." If it flies away like a locust, running here and there, it leaves its meal half-eaten; but if it remains on the poor crops, it consumes everything with a slow crawl and sluggish bites. 

The Greeks call wood-worms **teredos** <note>original: *teredonas*</note> because they eat by boring <note>original: *terendo*</note>. We call these **termites** <note>In the medieval sense, *termites* refers to any wood-boring larvae, such as woodworms, rather than the modern social insect.</note>; for that is what the Latins call wood-worms that are born when trees are cut down at an inopportune time. 

The **moth** <note>original: *tinea*</note> is a worm of clothing, so called because it "holds on" <note>original: *teneat*</note> and stays inside until it gnaws through. From this comes the word **pertinacious** <term>stubbornly persistent</term>, because it persists in the same thing just as doggedly.

Worms of the flesh include the **migraine-worm** <note>original: *emigneus*, likely a corruption of *migraneus*. Medieval doctors believed some headaches were caused by a small worm in the head.</note>, the **intestinal worm**, **pinworms**, **lice**, **fleas**, the **nit**, the **lard-worm**, the **tick**, the **burning-worm**, and the **bedbug**. 

The **migraine-worm** is called a worm of the head. 

The **intestinal worm** <note>original: *lumbricus*</note> is a worm of the guts, named either from the "loins" <note>original: *lumbis*</note>, or because it is "slippery" <note>original: *lubricus*</note> since it glides, or because it resides in the loins. 

**Pinworms** <note>original: *ascarides*</note> and **lice** <note>original: *pediculi*</note> are worms of the skin. They are named from "feet" <note>original: *pedibus*</note>. From this, those in whose bodies lice swarm are called "lousy" <note>original: *pediculosi*</note>. 

**Fleas** <note>original: *pulices*</note> are so named because they are nourished primarily from "dust" <note>original: *pulvere*</note>. 

The **lard-worm** <note>original: *tarnus*, likely *tarmes*</note> is a worm of bacon. 

The **tick** <note>original: *ricinus*</note> is called the "dog-worm" because it clings to the ears of dogs; for in Greek, a dog is called **kyon** <note>original: *cinos*</note>. 

The **usia** is a "burning worm," named because it "burns" <note>original: *urit*</note>. For where they bite, the place burns so much that blisters form there. 

The **bedbug** <note>original: *cimex*</note> is named from its similarity to a certain herb... <note>Ancient authors compared the smell of bedbugs to the herb coriander, known in some dialects as *cimiciata*.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs various types of "worms" that plague plants, wood, clothing, and the human body, providing etymological explanations for their names.</summary>
<keywords>caterpillar, woodworm, moth, parasites, lice, fleas, ticks, medieval medicine, Isidore of Seville, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval discussion of "vermes" (creeping things). The previous page ended while listing parasites of the human body, specifically the bedbug (cimex).</meta>

...**bug** <note>original: *catus*, likely the end of a word like *foeticatus*, meaning "stinking"</note>, from the stench <note>original: *ferorem*, for *foetorem*</note> they have. Properly, however, the **worm** is born from rotting flesh: the **moth** <note>original: *tinea*</note> in clothing; the **caterpillar** <note>original: *eruca*</note> in vegetables; the **wood-worm** <note>original: *teredo*</note> in wood; the **lard-worm** <note>original: *carmus*</note> in fat.

The worm does not crawl like a serpent by means of steps or scales <note>original: *scamatistibz*, likely *squamatibus*</note>, because it does not have the rigidity of a spine like a snake <note>original: *colubri*</note>. Instead, it unfolds its motion by gradually stretching out its non-linear little body <note>original: *corpusculum*</note> part by part, and by contracting and pulling together the parts it has joined; and thus, through this agitation, it glides forward.

Mystically, the **worm** can be understood as **Christ** through the humanity he assumed, as it says in the Psalm:
> I am a worm and no man. <note>Psalm 22:6</note>

Alternatively, it signifies the memory of a sinful soul situated in the punishment of hell, about which it is written:
> Their worm shall not die, and their fire shall not be extinguished. <note>Isaiah 66:24</note>

The **spider** <note>original: *aranea*</note>, moreover, signifies human frailty, whence it says in the Psalm:
> And you have made his soul waste away like a spider. <note>Psalm 39:11</note>

The spider has a thin body and does not live on the ground; instead, in higher places, it weaves certain webs through a very thin "digestion" of its internal organs. It is also said that **silk** <note>original: *sericum*</note> is spun out by certain worms. To this tiny little body, the soul of a person who is truly converted and afflicted is rightly compared; for such a soul is exhausted by long observations and vigils. Bringing forth tender and most subtle works of virtue, the soul is made alive by divine love. 

After these things, the author returns to the beginning of his proposition: that although the soul may be rebuked, and although it may waste away, nevertheless, through the frailty of humanity and the variety of diverse things, it is con-
