<meta>This page continues the discussion of "vermes" (creeping things or worms) from the previous page, moving from the physical description of spiders and silk to the spiritual allegories of various pests.</meta>

...poured out. Likewise, the **spider** <note>original: *aranea*</note> signifies the deceit of the devil,
as in Isaiah: *They have woven the spider's webs.* <note>Isaiah 59:5</note> The **leech** <note>original: *sanguisuga*, literally "blood-sucker"</note>,
truly, is the devil, from which comes that passage in **Proverbs**:
*The leech has two daughters saying, "Bring, bring!"* <note>Proverbs 30:15</note>
The daughters of the devil are also **lust** and **greed**,
with which that bloodstained enemy assaults the human
race. **Rust** <note>original: *erugo*, referring to mildew, canker, or the corrosion of metal</note> can similarly
signify the devil, who does not cease to gnaw at the buds of virtues
growing in man with evil persuasions, and
to uproot them with wicked vices. Therefore, in the sacred
scriptures, where mention is made of the **moth** <note>original: *tinea*</note> and **rust**,
a mystical sense must be sought in them.
For according to allegory, **rust** can signify
**arrogance** and **boasting**, which,
wherever they exist, cloud all the beauty and strength of virtues
and scatter the treasure of spiritual gifts;
wherefore the prophet says: *God scatters the bones
of those who please themselves* <note>Psalm 53:5; the author uses this to describe the self-satisfied</note>, and all arrogance
is an abomination before the Lord. Moreover, the **moth**
signifies an internal vice of the soul, wherefore the Lord
says in the Gospel: *Store up for yourselves treasures
in heaven, where neither rust nor moth destroys, and where
thieves do not dig through nor steal.* <note>Matthew 6:20</note> Now, the **moth**
which gnaws away at clothes secretly designates **envy**,
which tears apart good zeal and does not cease to destroy the
bond of unity. For the **thieves** are
demons and heretics, about whom the Truth <note>A common medieval title for Christ, referring to John 14:6</note> says:

<summary>This page provides a moral and allegorical interpretation of pests, linking the spider and leech to the devil's greed, and the moth and rust to internal vices like envy and arrogance.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, devil, spider, leech, rust, moth, envy, arrogance, spiritual treasure, Matthew 6:20</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moral commentary on "thieves" mentioned at the end of page 105—referencing the Gospel of Matthew—and begins a new section on aquatic life, heavily influenced by the "Etymologies" of Isidore of Seville.</meta>

## All who are held by earthly possessions are thieves and robbers.
<note>original: *Omnes qui rem tenentur fures sunt et latrones*. This heading likely concludes the previous discussion on the "thieves" who steal spiritual treasures by prioritizing material wealth.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular illuminated miniature set within a gold-leaf background and a double border of red and blue. The scene depicts a variety of aquatic and hybrid creatures. At the top, two blue fish swim alongside a blue sea-horse (hippocampus) with a horse’s head and a fish tail. Below them are several smaller fish in shades of green and grey. In the center, a large blue dolphin-like creature is shown near a coiled brown serpent. To the left, there is a grey four-legged animal resembling an otter or beaver and a red sea-dog with a canine head and fish tail. To the right is an orange creature with a long, swan-like neck. At the bottom, a large blue fish with detailed scales emerges from stylized green and white waves. The red frame is decorated with small yellow flower-like patterns.</image-desc>

**Fish** <note>original: *Pisces*</note> are named from the same origin as **livestock** <note>original: *pecora*</note>, namely from **pasturing** <note>original: *pascendo*; the author follows the medieval tradition of linking words by their sounds to find hidden meanings; here, fish "graze" in the water just as cattle graze on land</note>. These things which swim are also called **creeping things** <note>original: *reptilia*</note> for this reason: because they possess the appearance and nature of creeping. <note>In medieval taxonomy, the way a fish "glides" through water was seen as identical to how a serpent or insect "creeps" across the earth.</note>

<summary>This page transitions from a moral warning about how material wealth "steals" spiritual focus to the beginning of a bestiary section on fish, using etymology to connect aquatic life with land animals.</summary>
<keywords>fish, thieves, livestock, creeping things, Isidore of Seville, etymology, spiritual allegory</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of aquatic creatures from the previous page, moving from the general definition of fish as "creeping things" to a detailed categorization based on the medieval etymological system of Isidore of Seville. It explains how sea creatures were named by analogy to land animals.</meta>

Although they submerge themselves in the deep, they nonetheless guide themselves while swimming. For this reason, David also says: “Behold this great and spacious sea; there are creeping things without number.” <note>original: *Hic mare magnum et spatiosum; illic reptilia quorum non est numerus*; a reference to Psalm 104:25, which medieval writers used to categorize fish as "reptiles of the sea."</note>

**Amphibians** <note>original: *Anfibua*</note> are certain kinds of fish so named because they have the habit of walking on land and the function of swimming in the water. For in Greek, *amphi* means “both,” <note>original: *utrique*</note> because they live on land for the most part, such as seals, crocodiles, and **hippopotamuses** <note>original: *ypotami*</note>—that is, “river horses.”

Humans gave names to cattle, wild beasts, and birds before they did to fish, because these were seen and known first. Now, names were established for fish later as their kinds were gradually recognized, either from their similarity to land animals, or from their own unique appearance, or by their habits, color, shape, or sex.

From their similarity to land animals come names like frogs, sea-mules, sea-lions, and black **sea-thrushes** <note>original: *meruli*; likely referring to the wrasse, which was named after the blackbird</note>; and those called “cloths” <note>original: *panni*; perhaps referring to the variegated skin of certain fish resembling patches of fabric</note> because their backs and necks are painted with divided colors; and sea-thrushes <note>original: *turdi*</note> dappled with white; and others that have claimed names according to land-based species.

From the habits of land animals come those like **sea-dogs** <note>original: *canes*</note>, named after land dogs because they bite; and **sea-wolves** <note>original: *lupi*; a name often given to the sea bass</note>, because they pursue others with wicked greed.

From their color: such as the **shadow-fish** <note>original: *umbre*; likely the grayling or meigrim</note>, because they are the color of shadows. And **gilt-heads** <note>original: *aurate*</note>, because they have the color of gold on their heads. And the “varied ones,” named from their variety [of spots], which people commonly call **trout** <note>original: *tructas*</note>.

From their shape: like the **orb-fish** <note>original: *orbis*; likely the pufferfish or sunfish</note>, which is round and consists almost entirely of a head; or the **sole** <note>original: *solea*</note>, because it is shaped like the soles of shoes.

<image-desc>The right margin shows a narrow vertical slice of a bordered manuscript illustration. It contains traces of gold, blue, and red pigments, likely forming part of a larger bestiary illustration on the facing page or the right side of the folio.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explores the etymology of aquatic creatures, explaining that fish were named later than land animals and often borrowed their names from them based on appearance, behavior, or color. It highlights the medieval worldview that the sea contains a mirrored version of the terrestrial world.</summary>

<keywords>amphibians, hippopotamus, sea-dog, sea-wolf, trout, etymology, Isidore of Seville, taxonomy, aquatic life</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page begins the entry for the Aspidochelone, a legendary sea monster often identified with the whale. It follows the standard medieval Bestiary format: a description of the animal's physical nature followed by a Christian moralization.</meta>

**T**here is a beast in the sea which in Greek is called the <term>Aspidochelone</term><note>The name translates to "shield-turtle," though the text describes a whale-like creature.</note>: in Latin, however, it is called the Shield-turtle. It is also called a <term>Whale</term><note>original: "Cete"</note> because of the immense size of its body. For it is like the one that took in <term>Jonah</term><note>The biblical prophet who was swallowed by a great fish.</note>; it was of such great size that it was thought to be hell, as Jonah himself said:
->He heard me from the belly of hell.<-
This creature, in the middle of the sea, raises its back above the waves of the ocean, and stays in one place so long that from the gentle shifting of the sand a level plain is formed, and shrubs grow there. The sailors, thinking it to be an island, moor their ships to it. Then they build fires for themselves. But the beast, feeling the heat of the fire, suddenly plunges into the water and drags the ship with it into the depths. So do they suffer who are of an unbelieving mind and are ignorant of the <term>devil's</term> cunning; placing their hope in him and binding themselves to his works, they are plunged together to the bottom of the fire of <term>Gehenna</term><note>A term for the place of fiery punishment or hell.</note>. The nature of the beast is such: when it is hungry, it opens its mouth and exhales a certain sweet-smelling odor from its mouth; when the smaller fish sense its sweetness, they gather together in its mouth. When it feels that its mouth is full, it suddenly closes its mouth and swallows them down. So suffer those who are of little faith; addicted to pleasures and enticements, they are suddenly swallowed by the devil like food drawn by certain scents. Whales, moreover, are beasts of immense size, named from "emitting and pouring out" <note>The text cuts off here; it likely refers to the "ballena" (whale) emitting water from its blowhole.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Aspidochelone (a whale-like sea monster), recounting two myths: sailors mistaking its back for an island and its use of sweet breath to lure small fish, both of which serve as allegories for the devil's deceptions.</summary>
<keywords>Aspidochelone, Whale, Jonah, Devil, Gehenna, Bestiary, Moralization, Sea Monster</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the whale (Balena) and introduces the sawfish (Serra) and the dolphin (Delfines). It maintains the pattern of describing an animal's nature followed by its symbolic Christian meaning.</meta>

...to call [them into] the waters. For it sweeps through the waves among the other beasts of the deep sea. The **Whale** <note>original: "Balena"</note> is called *emmie* in Greek <note>original: "emmie"; likely a corruption of "cete" or "hemi-", terms often used in medieval bestiaries to describe large sea creatures</note>. The male of the whale is the **musculus** <note>Meaning "little mouse," this fish was traditionally believed to lead the whale and keep it from danger.</note>; for this beast is said to have a horn [like] a *conape* <note>original: "conape"; likely referring to a "canopy" or a specific pointed peak.</note>.

There is a beast in the sea which is called the **Sawfish** <note>original: "serra"</note>, having immense fins. When this creature sees a ship sailing in the deep, it raises its fins above the water and strives to race with the ship for thirty or forty stadia <note>An ancient unit of distance; thirty stadia is roughly 3.5 miles.</note>. But, unable to sustain the labor, it grows exhausted, and lowering its fins, it pulls them back to itself. Then the waves of the sea carry the tired creature back to its place in the deep. 

This beast represents the image of this world <note>original: "scl’i" (saeculi)</note>. The ship, however, serves as an example of the righteous, who have passed through the middle of the storms and tempests of this world without danger or the shipwreck of their faith. But the **Sawfish**—that is, the beast that was not strong enough to sail with the ship—represents those who at the beginning began to persist in good works, but later, failing to persevere, were overcome by various kinds of vices. These vices submerge them, like the tossing waves of the sea, even down to hell. For the reward is promised not to those who merely begin, but to those who persevere.

**Dolphins** <note>original: "Delfines"</note> have their particular name because they follow the voices of men, or because they gather together in a group at the sound of music <note>original: "simphoniam"</note>. Nothing in the sea is faster than they are; for they frequently fly over ships by leaping. When they play in the waves and throw themselves about with leaps in the movement of the waters, they are seen to signify coming storms.

<summary>This page describes the sawfish as an allegory for those who start the Christian life but fail to persevere, while also noting the nature of whales and the musical, storm-predicting habits of dolphins.</summary>
<keywords>Whale, Musculus, Sawfish (Serra), Dolphin (Delfines), Perseverance, Allegory, Spiritual Shipwreck, Storms</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of aquatic creatures from the previous entry on dolphins and sawfish. It transitions from the "simones" (snub-nosed dolphins) to the Nile dolphin, the sea-pig, the crocodile, the hippopotamus, and begins the entry on the mullet.</meta>

...are properly named **simones** <note>original: "symones"; from the Greek *simos*, meaning "snub-nosed," a common medieval name for dolphins.</note>. There is also a kind of **dolphin** <note>original: "delphinum"</note> in the Nile with a serrated back, which kills the **crocodile** <note>original: "cocodrillum"</note> by slicing its soft underbelly.

**Sea-pigs** <note>original: "Porci marini"</note> are those which are commonly called "sow-fish" <note>original: "suilli"</note>. While they seek food, they dig into the earth beneath the waters in the manner of swine. For they are compelled by the function of their mouths to submerge their snouts in the sands; otherwise, they cannot gather their fodder.

The **Crocodile** <note>original: "Crocodillus"</note> is named for its saffron color <note>original: "a croceo colore"; a popular medieval etymology linking the Greek *kroke* (pebble/saffron) to the animal's appearance.</note>. It is born in the Nile; it is a four-footed animal, powerful both on land and in the water. It is often twenty cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, suggesting a length of about 30 feet.</note> in length, armed with terrifying teeth and claws. Its skin is of such great hardness that it does not feel the impact of even the strongest stones, which are repelled from its back. At night it rests in the waters, by day upon the ground. It hatches its eggs in the earth. The male and female take turns [guarding them]. Certain fish possessing a serrated crest kill it by slicing its soft underbelly. It is said to be the only animal that moves its upper jaw.

The **Hippopotamus** <note>original: "Ippotamus"</note> is so called because it is like a horse <note>The name is derived from the Greek *hippos* (horse) and *potamos* (river).</note> in its back, its mane, and its neighing; it has an upturned snout, tusks like a boar, a twisted tail, and cloven hooves. During the day it remains in the waters; at night it feeds upon the crops. The Nile also produces this creature.

The **Mullet** <note>original: "Mullus"</note> is so named because it is soft and very tender <note>The author links *mullus* to the Latin *mollis* (soft).</note>. They say that [by eating it] the lust of the belly is restrained and the sharpness of the eyes is dimmed. Those men, indeed, for whom this fish is a frequent meal...

<summary>This page describes various aquatic and amphibious animals, focusing on their physical traits and behaviors, specifically highlighting the crocodile's unique anatomy and the hippopotamus's horse-like features.</summary>
<keywords>dolphin, Nile, crocodile, sea-pig, hippopotamus, mullet, natural history, bestiary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the descriptions of various aquatic creatures, following the entry on the hippopotamus and the beginning of the section on the mullet. It concludes the properties of the mullet and describes the gray mullet, the eel, the sea-dragon, the moray eel, and begins a section on shellfish and their lunar cycles.</meta>

...smell. If a **mullet** <note>original: "mullus"; specifically the red mullet, highly prized in antiquity for its color and flavor.</note> is killed in wine, those who drink from it will have a loathing for wine. The **gray mullet** <note>original: "Mugilis"</note> gets its name because it is very agile <note>original: "multum agilis"; a popular medieval etymology linking the name to its speed.</note>. For when it senses the traps laid by fishermen, it immediately turns back and leaps across them so that you would think you see a flying fish. The **eel** <note>original: "Anguille"</note> received its name because of its likeness to a snake <note>original: "anguis"</note>. Its origin is from mud <note>original: "ex limo"; many ancient authors believed eels were spontaneously generated from mud rather than born.</note>. Hence, whenever it is caught, it is so slippery <note>original: "lenis"</note> that the harder one presses it, the faster it escapes.

Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the influential *Natural History*.</note> says that the Ganges, a river of the East, produces eels thirty feet long. If eels <margin>are made</margin> to be killed in wine, those who drink from it develop a loathing for wine <note>This was a common medieval folk remedy for alcoholism.</note>. The **sea-dragon** <note>original: "Draco marinus"; likely referring to the weever fish, known for its venomous spines.</note> has spines in its gills pointing toward the tail. When it strikes someone, it pours out venom. From this, it gets its name.

The Greeks call the **moray eel** <note>original: "Murena"</note> a *smyrina* <note>original: "smirinam"; from the Greek *smyraina*.</note> because it coils itself into circles. They say that this fish is only of the female sex and conceives from a serpent. For this reason, it is lured out by fishermen with a hiss, as if from a serpent, and is caught. It is killed with difficulty by a blow from a club, but a light rod <note>original: "ferula"; literally a fennel-stalk or a switch.</note> kills it. It is certain that its life is in its tail. For if struck on the head, you will hardly kill it, but it dies immediately from the tail.

**Shellfish** <note>original: "Conche"</note> and **snails** <note>original: "coclee"</note> are so named for this reason: because they become hollowed out—that is, emptied—when the moon wanes. For with the growth of all enclosed sea animals and shellfish...

<summary>This page details the characteristics and folklore of several fish and aquatic creatures, including the eel's mud-born origin and the moray eel's legendary mating habits. It also introduces the ancient belief that shellfish grow and shrink in accordance with the phases of the moon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of aquatic life from the previous page, shifting from fish (like the moray eel) to the influence of the moon on shellfish and the specific natures of pearls, the murex, and the crab.</meta>

...limbs swell with moisture and are emptied by its lack. For when the **Moon** is waxing, it increases moisture; but when it wanes, the humors <note>In medieval science, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to be influenced by celestial bodies.</note> are diminished. Natural philosophers <note>original: "physica"; those who study the physical world or medicine.</note> say this. **Shell** <note>original: "Concha"</note> is the primary name, while **snail-shell** <note>original: "coclea"</note> is a diminutive, as if it were a "little shell" <note>original: "conchula"</note>. 

There are many kinds of shells, among which are the **pearl-bearers**, which are called *cocleae*. In their flesh, a precious stone is solidified. Regarding these, those who have written on the nature of gems <note>original: "adamantium"; here referring to precious stones generally.</note> report that at nighttime they seek the shores and conceive a pearl from celestial dew; hence they are called *cocleae*. 

The **murex** <note>A sea snail famous in antiquity as the source of Tyrian purple dye.</note> is a sea-snail, named for its sharpness and roughness. It is known by another name, **conchylium**, because when it is cut around with an iron blade, it emits "tears" of a purple color. From these, purple dye is tinted, and from this it is called **ostrum** <note>A term for purple or blood-red dye, often associated with high status.</note> because this dye is drawn out from the fluid of the shell. 

**Crabs** <note>original: "Cancros"</note> are so called because they are shells that gape against others <note>Isidore suggests a folk etymology: *conchae* (shells) + *contra* (against/opposite).</note>; they are animals hostile to oysters. For they live on their flesh by a marvelous trick. Because the crab cannot open the oyster's strong shell by force, it watches for when the oyster opens the enclosures of its shells; then the crab secretly tosses in a small stone, and because the closing is hindered, the crab eats away the oyster’s flesh. 

Some report that if ten crabs are tied together with a bundle of basil, all those who <unclear>drink</unclear>... <note>The text breaks off here. In other versions of this text, this refers to a folk remedy where drinking the mixture was believed to cure scorpion stings.</note>

<summary>This page describes how the moon's phases affect the moisture of sea creatures and details the origins of pearls from dew and purple dye from the murex. It also records a famous medieval observation of the symbiotic/predatory relationship between crabs and oysters.</summary>

manner of a vaulted chamber <note>original: "camere"; referring to an arched or domed ceiling.</note>. There are, however, four 
kinds: Land-dwelling, sea-dwelling, mud-dwelling—
living in filth and marshes—and the fourth kind 
are river-dwelling, which live in fresh water. 
Some report that it is not incredible that ships travel 
more slowly if they are carrying the right foot 
of a tortoise. **Frogs** <note>original: "Rane"</note> are named for their chatter <note>original: "garrulitate"</note>, 
because they make a noise around the marshes where they are born 
and repeat the sounds of their voice with persistent cries. 
Among these, some are called aquatic, some 
marsh-dwelling, and some "bramble-toads" <note>original: "rubete"; likely referring to the common toad, *Bufo bufo*.</note> because 
they live in thickets; these are larger than the others. 
These are also called *calamites* <note>original: "calamites"; from the Greek word for reed.</note> because they live 
among reeds and shrubs. The smallest of all and the 
greenest are mute and without a voice. 
**Little field-frogs** <note>original: "Agredule"</note> are small frogs staying 
in dry places or fields; hence their name. 
Some say that dogs cannot bark if a live 
frog has been placed in their mouths. The **Sponge** <note>original: "Spungia"</note> 
is named from "shaping" <note>original: "fingere"; the author links the name to cleaning or arranging a space.</note>, that is, to clean and wipe away; from which...

<meta>This page continues from a discussion of aquatic life, likely moving from amphibians like frogs into a detailed description of sponges and a general classification of sea creatures based on the works of Pliny the Elder.</meta>

...with vinegar. And blood was imitated with sponges, that is, it was wiped away. <note>This likely refers to the use of sponges in ancient theater or medical demonstrations to simulate or clean up wounds.</note> Moreover, it is taught that the **Sponge** is an animal because of the blood adhering to its "wings" <note>The author uses *pennis* (wings/fins), perhaps referring to the sponge's attachments or fibers.</note>; whence, even when it is pulled away, it releases blood. For the hollow sponge mystically signifies the deceitful unfaithfulness of the Jews, of whom it is written in the Gospel: "But they, putting a sponge full of vinegar around hyssop, offered it to His mouth." <note>A reference to the Crucifixion of Jesus as recorded in the Gospel of John 19:29.</note> 

Now, concerning these animals, some are living in the waters and run about like fish. Others stand fixed, like **oysters**, **sea-mushrooms**, <note>original: *rimi*; likely referring to *rhizae* or similar polyps.</note> and **sponges**. Among these, some are even called "males," because their tubes <note>original: *fistule*; referring to the pores or channels of the sponge.</note> are thin and more dense. Others are "females," which have larger and more spaced-out tubes. 

Others are harder, which the Greeks call *tragos* <note>meaning "goats"</note> and we can call **goatish** <note>original: *hircosas*</note> because of their roughness. The softest kind of these are called **brushes** <note>original: *penecilli*, literally "little tails"; the root of our word "pencil," here referring to small sponges used for delicate tasks.</note>, because they are suited for the humors of the eyes and are useful for wiping away eye-infections. 

**White sponges** are made through care. For during the summer, they are spread out in the sun and, just like Punic wax, they drink in the whiteness. <note>Punic wax was a high-quality beeswax bleached white by the sun and seawater.</note> 

Pliny says there are one hundred and forty-four names of all animals found in the waters. They are divided into the following types: 
*   **Sea-monsters** <note>original: *beluarum*</note>
*   **Serpents** common to both land and water
*   **Crabs**
*   **Shellfish**
*   **Lobsters**
*   **Giant clams** <note>original: *peloridum*</note>
*   **Octopuses**
*   **Soles**
*   **Lizards** <note>original: *lacertorum*; likely referring to the *lacertus*, a type of sea-fish mentioned by ancient naturalists.</note> such as the **squid** <note>original: *lolligo*</note> and things similar to it. 

From which many, by a certain [instinct] of nature...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of aquatic life and sponges, transitioning from the physical properties of sea creatures to their reproductive habits and finally to their symbolic meaning in Christian scripture.</meta>

...bleaching, they recognize the order of their seasons. <note>The text likely refers to the harvesting and processing of sponges according to the time of year.</note> Some, indeed, wander in their own places without change.

**Among** female fish, however, some conceive and give birth to young <note>original: *catulos*; while often meaning puppies, in this context it refers to the live-born offspring of sharks or rays, which were known to the ancients.</note> through the mingling of the male. Others lay eggs conceived without the mingling of the male; these eggs are then doused by the discharge of the males who follow the females. Those eggs which have been touched by this gift become fertile; those, however, which have not been doused remain sterile or rot. <note>This describes the external fertilization of fish eggs, interpreted through a medieval lens of "breath" or "gift" (*munere*).</note>

**By the name of fish**, however, sometimes good men and sometimes bad men are designated in the Scriptures. For instance, there is the passage in the Gospel which the Savior tells in the parable of the net cast into the sea, saying: 

> The kingdom of heaven is like a **net** <note>original: *sagene*; a large dragnet or seine used for fishing.</note> cast into the sea, gathering every kind of fish. When it was full, they pulled it out and, sitting by the shore, they chose the good into their vessels, but threw the bad away. <note>A translation of Matthew 13:47–48.</note>

**Under** this parable, the Holy Church is compared to a net, which has been entrusted to fishermen. Through it, everyone is drawn to the eternal kingdom from the waves of the present world, lest they be submerged in the depths by the death they deserve. This net gathers from every kind of fish, because the Church calls the wise and the foolish, the free and the enslaved, the rich and the poor, the strong and the weak, to the forgiveness of sins.

**But** this shore represents the end of the world; the net of the Holy Church shows what it was pulling. And indeed, the good fish are the good men...

<summary>This page explains the reproductive biology of fish (distinguishing between live birth and external fertilization) before moving into an allegorical interpretation of the "Parable of the Net" from the Gospel of Matthew.</summary>
<keywords>fish reproduction, eggs, dragnet, Parable of the Net, Matthew 13, Church allegory, salvation, spiritual classification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the parable of the net (Matthew 13), moving from the description of the gathering of fish to the final judgment and the symbolic meaning of various sea and air creatures.</meta>

then they will be received into vessels, that is, into **eternal dwellings** <note>original: *mansiones*; a reference to the "many rooms" or "mansions" in heaven mentioned in John 14:2.</note>. The bad, however, will be cast out into the furnace of fire—namely, where there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth. Fish can also signify the **wise men** of this world, as it is written in that Psalm: 

*The birds of the heavens and the fish of the sea, who walk through the paths of the sea.* <note>A translation of Psalm 8:8. Medieval scholars often used this verse to categorize types of humans.</note> 

The "birds of the heavens" are proud men who, inflated by the wind of boasting, are carried away by empty vanity; they look down upon humble things when they are raised up high. For birds <note>original: *volucres*</note> are named for their "flighty nature" <note>original: *avolatu cerebro*; a medieval etymological play suggesting birds are named for their flight or their airy, flighty heads.</note>. 

The **fish of the sea** perhaps signify the **philosophers**, who investigate the nature of this world with a wandering curiosity. For just as fish, with their faces set forward, uncover the gifts of the sea for themselves amidst the confused churning of the deep, so too these men, with bowed heads, search out the very "veins" of reality through human reason and constant labor. 

But since there are also fish of the rivers, it is not without reason that "fish of the sea" are mentioned here; this refers to those who appear to themselves to be wise. Truly, such men and those like them are happily subjected to the Lord’s feet <note>A reference to Psalm 8:6, "You have put all things under his feet," interpreted here as the conversion of intellectuals to the faith.</note> when they come to the devotion of the Christian religion. 

The **Fishermen** are the Apostles or other preachers, as we read in the Gospel: 

> Follow me, and I will make you become **fishers of men**. <note>original: *Ite per me & faciam uos piscatores hominū fieri*; a slightly varied citation of Matthew 4:19.</note> 

And in a negative sense, it is said through Isaiah: 

> The fishermen shall mourn. <note>original: *lugebunt piscatores*; Isaiah 19:8, here interpreted as those who "fish" for worldly gain or false wisdom.</note> 

The "good fish" are mentioned in the Gospel: 

*And they drew the net full of great fish.* <note>John 21:11, referring to the miraculous catch of 153 fish.</note> 

The "reprobate fish" in the Gospel [are] the bad... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with the "bad fish" being cast away from the net.</note>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of fish and birds in Scripture, equating birds with proud men and sea fish with worldly philosophers who seek truth through reason alone before converting to faith.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the parable of the net (Matthew 13). It concludes the discussion on fish—shifting from the judgment of the good and bad to the specific symbolic identity of Christ as the "Fish"—before beginning a new section on the nature and names of trees and herbs, likely drawing from Isidore of Seville’s *Etymologies*.</meta>

...but they cast out. And in Amos: *Thus also the fish of the sea shall be gathered together.* <note>original: *Sic & pisces maris congregabuntur*; a reference to the prophetic gathering of all creatures for judgment, as seen in Amos 4:2 or Zephaniah 1:3.</note> The **Fish** <note>original: *Piscis*</note> is the Lord Jesus Christ, who was roasted in the fire of tribulation. As written in the Gospel: *They placed before him a piece of roasted fish and a honeycomb.* <note>Luke 24:42. In medieval theology, the "roasted fish" was a common symbol for Christ’s suffering on the cross.</note> The **two fish** joined with the five loaves signify the two testaments of the Law, or the two precepts of charity <note>The commandments to love God and to love one's neighbor (Matthew 22:37-40).</note>, or even the two persons: the royal and the priestly. In the Gospel: *And he took the five loaves and the two fish.*

The **Hook** <note>original: *Hamus*</note> represents the divinity of the Son of God, which lay hidden, clothed in flesh, like a hook concealed within bait. Concerning this "hook," the Lord, speaking to Job, says: *Can you pull out Leviathan with a hook?* <note>Job 41:1. This reflects a famous medieval "mousetrap" or "fishhook" theory of the Atonement: Christ's humanity was the bait that lured the Devil (Leviathan), while his hidden divinity was the hook that captured and defeated him.</note> Because that same enemy of the human race, while he bit the "bait" of the Lord’s body through the Passion, was killed by the power of the divinity. Likewise, in a negative sense, it refers to the devil, according to Habakkuk: *He will lift them all up with his hook.* <note>Habakkuk 1:15.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'A' in red ink. The center is filled with blue pen-flourished patterns. Delicate blue and red penwork flourishes extend into the left margin and down the side of the text block.</image-desc>

The name of **Trees** <note>original: *Arborum*</note> or **herbs** is believed to be derived from "fields" <note>original: *arvis*; a medieval etymological theory suggesting trees are named for the soil they inhabit.</note>, because they cling to the earth with fixed roots. Both, however, are for this reason almost similar to one another: because one is born from the other. For while the seed is fresh in the earth, the **herb** arises first. From there, its companion rises into a **tree**; and within a short time, what you had seen as an herb, you look up at as a **grove** <note>original: *arbustam*; here referring to a young tree or a plantation.</note>. A "grove-tree" is a new and tender tree in which a graft can be made. It is called *arbusta* as if it were the "spear of the tree" <note>original: *arboris hasta*; a pun on the straight, spear-like trunk of a young sapling.</note>. Others [say]...
